Me: what is p95 latency , short answer with example

AI: p95 latency = the response time that 95% of your requests are faster than (and 5% are slower). It's a tail-latency metric — it tells you about your worst typical case, not the average.

**Example:** if you run 100 queries against your FAISS index and sort the latencies, the p95 is the 95th value in that sorted list. So if 95 queries return in under 280ms and the remaining 5 take longer, your p95 latency is ~280ms — even if your *average* latency is much lower, say 50ms, because a few slow outlier queries don't get averaged away.

That's why it's a better scalability metric than a simple average: it catches the queries that hit worst-case behavior (e.g., a query landing in a dense region of the HNSW graph) rather than being hidden by all the fast, typical ones.

Me: allenai/olmOCR-2-7B-1025 and Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct, which of these two model is better for dealing with a multi column and table/figure heavy larger corpus ?

AI: Based on what's actually documented about these two models, this isn't close — olmOCR-2-7B-1025 is built specifically for your exact failure modes.

Why olmOCR-2-7B wins on capability:

It's fine-tuned via GRPO reinforcement learning specifically to boost performance at math equations, tables, and other tricky OCR cases. 
alphaXiv
Its training explicitly includes unit-test rewards for Natural Reading Order — ensuring correct sequential text flow and Table Accuracy — sampling random cells to verify positions and values. "Natural reading order" is literally your data speciality (multi-column) as a training objective.
This is a release of the olmOCR model that's fine tuned from Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct using the olmOCR-mix-1025 dataset — a much larger, more diverse training set (267,962 pages from over 100,000 PDFs sampled from diverse sources) than Qwen2-VL-2B ever saw for document-specific OCR. 
alphaXiv
It scores 82.4 on the olmOCR-bench evaluation, demonstrating strong performance on challenging OCR tasks including mathematical equations, tables, and complex document layouts — the exact task profile of BYTE pages (code listings, sidebars, tables, schematics).
Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct, by contrast, is a general-purpose small VLM — Qwen2-VL-2B features a 600M vision encoder and a 1.5B language model — not specifically tuned for document reading-order or table extraction at all.

Why Qwen2-VL-2B is still in your comparison at all — it's the scalability lever:
olmOCR-2-7B is a 7B model; Qwen2-VL-2B is 2B. That's your real trade-off, and it maps directly onto your scalable NFR:

7B costs more VRAM/latency per page. At ~45k full-corpus pages, that compute cost compounds — relevant to whatever ingestion-time budget you're implicitly working with (even though your NFR's 1.5s target is query-time, not OCR-build-time, a 7B model still makes your dev-subset OCR pass slower this week).
Note also: the team used the 72B teacher model rather than 7B, which improved performance by 11.8 points overall on olmOCR-Bench, with particularly strong gains on multi-column pages (+22.2) — meaning even within the olmOCR family, multi-column performance scales strongly with model size. That's a signal in favor of bigger models generally for your speciality, not evidence that 2B is competitive.

Recommendation: use olmOCR-2-7B-1025 as your primary/benchmarked choice — it's purpose-built for exactly your data speciality (multi-column + tables), which directly strengthens Section 3's "why this model" justification. Keep Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct as your fast/cheap fallback comparison point in the benchmark table, so your design_choices.md shows you actually traded off accuracy vs. compute rather than picking blind — that's the "one real trade-off" the form wants, and it's honest given your 4-day timeline (7B inference will be slower on your subset).

One practical note: olmOCR-2-7B is designed to run through the olmOCR toolkit (via VLLM that can handle millions of documents at scale) rather than bare transformers. Worth checking your GPU access can handle that before locking it in as your Day 2 pick. 
alphaXiv

Me: But is it said the the Qwen2B model is better at capturing tables and multi column at all? This is not because of comparison with the other 7B model but since we are already making an accuracy tradeoff i want to know whether Qwen2B can even do those tasks

AI: Good pushback — and honestly, I don't have direct published benchmark numbers for Qwen2-VL-**2B** specifically on tables/multi-column layout. What I found instead is evidence that should make you cautious, not reassured:

**No direct evidence it's good at this — and some evidence pointing the other way:**
- OmniDocBench found that most VLMs fail to recognize when dealing with newspapers, while pipeline tools achieve significantly better performance — and BYTE pages are structurally close to newspaper layout (dense multi-column, mixed with ads/sidebars). That's a warning sign for *any* general VLM, 2B included.
- Within the Qwen2/2.5-VL family specifically, multi-column performance scales heavily with model size: the earlier citation showed a 72B teacher model improving multi-column scores by **+22.2 points** over the 7B version. That's a steep size-dependence on *exactly* your speciality — and 2B is smaller still than the 7B point on that curve. There's no published number for 2B, but the trend line doesn't favor it.
- The general finding across sources: general-purpose VLMs demonstrate stronger generalization on unusual layouts, but that's a relative statement against pipeline tools — not evidence the 2B-scale model specifically handles multi-column/table structure well.

**Honest answer: unproven, and the indirect evidence leans toward real risk**, not just "smaller = a bit weaker." Since I can't confirm this from published benchmarks either way, the responsible move — and the one your assignment explicitly wants (Section 3 asks for reproduced, benchmarked evidence, not assumed capability) — is to test Qwen2-VL-2B against your own held-out labelled BYTE pages *before* locking it in. If its F1 on genuinely multi-column pages is close to olmOCR-2-7B's, you have real justification for the lightweight choice. If it collapses specifically on multi-column pages (vs. doing fine on single-column ones), that's your evidence to either keep 7B or add a region-splitting preprocessing step so the 2B model only ever sees single-column crops.

Me: but can you find evidence that the 7B model does pretty well in the OCR tasks of multi column and table figure heavy corpus? 
also approximate how much time it will need to scan 224 issues

AI: Now I have real numbers — and they answer both questions clearly.

**Yes — strong direct evidence olmOCR-2-7B excels specifically at your two problem areas:**

Per-category gains on olmOCR-Bench, olmOCR-2-7B-1025 achieves improvements of +19.7 (ArXiv), +14.8 (Old Scans Math), **+22.6 (Tables)**, **+16.1 (Multi-column)**, and +27.1 (Long Text) percentage points — Tables and Multi-column are literally its two largest documented gains. That's about as direct as evidence gets: it's not just "does OCR well," it's specifically strongest on your exact two failure modes.

**And this also answers your last question about the 2B model, with real numbers:** a same-scale comparison table shows **Qwen3-VL-2B scoring 51.2 on Multi-column**, versus **Qwen3-VL-4B at 88.8** — a ~38-point collapse at the 2B tier, on the exact category you care about (this is Qwen3-VL not Qwen2-VL, so treat it as a same-family proxy rather than your exact model, but it's a much more direct data point than anything found earlier, and it strongly reinforces the caution from last time). Small VLMs cratering specifically on multi-column, while other categories degrade far less, looks like a real, reproducible pattern — not just family-size scaling in general.

**Time estimate for 224 issues:****Throughput data found:** OlmOCR-2 scores 82.4 at 1.78 pages/sec (153,792 pages/day) benchmarked on a single H100 via vLLM. AI2 also states the FP8 build runs about 3,400 output tokens/sec on a single H100, fast enough that the release post quotes "10,000 pages for less than $2," with a batch estimate around $176–190 per million pages on rented GPUs.

**Estimate for your corpus, using the 1.78 pages/sec figure (single H100, OCR inference only):**

| Scope | Pages (per your A1 estimate) | Pure OCR compute time |
|---|---|---|
| Full 224 issues | ~45,000–50,000 | ~7–8 hours |
| Your A2 dev subset (10–15 issues) | ~1,500–3,000 | ~14–28 minutes |

**Important caveat — this is inference-only, not your real wall-clock time.** That 1.78 pages/sec figure assumes pages are already rendered as images and queued efficiently. Your actual pipeline also has to:
- Download PDFs/JP2 sets from Internet Archive (200+ GB for the full run — bandwidth-bound, likely hours on its own)
- Render pages to images at the right resolution
- Run layout detection *before* OCR (a separate stage, separate compute)
- Handle retries/rotation on failed pages

A realistic multiplier for full end-to-end wall time is **2–4x the pure-OCR number** — so the full 224-issue run is more like **15–30 hours** all-in, not 7–8. For your subset, that's roughly **1–2 hours** end-to-end, which is exactly why the subset strategy from the original plan is the right call — the full run is a genuine multi-day compute job on its own, completely separate from your 4-day deadline.

This is also good, citable evidence for `design_choices.md`: you can now write your subset-vs-full-corpus trade-off with an actual number attached ("full corpus ≈ 15–30h compute alone, infeasible in a 4-day sprint; subset ≈ 1–2h") instead of a vague "it's too big" — that specificity is exactly what the rubric rewards.

Me: """
BYTE Magazine OCR pipeline (single page / page range / full PDF) — local, open-weight OCR.

Kaggle notebook setup (run once, in a notebook cell):
    !pip install -q pymupdf opencv-python-headless bitsandbytes accelerate
    !pip install -q "transformers>=4.49" --upgrade
    # Notebook Settings -> Accelerator -> GPU (T4 x2 or P100) -- required for
    # practical inference speed with a 7B vision-language model.
    # No internet/API key needed: OCR runs fully locally, satisfying the
    # sovereign/self-hosted deployment requirement.

OCR model: allenai/olmOCR-2-7B-1025 (Apache-2.0, open-weight), a Qwen2.5-VL-7B
model fine-tuned specifically for document OCR (reading order, tables, code,
mixed layouts) -- see chat for full justification vs. Tesseract/TrOCR/Donut.
Loaded once with 4-bit quantization by default so it fits Kaggle's free GPU.

Design notes (carried over from the preprocessing pipeline, unchanged):
    - No blind deskewing: each page is INSPECTED for skew; rotation only
      applied if the measured angle exceeds SKEW_THRESHOLD_DEG. BYTE scans
      are generally well-aligned, so most pages will show "deskewed: False".
    - No blind denoising / contrast enhancement: both are gated by a
      lightweight measurement of the page itself.
    - No cropping, no column-splitting, no binarization, ever. The OCR model
      receives the full, unmodified-geometry page and is explicitly
      instructed to read multi-column layouts in correct order -- this
      pipeline does not implement Stage 2 (Layout) yet.

Three modes (pick one via CONFIG below, or CLI flags):
    "single" -> OCR one page. Useful for quickly testing the pipeline or
                inspecting prompt/preprocessing behavior on one page.
    "range"  -> OCR a specific list/range of pages. Useful for benchmarking
                a handful of representative pages (e.g., one per decade)
                before committing to an OCR model or settings.
    "all"    -> OCR every page in the PDF, once the pipeline is validated.

All three modes reuse the exact same preprocessing + OCR functions, and the
OCR model is loaded ONCE and reused across every page in the run.
"""

import argparse
import math
import os

import cv2
import fitz  # PyMuPDF
import numpy as np
import torch
from PIL import Image
from transformers import AutoProcessor, BitsAndBytesConfig, Qwen2_5_VLForConditionalGeneration


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Config — edit this for quick notebook use, or override via CLI flags
# (CLI flags take priority; see build_config() below).
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFAULT_CONFIG = {
    "pdf_path": "/kaggle/input/datasets/birotihs/cse429-byte-01/magazine.pdf",       # path to the scanned PDF
    "mode": "range",              # "single" | "range" | "all"
    "page": 12,                    # used when mode == "single" (1-indexed)
    "page_spec": "1-30",      # used when mode == "range"; comma list + ranges
    "dpi": 300,                    # rendering resolution
    "skew_threshold_deg": 0.5,     # only rotate if estimated skew exceeds this
    "noise_threshold": 4.0,        # only denoise if estimated noise sigma exceeds this
    "contrast_threshold": 45.0,    # only run CLAHE if grayscale std is below this
    "ocr_model_id": "allenai/olmOCR-2-7B-1025",
    "use_4bit_quant": True,        # keeps VRAM usage ~6GB; fits Kaggle's free GPU
    "max_new_tokens": 4096,        # generation cap per page; raise for very dense pages
    "max_image_longest_side": 1288,  # olmOCR's documented expected input scale --
                                      # feeding it a raw 300 DPI page (thousands of
                                      # patches) causes the vision encoder's
                                      # quadratic self-attention to OOM.
    "output_dir": "/kaggle/working/ocr_output",  # default Kaggle-writable path
}


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# OCR model loading — call ONCE, reuse across all pages in a run.
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def load_ocr_model(model_id: str, use_4bit_quant: bool = True):
    """
    Load the local OCR model + processor once. Reusing this across pages
    (instead of reloading per page) is what makes "range"/"all" modes
    practical -- model load time dwarfs a single page's inference time.
    """
    if not torch.cuda.is_available():
        print(
            "WARNING: no GPU detected. A 7B vision-language model will be "
            "extremely slow on CPU. On Kaggle, enable Settings -> Accelerator -> GPU."
        )

    quant_config = None
    if use_4bit_quant and torch.cuda.is_available():
        quant_config = BitsAndBytesConfig(
            load_in_4bit=True,
            bnb_4bit_compute_dtype=torch.bfloat16,
            bnb_4bit_quant_type="nf4",
            bnb_4bit_use_double_quant=True,
        )

    print(f"Loading OCR model: {model_id} (4-bit quant: {use_4bit_quant})...")
    model = Qwen2_5_VLForConditionalGeneration.from_pretrained(
        model_id,
        quantization_config=quant_config,
        torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16,
        device_map="auto",
    ).eval()

    # olmOCR shares Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct's processor/tokenizer.
    processor = AutoProcessor.from_pretrained("Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct")

    print("OCR model loaded.")
    return model, processor


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Page extraction
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def extract_page_as_image(pdf_path: str, page_number: int, dpi: int = 300) -> np.ndarray:
    """Render one PDF page (1-indexed) to a BGR image array."""
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    if not (1 <= page_number <= doc.page_count):
        doc.close()
        raise ValueError(f"page_number={page_number} out of range (PDF has {doc.page_count} pages).")

    page = doc[page_number - 1]
    zoom = dpi / 72
    pix = page.get_pixmap(matrix=fitz.Matrix(zoom, zoom))

    img = np.frombuffer(pix.samples, dtype=np.uint8).reshape(pix.height, pix.width, pix.n)
    if pix.n == 4:
        img = cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_RGBA2BGR)
    elif pix.n == 3:
        img = cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_RGB2BGR)

    doc.close()
    return img


def get_pdf_page_count(pdf_path: str) -> int:
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    count = doc.page_count
    doc.close()
    return count


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Preprocessing — each step inspects the page first, and only acts if needed.
# Nothing here crops, splits columns, or binarizes. (Unchanged from before.)
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def measure_skew_angle(image: np.ndarray) -> float:
    gray = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    _, thresh = cv2.threshold(gray, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV + cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    coords = np.column_stack(np.where(thresh > 0))
    if coords.shape[0] < 50:
        return 0.0
    angle = cv2.minAreaRect(coords)[-1]
    if angle < -45:
        angle = 90 + angle
    return -angle


def deskew_if_needed(image: np.ndarray, threshold_deg: float) -> tuple[np.ndarray, bool]:
    angle = measure_skew_angle(image)
    if abs(angle) <= threshold_deg:
        return image, False
    (h, w) = image.shape[:2]
    rot_matrix = cv2.getRotationMatrix2D((w // 2, h // 2), angle, 1.0)
    rotated = cv2.warpAffine(image, rot_matrix, (w, h), flags=cv2.INTER_CUBIC, borderMode=cv2.BORDER_REPLICATE)
    return rotated, True


def estimate_noise_sigma(image: np.ndarray) -> float:
    """Fast noise-level estimate (Immerkaer's method). Higher = noisier."""
    gray = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    H, W = gray.shape
    M = np.array([[1, -2, 1], [-2, 4, -2], [1, -2, 1]], dtype=np.float64)
    conv = cv2.filter2D(gray.astype(np.float64), -1, M)
    return np.sum(np.abs(conv)) * math.sqrt(0.5 * math.pi) / (6 * (W - 2) * (H - 2))


def denoise_if_needed(image: np.ndarray, threshold: float) -> tuple[np.ndarray, bool]:
    sigma = estimate_noise_sigma(image)
    if sigma <= threshold:
        return image, False
    if image.ndim == 3:
        result = cv2.fastNlMeansDenoisingColored(image, None, h=5, hColor=5, templateWindowSize=7, searchWindowSize=21)
    else:
        result = cv2.fastNlMeansDenoising(image, None, h=5, templateWindowSize=7, searchWindowSize=21)
    return result, True


def measure_contrast(image: np.ndarray) -> float:
    gray = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    return float(gray.std())


def enhance_contrast_if_needed(image: np.ndarray, threshold: float) -> tuple[np.ndarray, bool]:
    contrast = measure_contrast(image)
    if contrast >= threshold:
        return image, False
    clahe = cv2.createCLAHE(clipLimit=2.0, tileGridSize=(8, 8))
    if image.ndim == 3:
        lab = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2LAB)
        l, a, b = cv2.split(lab)
        result = cv2.cvtColor(cv2.merge((clahe.apply(l), a, b)), cv2.COLOR_LAB2BGR)
    else:
        result = clahe.apply(image)
    return result, True


def preprocess_page(image: np.ndarray, config: dict) -> tuple[np.ndarray, dict]:
    """Inspect-then-act preprocessing. Returns the image plus a log of what fired."""
    log = {}
    image, log["deskewed"] = deskew_if_needed(image, config["skew_threshold_deg"])
    image, log["denoised"] = denoise_if_needed(image, config["noise_threshold"])
    image, log["contrast_enhanced"] = enhance_contrast_if_needed(image, config["contrast_threshold"])
    return image, log


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# OCR via the local olmOCR model — same function used regardless of mode
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
OCR_PROMPT = (
    "This is a full, unmodified scanned page from a 1970s-1990s computer "
    "magazine (BYTE Magazine). It may use a 2-3 column layout, with code "
    "listings, tables, figures, captions, and advertisements.\n\n"
    "Transcribe ALL text on the page, reading in correct order: "
    "top-to-bottom within each column, left column before right column. "
    "Preserve code listings verbatim, including indentation. For "
    "figures/tables/ads, transcribe any caption or label text and skip "
    "non-text visual content. Do not summarize, reorder, or omit anything."
)


def resize_for_ocr(image: np.ndarray, max_longest_side: int) -> np.ndarray:
    """
    Downscale so the longest side matches max_longest_side, preserving aspect
    ratio. This does NOT touch the preprocessed/saved image elsewhere in the
    pipeline -- it only affects what's fed to the OCR model, following
    olmOCR's documented expected input scale (longest dim = 1288px). Feeding
    a raw 300 DPI page instead produces thousands of vision-encoder patches
    and causes an out-of-memory error in self-attention (quadratic cost).
    """
    h, w = image.shape[:2]
    longest = max(h, w)
    if longest <= max_longest_side:
        return image  # already small enough; upscaling would just waste compute
    scale = max_longest_side / longest
    new_w, new_h = int(round(w * scale)), int(round(h * scale))
    return cv2.resize(image, (new_w, new_h), interpolation=cv2.INTER_AREA)


def run_local_ocr(image: np.ndarray, model, processor, max_new_tokens: int, max_longest_side: int) -> str:
    """Send one full page image to the locally-loaded OCR model, return transcribed text."""
    image = resize_for_ocr(image, max_longest_side)
    rgb_image = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    pil_image = Image.fromarray(rgb_image)

    messages = [{"role": "user", "content": [{"type": "image"}, {"type": "text", "text": OCR_PROMPT}]}]
    chat_text = processor.apply_chat_template(messages, tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True)

    inputs = processor(text=[chat_text], images=[pil_image], padding=True, return_tensors="pt")
    inputs = {k: v.to(model.device) for k, v in inputs.items()}

    with torch.no_grad():
        generated_ids = model.generate(**inputs, max_new_tokens=max_new_tokens, do_sample=False)

    # Strip the input prompt tokens, keep only newly generated text.
    trimmed_ids = [out_ids[len(in_ids):] for in_ids, out_ids in zip(inputs["input_ids"], generated_ids)]
    output_text = processor.batch_decode(trimmed_ids, skip_special_tokens=True, clean_up_tokenization_spaces=False)
    return output_text[0]


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Page selection (shared by "single" / "range" / "all")
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def parse_page_spec(spec: str, total_pages: int) -> list[int]:
    """Parse a page spec like "1,5,10-12" into a sorted list of unique page numbers."""
    pages = set()
    for part in spec.split(","):
        part = part.strip()
        if not part:
            continue
        if "-" in part:
            start, end = part.split("-")
            pages.update(range(int(start), int(end) + 1))
        else:
            pages.add(int(part))
    return sorted(p for p in pages if 1 <= p <= total_pages)


def get_page_list(config: dict) -> list[int]:
    """Resolve the page list to process based on the selected mode."""
    total_pages = get_pdf_page_count(config["pdf_path"])
    if config["mode"] == "single":
        return [config["page"]]
    elif config["mode"] == "range":
        return parse_page_spec(config["page_spec"], total_pages)
    elif config["mode"] == "all":
        return list(range(1, total_pages + 1))
    else:
        raise ValueError(f"Unknown mode: {config['mode']!r} (expected 'single', 'range', or 'all')")


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Per-page processing (extract -> preprocess -> OCR), reused by every mode
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def process_page(pdf_path: str, page_number: int, config: dict, model, processor) -> dict:
    image = extract_page_as_image(pdf_path, page_number, dpi=config["dpi"])
    processed_image, preprocess_log = preprocess_page(image, config)
    text = run_local_ocr(
        processed_image, model, processor,
        max_new_tokens=config["max_new_tokens"],
        max_longest_side=config["max_image_longest_side"],
    )
    return {"page": page_number, "text": text, "preprocessing_applied": preprocess_log}


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Orchestration
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def run(config: dict) -> list[dict]:
    os.makedirs(config["output_dir"], exist_ok=True)

    # Load once, reuse for every page -- critical for "range"/"all" modes.
    model, processor = load_ocr_model(config["ocr_model_id"], config["use_4bit_quant"])

    page_list = get_page_list(config)
    print(f"Mode: {config['mode']} | Pages to process: {page_list}")

    results = []
    for page_number in page_list:
        print(f"\nProcessing page {page_number}...")
        result = process_page(config["pdf_path"], page_number, config, model, processor)
        print(f"  Preprocessing applied: {result['preprocessing_applied']}")

        out_path = os.path.join(config["output_dir"], f"page_{page_number:04d}.txt")
        with open(out_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            f.write(result["text"])
        print(f"  Saved: {out_path}")

        results.append(result)

    return results


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# CLI — optional; overrides DEFAULT_CONFIG. Safe to ignore in a notebook
# (just edit DEFAULT_CONFIG above and call run(DEFAULT_CONFIG) directly).
# Uses parse_known_args so it won't crash on Jupyter/Kaggle's own kernel args.
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def build_config() -> dict:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="BYTE Magazine local OCR pipeline")
    parser.add_argument("--pdf", dest="pdf_path")
    parser.add_argument("--mode", choices=["single", "range", "all"])
    parser.add_argument("--page", type=int)
    parser.add_argument("--page-spec", dest="page_spec")
    parser.add_argument("--dpi", type=int)
    parser.add_argument("--output-dir", dest="output_dir")
    parser.add_argument("--no-quant", dest="use_4bit_quant", action="store_false")
    args, _unknown = parser.parse_known_args()

    config = dict(DEFAULT_CONFIG)
    for key, value in vars(args).items():
        if value is not None:
            config[key] = value
    return config


if __name__ == "__main__":
    cfg = build_config()
    run(cfg)
I used this code to run the OCR, also pls can you tell me for the vectored database is it necessary to also save the page numbers in the OCR? and for this particular code ran on kaggle it took around 40 mins for completion of scanning 12 pages , so pls recalculate the time or try to find things in the code that would make this process faster

AI: **1. Page numbers in the vector DB — yes, absolutely necessary.**

This isn't optional for your project — it's structurally required:
- Your own A1 schema (Section 3) already includes `page_num`, `doc_id`, `chunk_id`, `reading_order_idx` as per-chunk fields — you designed this correctly back in A1.
- The `cite` tool in the fixed agent interface exists specifically to attach the source page to the answer — without page numbers in your index, `cite()` has nothing to attach.
- Grounding/citation accuracy is graded (Section 5's evidence, and E14 in eval/metrics.py) — an answer with no traceable page reference can't be scored as grounded.
- Practically: store `page_num` (and ideally `doc_id`/issue identifier) as metadata on every chunk in `index/chunk.py` and `index/store.py`, not just the embedding vector — FAISS itself doesn't carry metadata, so you need a parallel lookup (dict/dataframe/sqlite) mapping vector ID → `{doc_id, page_num, chunk_id, reading_order_idx}`.

**2. Why 40 minutes for 12 pages, and the recalculated full-corpus time**

40 min ÷ 12 pages = **~200 sec/page**, versus the 1.78 pages/sec (≈0.56 sec/page) H100+vLLM benchmark from earlier — you're running **~350x slower** than that reference number. That gap is fully explained by your code, and it's fixable. Three real bottlenecks:

- **`load_in_4bit` (bitsandbytes/NF4) is a memory-saving trick, not a speed trick.** It keeps VRAM low enough to fit Kaggle's free GPU, but bnb 4-bit *inference* dequantizes weights on-the-fly during generation, which is genuinely slow — commonly 3–10x slower than bf16 for autoregressive decoding on the same hardware. You need this on Kaggle's T4/P100 (16GB) to fit a 7B model at all, but it's a major cost.
- **No vLLM / no batching.** Your loop calls `model.generate()` once per page (batch size 1) using plain HuggingFace `transformers`. The 1.78 pages/sec H100 figure came from **vLLM**, which uses continuous batching and PagedAttention to keep the GPU saturated across many pages simultaneously — bare HF `generate()` in a for-loop leaves most of the GPU idle between forward passes.
- **Hardware gap.** Kaggle's free T4/P100 is roughly 10–15x slower than an H100 for this kind of workload even before the above two factors.

**Recalculated full-corpus estimate at your measured 200 sec/page:**

| Scope | Pages | Time at current rate |
|---|---|---|
| Full 224 issues | ~45,000–50,000 | **~104–116 days** |
| Your dev subset (10–15 issues) | ~1,500–3,000 | **~3.5–7 days** |

Your subset alone now exceeds your entire 4-day deadline on Kaggle's free GPU as configured. This needs fixing before Day 2/3, not after.

**What will actually make this faster, ranked by impact:**

1. **Switch off bnb 4-bit if VRAM allows, or move to a quantization format with fast inference kernels (AWQ/GPTQ/FP8), not bnb-NF4.** bnb-NF4 is optimized for QLoRA *training* memory savings, not generation speed. If your Kaggle GPU is P100 (16GB) or T4×2, check whether bf16 fits (7B model ≈14GB in bf16) — that alone could be several times faster than 4-bit bnb, even with less headroom.
2. **Batch multiple pages per `generate()` call** instead of looping one page at a time — `processor(...)` already accepts lists of images; feeding e.g. 4–8 pages per batch will use the GPU far more efficiently than one at a time, since attention/matmul throughput scales sub-linearly with batch size on modern GPUs.
3. **Use the actual olmOCR toolkit (vLLM-backed) instead of raw `transformers.generate()`**, if you can get it running in the Kaggle environment — this is what produced the 1.78 pages/sec H100 number, and vLLM's continuous batching would help even on a weaker GPU, just proportionally less than on H100.
4. **Cap `max_new_tokens` closer to what pages actually need.** 4096 is a hard ceiling; if your held-out labelled pages show most transcriptions finish well under that (check actual generated token counts), lowering it reduces worst-case decode time per page without losing text.
5. **Seriously consider renting a cloud GPU for the OCR run instead of Kaggle's free tier.** Given the earlier citation — a batch estimate around $176–190 per million pages on rented GPUs — even your full 45–50k page corpus would cost under $10 on a rented H100 with the proper vLLM pipeline, and finish in the ~7–8 hours estimated earlier instead of 100+ days. For a 4-day deadline, this is very likely the pragmatic move for at least your dev subset, and it's a completely legitimate design choice to document ("switched from local Kaggle GPU to rented cloud H100 for OCR throughput" is a real, gradable trade-off for `design_choices.md`).

**Bottom line:** don't run "all" mode on Kaggle's free GPU with this exact config — even your subset won't finish in time. Fix bullets 1–2 first (they're free, just code changes), test the new pages/sec rate on your 12-page benchmark again, and recalculate before deciding whether you still need to move to rented compute.

Me: If I use this tool  layout:    {model: "detectron2:layout", score_thr: 0.5} along with the QwenVL2B , then will it perform almost as good as the 7B model?

AI: Genuinely good instinct — and there's real evidence for it, though with an important caveat.

**Yes, this pattern is well-documented: pairing layout detection with a small recognition model can close (or even beat) the gap against a much larger end-to-end VLM.**

- A striking example: GLM-OCR, at just **0.9B parameters**, achieves a score of 94.62 on OmniDocBench V1.5, ranking #1 overall — beating far larger models — via a two-stage pipeline that pairs the language decoder with the PP-DocLayout-V3 layout detection model. That's the exact architecture you're proposing (layout-first, then a lighter recognizer), and it's currently a top performer.
- More generally: pipeline systems ... pair lightweight detection models with VLM-based recognizers, achieving strong accuracy with modular efficiency — this is described as a real, competitive category, not a hack.
- Direct evidence it beats "just throw it all at a big VLM": a financial-document study found directly feeding full PDFs into the VLM yields the lowest performance in every configuration ... structured processing is essential for reliable data extraction — and this held even when compared against Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct as a strong end-to-end baseline.

**Why this logically fits your case specifically:** the reason small VLMs (like Qwen2-VL-2B, or the Qwen3-VL-2B data point from earlier at 51.2 on multi-column) struggle isn't that they can't *read* text — it's that they can't reliably *figure out reading order* across a messy multi-column page on their own. If `vision/layout.py` does that job first — segmenting the page into ordered regions — you're no longer asking the 2B model to solve reading-order-detection *and* transcription simultaneously; it just has to transcribe cleanly-cropped single-column regions in the order layout gives it. That's a much easier task, and it's exactly why the pattern above works.

**The real caveat — this isn't free, and it's not guaranteed to fully close the gap:**
- Pipeline tools ... suffer from inter-stage error propagation ... every downstream field extractor has to reconstruct structure from that stream — if `detectron2:layout` misdetects a column boundary or merges two regions, your OCR model faithfully transcribes the *wrong* thing, and there's no recovery. A monolithic 7B model reading the whole page can sometimes route around a single ambiguous region; a pipeline can't self-correct across stages the same way.
- No source above benchmarks *this specific pairing* (Detectron2 + Qwen2-VL-2B) against olmOCR-2-7B. GLM-OCR's 0.9B result used a purpose-built layout model (PP-DocLayout-V3) and a recognizer co-designed/trained for that pipeline — not an off-the-shelf combination. Your result depends heavily on how good `detectron2:layout`'s column segmentation actually is on BYTE's specific pages (sidebars, boxed code, ads breaking columns).

**Practical recommendation:** this is worth testing, not assuming. Run your held-out labelled pages through: (a) raw Qwen2-VL-2B, (b) layout-segmented-then-Qwen2-VL-2B, (c) raw olmOCR-2-7B. If (b) closes most of the gap to (c), you have a genuinely strong, well-justified, fast pipeline — and a great `design_choices.md` trade-off story backed by both external evidence and your own numbers. If layout segmentation itself struggles on your messiest pages (heavy ads/sidebars), the errors will show up directly in (b)'s F1, telling you honestly whether to keep 7B for this corpus instead.

"""
BYTE Magazine OCR pipeline comparison — 3 pipelines, same page(s), separate
output folders + timing, so you can benchmark before committing to one model.

Kaggle notebook setup (run once, in a notebook cell):
    !pip install -q pymupdf opencv-python-headless bitsandbytes accelerate
    !pip install -q "transformers>=4.49" --upgrade
    !pip install -q layoutparser
    !pip install -q "git+https://github.com/facebookresearch/detectron2.git"
    # Notebook Settings -> Accelerator -> GPU (T4 x2 or P100)
    # Detectron2 install from source can take several minutes on first run.
    # No internet/API key needed for inference: everything runs locally.

Three pipelines compared, on the SAME page(s):
    A) "qwen2vl_2b_raw"     -> Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct, full page, no layout step.
                               Fast, lightweight, but per earlier benchmarking
                               discussion, weakest on multi-column reading order.
    B) "qwen2vl_2b_layout"  -> detectron2 (PubLayNet) layout detection first,
                               crops regions in reading order (column-aware),
                               then Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct OCRs each region and
                               results are concatenated. Tests whether an
                               explicit layout stage closes the gap for a
                               small model (see chat for the GLM-OCR /
                               PP-DocLayout evidence this is based on).
    C) "olmocr_7b_raw"      -> allenai/olmOCR-2-7B-1025, full page, no layout
                               step (it's trained end-to-end for reading
                               order). This is your accuracy reference point.

All three pipelines share the EXACT SAME preprocessing (inspect-then-act
deskew/denoise/contrast) and the EXACT SAME page extraction, so the only
variable being tested is the OCR/layout approach itself.

Outputs land in:
    {output_dir}/qwen2vl_2b_raw/page_XXXX.txt
    {output_dir}/qwen2vl_2b_layout/page_XXXX.txt   (+ page_XXXX_regions.json)
    {output_dir}/olmocr_7b_raw/page_XXXX.txt
    {output_dir}/comparison_summary.json           (timing + region counts)

Start with mode="single" on one page (default: page 12, matching your
earlier benchmark) before spending time/GPU-hours on a full range.
"""

import argparse
import json
import math
import os
import time

import cv2
import fitz  # PyMuPDF
import numpy as np
import torch
from PIL import Image
from transformers import AutoProcessor, BitsAndBytesConfig, Qwen2_5_VLForConditionalGeneration, Qwen2VLForConditionalGeneration


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Config
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFAULT_CONFIG = {
    "pdf_path": "/kaggle/input/datasets/birotihs/cse429-byte-01/magazine.pdf",
    "mode": "single",              # "single" | "range" | "all"
    "page": 12,                    # used when mode == "single" (1-indexed)
    "page_spec": "1-30",           # used when mode == "range"
    "dpi": 300,
    "skew_threshold_deg": 0.5,
    "noise_threshold": 4.0,
    "contrast_threshold": 45.0,

    "qwen2vl_model_id": "Qwen/Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct",
    "olmocr_model_id": "allenai/olmOCR-2-7B-1025",
    "use_4bit_quant": True,        # applies to BOTH models; keeps VRAM low on Kaggle

    "layout_model_config": "lp://PubLayNet/faster_rcnn_R_50_FPN_3x/config",
    "layout_score_thr": 0.5,       # matches config.yaml's layout.score_thr
    "layout_column_gap_px": 40,    # x-gap used to group regions into columns

    "max_new_tokens": 4096,
    "max_image_longest_side": 1288,  # olmOCR's + Qwen2-VL's expected input scale

    "output_dir": "/kaggle/working/ocr_output",
    "run_pipelines": ["qwen2vl_2b_raw", "qwen2vl_2b_layout", "olmocr_7b_raw"],
}


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Page extraction (shared by all 3 pipelines)
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def extract_page_as_image(pdf_path: str, page_number: int, dpi: int = 300) -> np.ndarray:
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    if not (1 <= page_number <= doc.page_count):
        doc.close()
        raise ValueError(f"page_number={page_number} out of range (PDF has {doc.page_count} pages).")
    page = doc[page_number - 1]
    zoom = dpi / 72
    pix = page.get_pixmap(matrix=fitz.Matrix(zoom, zoom))
    img = np.frombuffer(pix.samples, dtype=np.uint8).reshape(pix.height, pix.width, pix.n)
    if pix.n == 4:
        img = cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_RGBA2BGR)
    elif pix.n == 3:
        img = cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_RGB2BGR)
    doc.close()
    return img


def get_pdf_page_count(pdf_path: str) -> int:
    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    count = doc.page_count
    doc.close()
    return count


def parse_page_spec(spec: str, total_pages: int) -> list[int]:
    pages = set()
    for part in spec.split(","):
        part = part.strip()
        if not part:
            continue
        if "-" in part:
            start, end = part.split("-")
            pages.update(range(int(start), int(end) + 1))
        else:
            pages.add(int(part))
    return sorted(p for p in pages if 1 <= p <= total_pages)


def get_page_list(config: dict) -> list[int]:
    total_pages = get_pdf_page_count(config["pdf_path"])
    if config["mode"] == "single":
        return [config["page"]]
    elif config["mode"] == "range":
        return parse_page_spec(config["page_spec"], total_pages)
    elif config["mode"] == "all":
        return list(range(1, total_pages + 1))
    raise ValueError(f"Unknown mode: {config['mode']!r}")


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Preprocessing (shared, unchanged, inspect-then-act)
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def measure_skew_angle(image: np.ndarray) -> float:
    gray = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    _, thresh = cv2.threshold(gray, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV + cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    coords = np.column_stack(np.where(thresh > 0))
    if coords.shape[0] < 50:
        return 0.0
    angle = cv2.minAreaRect(coords)[-1]
    if angle < -45:
        angle = 90 + angle
    return -angle


def deskew_if_needed(image: np.ndarray, threshold_deg: float) -> tuple[np.ndarray, bool]:
    angle = measure_skew_angle(image)
    if abs(angle) <= threshold_deg:
        return image, False
    (h, w) = image.shape[:2]
    rot_matrix = cv2.getRotationMatrix2D((w // 2, h // 2), angle, 1.0)
    rotated = cv2.warpAffine(image, rot_matrix, (w, h), flags=cv2.INTER_CUBIC, borderMode=cv2.BORDER_REPLICATE)
    return rotated, True


def estimate_noise_sigma(image: np.ndarray) -> float:
    gray = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    H, W = gray.shape
    M = np.array([[1, -2, 1], [-2, 4, -2], [1, -2, 1]], dtype=np.float64)
    conv = cv2.filter2D(gray.astype(np.float64), -1, M)
    return np.sum(np.abs(conv)) * math.sqrt(0.5 * math.pi) / (6 * (W - 2) * (H - 2))


def denoise_if_needed(image: np.ndarray, threshold: float) -> tuple[np.ndarray, bool]:
    sigma = estimate_noise_sigma(image)
    if sigma <= threshold:
        return image, False
    if image.ndim == 3:
        result = cv2.fastNlMeansDenoisingColored(image, None, h=5, hColor=5, templateWindowSize=7, searchWindowSize=21)
    else:
        result = cv2.fastNlMeansDenoising(image, None, h=5, templateWindowSize=7, searchWindowSize=21)
    return result, True


def measure_contrast(image: np.ndarray) -> float:
    gray = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    return float(gray.std())


def enhance_contrast_if_needed(image: np.ndarray, threshold: float) -> tuple[np.ndarray, bool]:
    contrast = measure_contrast(image)
    if contrast >= threshold:
        return image, False
    clahe = cv2.createCLAHE(clipLimit=2.0, tileGridSize=(8, 8))
    if image.ndim == 3:
        lab = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2LAB)
        l, a, b = cv2.split(lab)
        result = cv2.cvtColor(cv2.merge((clahe.apply(l), a, b)), cv2.COLOR_LAB2BGR)
    else:
        result = clahe.apply(image)
    return result, True


def preprocess_page(image: np.ndarray, config: dict) -> tuple[np.ndarray, dict]:
    log = {}
    image, log["deskewed"] = deskew_if_needed(image, config["skew_threshold_deg"])
    image, log["denoised"] = denoise_if_needed(image, config["noise_threshold"])
    image, log["contrast_enhanced"] = enhance_contrast_if_needed(image, config["contrast_threshold"])
    return image, log


def resize_for_ocr(image: np.ndarray, max_longest_side: int) -> np.ndarray:
    h, w = image.shape[:2]
    longest = max(h, w)
    if longest <= max_longest_side:
        return image
    scale = max_longest_side / longest
    new_w, new_h = int(round(w * scale)), int(round(h * scale))
    return cv2.resize(image, (new_w, new_h), interpolation=cv2.INTER_AREA)


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Shared OCR-call helper — works for any Qwen2-VL-family causal LM + processor
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
OCR_PROMPT_FULL_PAGE = (
    "This is a full, unmodified scanned page from a 1970s-1990s computer "
    "magazine (BYTE Magazine). It may use a 2-3 column layout, with code "
    "listings, tables, figures, captions, and advertisements.\n\n"
    "Transcribe ALL text on the page, reading in correct order: "
    "top-to-bottom within each column, left column before right column. "
    "Preserve code listings verbatim, including indentation. For "
    "figures/tables/ads, transcribe any caption or label text and skip "
    "non-text visual content. Do not summarize, reorder, or omit anything."
)

OCR_PROMPT_REGION = (
    "This is one cropped region from a scanned page of a 1970s-1990s "
    "computer magazine (BYTE Magazine). It has already been isolated as a "
    "single reading-order unit (e.g. one column, one sidebar, or one code "
    "listing) by a layout-detection step.\n\n"
    "Transcribe ALL text in this region, top to bottom. Preserve code "
    "listings verbatim, including indentation. Do not summarize, reorder, "
    "or omit anything."
)


def run_vlm_ocr(image: np.ndarray, model, processor, prompt: str,
                 max_new_tokens: int, max_longest_side: int) -> str:
    """Send one image to a locally-loaded Qwen2-VL-family model, return text."""
    image = resize_for_ocr(image, max_longest_side)
    rgb_image = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    pil_image = Image.fromarray(rgb_image)

    messages = [{"role": "user", "content": [{"type": "image"}, {"type": "text", "text": prompt}]}]
    chat_text = processor.apply_chat_template(messages, tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True)

    inputs = processor(text=[chat_text], images=[pil_image], padding=True, return_tensors="pt")
    inputs = {k: v.to(model.device) for k, v in inputs.items()}

    with torch.no_grad():
        generated_ids = model.generate(**inputs, max_new_tokens=max_new_tokens, do_sample=False)

    trimmed_ids = [out_ids[len(in_ids):] for in_ids, out_ids in zip(inputs["input_ids"], generated_ids)]
    output_text = processor.batch_decode(trimmed_ids, skip_special_tokens=True, clean_up_tokenization_spaces=False)
    return output_text[0]


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Model loaders — each called ONCE, reused across every page/pipeline call
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def load_qwen2vl_2b(model_id: str, use_4bit_quant: bool):
    quant_config = None
    if use_4bit_quant and torch.cuda.is_available():
        quant_config = BitsAndBytesConfig(
            load_in_4bit=True, bnb_4bit_compute_dtype=torch.bfloat16,
            bnb_4bit_quant_type="nf4", bnb_4bit_use_double_quant=True,
        )
    print(f"Loading Qwen2-VL-2B: {model_id} (4-bit quant: {use_4bit_quant})...")
    model = Qwen2VLForConditionalGeneration.from_pretrained(
        model_id, quantization_config=quant_config, torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16, device_map="auto",
    ).eval()
    processor = AutoProcessor.from_pretrained(model_id)
    print("Qwen2-VL-2B loaded.")
    return model, processor


def load_olmocr_7b(model_id: str, use_4bit_quant: bool):
    quant_config = None
    if use_4bit_quant and torch.cuda.is_available():
        quant_config = BitsAndBytesConfig(
            load_in_4bit=True, bnb_4bit_compute_dtype=torch.bfloat16,
            bnb_4bit_quant_type="nf4", bnb_4bit_use_double_quant=True,
        )
    print(f"Loading olmOCR-2-7B: {model_id} (4-bit quant: {use_4bit_quant})...")
    model = Qwen2_5_VLForConditionalGeneration.from_pretrained(
        model_id, quantization_config=quant_config, torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16, device_map="auto",
    ).eval()
    processor = AutoProcessor.from_pretrained("Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct")
    print("olmOCR-2-7B loaded.")
    return model, processor


def load_layout_model(layout_model_config: str, score_thr: float):
    """
    Lazy import — layoutparser/detectron2 are heavy and only needed for
    pipeline B. Uses a PubLayNet-pretrained Faster R-CNN (text/title/list/
    table/figure regions) as a stand-in for 'detectron2:layout' in
    config.yaml. Swap the model config string for a different pretrained
    layout model if you find one that fits BYTE pages better.
    """
    import layoutparser as lp
    print(f"Loading layout model: {layout_model_config} (score_thr={score_thr})...")
    model = lp.Detectron2LayoutModel(
        layout_model_config,
        extra_config=["MODEL.ROI_HEADS.SCORE_THRESH_TEST", score_thr],
        label_map={0: "Text", 1: "Title", 2: "List", 3: "Table", 4: "Figure"},
    )
    print("Layout model loaded.")
    return model


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Reading-order sort — groups detected regions into columns (by x-position),
# then orders columns left-to-right and regions top-to-bottom within each.
# This is the "column/region-segmentation stage before OCR" your A1 form
# named as required for multi-column reading order.
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def sort_regions_reading_order(regions: list, column_gap_px: int) -> list:
    if not regions:
        return []
    # Each region: {"bbox": [x1, y1, x2, y2], "type": str, "score": float}
    sorted_by_x = sorted(regions, key=lambda r: r["bbox"][0])
    columns = []
    current_column = [sorted_by_x[0]]
    current_right_edge = sorted_by_x[0]["bbox"][2]
    for region in sorted_by_x[1:]:
        x1 = region["bbox"][0]
        if x1 - current_right_edge > column_gap_px:
            columns.append(current_column)
            current_column = [region]
        else:
            current_column.append(region)
        current_right_edge = max(current_right_edge, region["bbox"][2])
    columns.append(current_column)

    ordered = []
    for column in columns:
        column_sorted_top_to_bottom = sorted(column, key=lambda r: r["bbox"][1])
        ordered.extend(column_sorted_top_to_bottom)
    return ordered


def detect_and_order_regions(image: np.ndarray, layout_model, column_gap_px: int) -> list:
    rgb_image = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB) if image.ndim == 3 else image
    layout = layout_model.detect(rgb_image)
    regions = [
        {"bbox": [block.block.x_1, block.block.y_1, block.block.x_2, block.block.y_2],
         "type": block.type, "score": float(block.score)}
        for block in layout
    ]
    return sort_regions_reading_order(regions, column_gap_px)


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Pipeline A — raw Qwen2-VL-2B, no layout step
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def pipeline_qwen2vl_2b_raw(image: np.ndarray, model, processor, config: dict) -> dict:
    text = run_vlm_ocr(
        image, model, processor, OCR_PROMPT_FULL_PAGE,
        config["max_new_tokens"], config["max_image_longest_side"],
    )
    return {"text": text, "regions": None}


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Pipeline B — detectron2 layout detection, then Qwen2-VL-2B per region
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def pipeline_qwen2vl_2b_layout(image: np.ndarray, qwen_model, qwen_processor,
                                layout_model, config: dict) -> dict:
    ordered_regions = detect_and_order_regions(image, layout_model, config["layout_column_gap_px"])

    if not ordered_regions:
        # Layout model found nothing usable -- fall back to full-page OCR
        # rather than silently returning empty text.
        fallback = pipeline_qwen2vl_2b_raw(image, qwen_model, qwen_processor, config)
        fallback["regions"] = []
        fallback["fallback_to_full_page"] = True
        return fallback

    region_texts = []
    for region in ordered_regions:
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = [int(v) for v in region["bbox"]]
        x1, y1 = max(0, x1), max(0, y1)
        x2, y2 = min(image.shape[1], x2), min(image.shape[0], y2)
        if x2 <= x1 or y2 <= y1:
            continue
        crop = image[y1:y2, x1:x2]
        if region["type"] == "Figure":
            # Skip pure figures/ads for text OCR, per your OCR_PROMPT policy
            # of skipping non-text visual content -- but keep the region
            # logged so you can see what was excluded.
            region["ocr_skipped"] = True
            continue
        crop_text = run_vlm_ocr(
            crop, qwen_model, qwen_processor, OCR_PROMPT_REGION,
            config["max_new_tokens"], config["max_image_longest_side"],
        )
        region["ocr_skipped"] = False
        region_texts.append(crop_text)

    combined_text = "\n\n".join(region_texts)
    return {"text": combined_text, "regions": ordered_regions, "fallback_to_full_page": False}


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Pipeline C — raw olmOCR-2-7B, no layout step
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def pipeline_olmocr_7b_raw(image: np.ndarray, model, processor, config: dict) -> dict:
    text = run_vlm_ocr(
        image, model, processor, OCR_PROMPT_FULL_PAGE,
        config["max_new_tokens"], config["max_image_longest_side"],
    )
    return {"text": text, "regions": None}


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Orchestration — runs all requested pipelines on the same page(s),
# times each, and saves outputs into separate folders.
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def run(config: dict) -> dict:
    os.makedirs(config["output_dir"], exist_ok=True)
    pipeline_dirs = {}
    for name in config["run_pipelines"]:
        d = os.path.join(config["output_dir"], name)
        os.makedirs(d, exist_ok=True)
        pipeline_dirs[name] = d

    # Load only the models needed for the requested pipelines.
    qwen_model = qwen_processor = None
    olmocr_model = olmocr_processor = None
    layout_model = None

    if "qwen2vl_2b_raw" in config["run_pipelines"] or "qwen2vl_2b_layout" in config["run_pipelines"]:
        qwen_model, qwen_processor = load_qwen2vl_2b(config["qwen2vl_model_id"], config["use_4bit_quant"])
    if "olmocr_7b_raw" in config["run_pipelines"]:
        olmocr_model, olmocr_processor = load_olmocr_7b(config["olmocr_model_id"], config["use_4bit_quant"])
    if "qwen2vl_2b_layout" in config["run_pipelines"]:
        layout_model = load_layout_model(config["layout_model_config"], config["layout_score_thr"])

    page_list = get_page_list(config)
    print(f"Mode: {config['mode']} | Pages to process: {page_list}")
    print(f"Pipelines: {config['run_pipelines']}")

    summary = {"config": {k: v for k, v in config.items()}, "pages": {}}

    for page_number in page_list:
        print(f"\n=== Page {page_number} ===")
        raw_image = extract_page_as_image(config["pdf_path"], page_number, dpi=config["dpi"])
        processed_image, preprocess_log = preprocess_page(raw_image, config)
        print(f"  Preprocessing applied: {preprocess_log}")

        page_summary = {"preprocessing_applied": preprocess_log, "pipelines": {}}

        if "qwen2vl_2b_raw" in config["run_pipelines"]:
            t0 = time.time()
            result = pipeline_qwen2vl_2b_raw(processed_image, qwen_model, qwen_processor, config)
            elapsed = time.time() - t0
            _save_result(pipeline_dirs["qwen2vl_2b_raw"], page_number, result)
            page_summary["pipelines"]["qwen2vl_2b_raw"] = {"seconds": round(elapsed, 2), "num_regions": None}
            print(f"  [qwen2vl_2b_raw] {elapsed:.1f}s")

        if "qwen2vl_2b_layout" in config["run_pipelines"]:
            t0 = time.time()
            result = pipeline_qwen2vl_2b_layout(processed_image, qwen_model, qwen_processor, layout_model, config)
            elapsed = time.time() - t0
            _save_result(pipeline_dirs["qwen2vl_2b_layout"], page_number, result)
            page_summary["pipelines"]["qwen2vl_2b_layout"] = {
                "seconds": round(elapsed, 2),
                "num_regions": len(result["regions"]) if result["regions"] is not None else 0,
                "fallback_to_full_page": result.get("fallback_to_full_page", False),
            }
            print(f"  [qwen2vl_2b_layout] {elapsed:.1f}s, "
                  f"{page_summary['pipelines']['qwen2vl_2b_layout']['num_regions']} regions")

        if "olmocr_7b_raw" in config["run_pipelines"]:
            t0 = time.time()
            result = pipeline_olmocr_7b_raw(processed_image, olmocr_model, olmocr_processor, config)
            elapsed = time.time() - t0
            _save_result(pipeline_dirs["olmocr_7b_raw"], page_number, result)
            page_summary["pipelines"]["olmocr_7b_raw"] = {"seconds": round(elapsed, 2), "num_regions": None}
            print(f"  [olmocr_7b_raw] {elapsed:.1f}s")

        summary["pages"][str(page_number)] = page_summary

    summary_path = os.path.join(config["output_dir"], "comparison_summary.json")
    with open(summary_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(summary, f, indent=2)
    print(f"\nSaved comparison summary: {summary_path}")

    return summary


def _save_result(out_dir: str, page_number: int, result: dict) -> None:
    text_path = os.path.join(out_dir, f"page_{page_number:04d}.txt")
    with open(text_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        f.write(result["text"])
    if result.get("regions") is not None:
        regions_path = os.path.join(out_dir, f"page_{page_number:04d}_regions.json")
        with open(regions_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            json.dump(result["regions"], f, indent=2)


# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
# CLI — optional; safe to ignore in a notebook, just edit DEFAULT_CONFIG
# and call run(DEFAULT_CONFIG) directly.
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
def build_config() -> dict:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="BYTE Magazine 3-pipeline OCR comparison")
    parser.add_argument("--pdf", dest="pdf_path")
    parser.add_argument("--mode", choices=["single", "range", "all"])
    parser.add_argument("--page", type=int)
    parser.add_argument("--page-spec", dest="page_spec")
    parser.add_argument("--output-dir", dest="output_dir")
    parser.add_argument("--pipelines", nargs="+",
                         choices=["qwen2vl_2b_raw", "qwen2vl_2b_layout", "olmocr_7b_raw"],
                         dest="run_pipelines")
    parser.add_argument("--no-quant", dest="use_4bit_quant", action="store_false")
    args, _unknown = parser.parse_known_args()

    config = dict(DEFAULT_CONFIG)
    for key, value in vars(args).items():
        if value is not None:
            config[key] = value
    return config


if __name__ == "__main__":
    cfg = build_config()
    run(cfg)

Since your Makefile/scripts run from the repo root (e.g. `python scripts/run_ingest.py`), `.env` belongs at the **repo root**, not inside `src/doc-agent/`. **2. `settings.py` — nothing to write**

**3. `logging_conf.py` — what to actually implement**

`get_logger()` is done (also FIXED). The only real work is the `register()` function's `_trace` closure, which currently just raises `NotImplementedError`. It needs to append each `ctx` dict to `traces/run.jsonl` as a JSON line — this is your auditable-NFR trail that A3's agentic-behavior check reads to confirm the agent's path actually depends on observations. Rough shape:

```python
import json
from pathlib import Path

def register(hooks) -> None:
    trace_path = Path("traces/run.jsonl")
    trace_path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    def _trace(ctx: dict) -> dict:
        with trace_path.open("a", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            f.write(json.dumps(ctx) + "\n")
        return ctx

    hooks.register(hooks.ON_STEP, _trace)
    hooks.register(hooks.ON_TOOL_CALL, _trace)
    hooks.register(hooks.AFTER_ANSWER, _trace)
```

This is really an A3 (agent-loop) concern rather than A2 — you can implement it now since it's small, but it won't be exercised until `hooks`/the agent loop exists.

AI: 1. **pydantic-settings** — a library that reads config/secrets from environment variables (or a `.env` file) into a typed Python object, with validation. Instead of `os.environ.get("LLM_API_KEY")` scattered everywhere (untyped, error-prone), you get `settings.llm_api_key` as a validated string, defined once in `settings.py`.

2. **Auditable NFR trail** — a log of every step/tool-call the agent makes, written to `traces/run.jsonl`, so anyone can later reconstruct exactly what the agent did and why. This isn't your NFR (yours is **scalable**) — "auditable" is one of the *other* NFR options other teams might pick. You're implementing this piece anyway because `logging_conf.py` is a **mandatory** file for everyone regardless of your chosen NFR (per the Codebase Guide: "Governance and data files are mandatory").

3. **What they are:**
   - **LLM API key** — credentials for whichever model provider you call from `llm/client.py` (e.g. OpenAI, Anthropic, OpenRouter, or a self-hosted endpoint). Get it from that provider's dashboard after creating an account.
   - **WANDB API key** — Weights & Biases, an experiment-tracking service (logs training runs, metrics, charts). Get it free at wandb.ai → sign up → **Settings → API keys**.

   **Do you need both?** Not necessarily right now:
   - You're using **pretrained, locally-run models** (Qwen2-VL-2B / olmOCR-2-7B) for OCR — no LLM API call needed for that.
   - You'll likely need an **LLM API key** later for the *agent's reasoning brain* (A3) — unless you're also self-hosting that.
   - **WANDB** is optional infra for tracking metrics/experiments — useful but not strictly required unless your instructor mandates it for evaluation logging. Leave it blank in `.env` if unused; `settings.py` defaults both to `""` so nothing breaks.

```python
"""FIXED data contracts. Do not change fields or types."""
from __future__ import annotations
from pydantic import BaseModel

class Page(BaseModel):
    id: str
    image_path: str
    doc_id: str

class Region(BaseModel):
    page_id: str
    bbox: tuple[int, int, int, int]
    kind: str  # text | table | figure | heading

class Chunk(BaseModel):
    id: str
    doc_id: str
    text: str
    page_ids: list[str]
    score: float = 0.0        # relevance score set by retrieval; decide() reads the top score to judge weak evidence

class Query(BaseModel):
    text: str
    verifiable: bool          # True = checkable by exact match (anchors objective grading + RLVR)
    judged: bool              # True = scored by LLM-judge / human (non-verifiable inference)

class Citation(BaseModel):
    chunk_id: str
    span: tuple[int, int]

class Answer(BaseModel):
    text: str
    citations: list[Citation]
    grounded: bool
    confidence: float

class ToolResult(BaseModel):
    ok: bool
    payload: dict


class TraceStep(BaseModel):
    """One agent step, emitted to traces/run.jsonl so the A3 agentic-feature check can read the path."""
    step: int
    tool: str                 # tool name, or "decide" / "answer"
    args: dict                # e.g. {"query": "..."} — for retrieve, the query actually used
    obs: dict                 # what decide() saw, e.g. {"top_score": 0.31, "n": 10}

# MANDATORY agentic behaviour (graded in A3): evidence-gated re-search — decide() must re-retrieve with a
# reformulated query when the top evidence is weak. That runtime branch is what makes the system an agent.
# (multi-hop -> recall NFR, verify-and-correct -> precision NFR, tool-routing -> bonus; see the codebase guide.)




this is the contracts.py 
```
AI: Good — now I can implement this against the real contract. Two things jump out from `Page`'s fields:

- **No `page_num` field** — only `id`, `image_path`, `doc_id`. So page number has to live *inside* `id` as a convention (e.g. `"byte_1978-01_p0012"`), which later stages parse back out when they need it. Worth noting in `provenance.md` since it's an implicit contract nothing enforces except convention.
- **No explicit "raw scan" vs "rendered image" distinction** — `Page.image_path` just points to *some* image file. Since your corpus lands as PDFs in `data/raw/` (matching your earlier OCR script's `pdf_path` pattern), `load_pages()` needs to actually render each PDF page to an image file first, then point `Page.image_path` at that.

**Implementation, assuming `data/raw/` holds one PDF per issue** (tell me if `get_data.sh` actually saves something different — e.g. pre-rendered images or one PDF for the whole corpus — and I'll adjust):

```python
"""Stage 1 — load scanned page-images"""
from __future__ import annotations
from pathlib import Path
import pymupdf as fitz  # PyMuPDF
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

RENDERED_IMAGE_DIR = "data/interim/page_images"
DEFAULT_DPI = 300


def _render_page_image(pdf_path: Path, page_index: int, doc_id: str, dpi: int) -> str:
    """Render one page (0-indexed) to PNG. Cached: skips re-render if the
    file already exists, so re-running load_pages() is cheap."""
    out_dir = Path(RENDERED_IMAGE_DIR) / doc_id
    out_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    out_path = out_dir / f"page_{page_index + 1:04d}.png"
    if out_path.exists():
        return str(out_path)

    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    page = doc[page_index]
    pix = page.get_pixmap(matrix=fitz.Matrix(dpi / 72, dpi / 72))
    pix.save(str(out_path))
    doc.close()
    return str(out_path)


def load_pages(cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
    """Read data/raw/ -> list[Page].

    Assumes data/raw/ holds one PDF per issue, filename stem used as
    doc_id (e.g. byte_1978-01.pdf -> doc_id "byte_1978-01"). Each page
    is rendered once to data/interim/page_images/<doc_id>/page_NNNN.png
    and returned as Page(id="<doc_id>_pNNNN", image_path=..., doc_id=...).
    Page number is NOT a separate contract field -- it's encoded in `id`
    (1-indexed, zero-padded to 4 digits); downstream stages needing the
    number parse it back out of `id`.
    """
    raw_dir = Path(cfg.get("raw_data_dir", "data/raw"))
    dpi = cfg.get("dpi", DEFAULT_DPI)

    pdf_paths = sorted(raw_dir.glob("*.pdf"))
    if not pdf_paths:
        raise FileNotFoundError(
            f"No PDFs found in {raw_dir} -- did scripts/get_data.sh run successfully?"
        )

    pages: list[Page] = []
    for pdf_path in pdf_paths:
        doc_id = pdf_path.stem
        doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
        num_pages = doc.page_count
        doc.close()

        for page_index in range(num_pages):
            image_path = _render_page_image(pdf_path, page_index, doc_id, dpi)
            page_id = f"{doc_id}_p{page_index + 1:04d}"
            pages.append(Page(id=page_id, image_path=image_path, doc_id=doc_id))

    return pages
```

**Standalone unit test — run this before touching `preprocess.py` or `pipeline.py`:**

```python
"""test_loader.py — run with: pytest test_loader.py -v
Creates a tiny throwaway 2-page PDF so this needs no real corpus data."""
import pymupdf as fitz
from pathlib import Path
from src.doc_agent.ingest.loader import load_pages  # adjust import path to your actual package layout


def _make_fake_pdf(path: Path, num_pages: int = 2):
    doc = fitz.open()
    for i in range(num_pages):
        page = doc.new_page()
        page.insert_text((72, 72), f"Fake page {i + 1}")
    doc.save(str(path))
    doc.close()


def test_load_pages_basic(tmp_path):
    raw_dir = tmp_path / "raw"
    raw_dir.mkdir()
    _make_fake_pdf(raw_dir / "byte_1978-01.pdf", num_pages=2)

    cfg = {"raw_data_dir": str(raw_dir), "dpi": 150}
    pages = load_pages(cfg)

    assert len(pages) == 2
    assert pages[0].id == "byte_1978-01_p0001"
    assert pages[0].doc_id == "byte_1978-01"
    assert Path(pages[0].image_path).exists()
    assert pages[1].id == "byte_1978-01_p0002"


def test_load_pages_missing_dir(tmp_path):
    cfg = {"raw_data_dir": str(tmp_path / "nonexistent")}
    try:
        load_pages(cfg)
        assert False, "expected FileNotFoundError"
    except FileNotFoundError:
        pass


def test_load_pages_multiple_pdfs(tmp_path):
    raw_dir = tmp_path / "raw"
    raw_dir.mkdir()
    _make_fake_pdf(raw_dir / "byte_1978-01.pdf", num_pages=1)
    _make_fake_pdf(raw_dir / "byte_1978-02.pdf", num_pages=3)

    pages = load_pages({"raw_data_dir": str(raw_dir)})
    doc_ids = {p.doc_id for p in pages}
    assert doc_ids == {"byte_1978-01", "byte_1978-02"}
    assert len(pages) == 4
```

This needs **zero real corpus data** — it builds a fake 2-page PDF on the fly with PyMuPDF itself, so you can confirm the contract shape, ID convention, caching behavior, and error handling are all correct before ever touching your actual BYTE files. Run `pytest test_loader.py -v` — all three should pass.

"Reading it from `cfg`" just means: instead of hardcoding `dpi=300` inside the function, the function asks the config dict for it (`cfg.get("dpi", 300)`), so you can change resolution by editing `config.yaml` instead of editing code. Me: pipeline.py 

```python
"""FIXED end-to-end order (Stages 0-9) + cross-cutting seams.
Do not reorder stages or remove hooks.run()/register_all() calls."""
from __future__ import annotations
from . import config, hooks, wiring  # noqa: F401
from .ingest import loader, preprocess, enhance
from .vision import layout, ocr
from .index import chunk, embed, store
from .retrieval import retriever
from .agent import agent

def build_knowledge_base(cfg: dict) -> None:
    wiring.register_all(cfg)                        # wire cross-cutting features
    pages = loader.load_pages(cfg)
    pages = preprocess.run(pages, cfg)
    pages = enhance.run(pages, cfg)                 # Stage 1 - enhancement (VAE/diffusion)
    hooks.run(hooks.AFTER_INGEST, {"pages": pages})
    regions = layout.detect(pages, cfg)             # Stage 2
    text = ocr.transcribe(regions, cfg)             # Stage 3
    hooks.run(hooks.AFTER_OCR, {"chunks": text})    # e.g. PII redaction on extracted text
    chunks = chunk.split(text, cfg)                 # Stage 4
    hooks.run(hooks.BEFORE_INDEX, {"chunks": chunks})
    vectors = embed.encode(chunks, cfg)
    store.build(chunks, vectors, cfg)

def answer(query_text: str, cfg: dict):
    wiring.register_all(cfg)
    r = retriever.Retriever(cfg)                    # Stage 5
    return agent.Agent(cfg, r).run(query_text)      # Stage 6 (seams run inside the loop)

config.py
"""FIXED config loader."""
from __future__ import annotations
import yaml
from pathlib import Path

def load(path: str | Path = "configs/config.yaml") -> dict:
    with open(path) as f:
        return yaml.safe_load(f)

def load_task(path: str | Path = "configs/task.yaml") -> dict:
    with open(path) as f:
        return yaml.safe_load(f)

```

Me: Cross-cutting features (grounding, security, PII, tracing) don't live in one file: their logic sits in an owner module and they run at fixed seams via `hooks.py`, wired in `wiring.py`. See `docs/cross-cutting.md` for the full owner→touchpoint map.
what does this mean?

AI: This describes the **hooks/wiring pattern** you can already see in your actual `pipeline.py` — let me connect it to what you've got.

**"Cross-cutting features don't live in one file"** — things like grounding, PII redaction, security/injection defence, and audit tracing aren't self-contained modules you call once. They need to activate at *multiple different points* throughout the pipeline. PII redaction, for example, might need to run after OCR (`AFTER_OCR`) *and* before an answer is returned — not just in one place.

**"Owner module"** — each feature still has one real home where its logic is actually written (e.g. PII redaction logic lives in `governance/pii.py`, tracing logic lives in your `logging_conf.py`'s `register()` function you already implemented).

**"Runs at fixed seams via hooks.py"** — a "seam" is a named point in the pipeline where cross-cutting code is allowed to hook in. Your `pipeline.py` already shows these explicitly:
```python
hooks.run(hooks.AFTER_INGEST, {"pages": pages})
hooks.run(hooks.AFTER_OCR, {"chunks": text})    # e.g. PII redaction on extracted text
hooks.run(hooks.BEFORE_INDEX, {"chunks": chunks})
```
`AFTER_INGEST`, `AFTER_OCR`, `BEFORE_INDEX` are the seams — fixed points where any registered feature's code gets a chance to run, without the main pipeline stages (`loader`, `ocr`, `chunk`, etc.) needing to know or care that PII redaction/tracing/grounding exist at all.

**"Wired in wiring.py"** — `register_all(cfg)` (called at the top of both `build_knowledge_base()` and `answer()`) is where each feature's function gets *attached* to its seam(s) — e.g. wiring might do `hooks.register(hooks.AFTER_OCR, pii.redact)`. This is exactly the same pattern you already implemented for tracing in `logging_conf.py`'s `register()` — that's a cross-cutting feature (audit trail) hooking into `ON_STEP`/`ON_TOOL_CALL`/`AFTER_ANSWER`.

**Practical takeaway:** the design keeps your pipeline stages "clean" — `loader.py`/`ocr.py`/`chunk.py` never import or call PII/tracing/grounding code directly. All of that gets attached from the outside, at declared seams, via `wiring.py`. `docs/cross-cutting.md` should be your reference table of which feature owns which file and which seam(s) it hooks into — worth reading before you implement `governance/pii.py` or anything else touching a seam, so you wire it the same way rather than inventing a new pattern.

what does author mean

AI: `[author]` means the deliverable is **content you write directly** — YAML, Markdown, JSON, test cases — not Python code you're implementing inside a function body.

**Concretely, from what you've already worked on:** things like `configs/task.yaml`, `configs/config.yaml`, `data/provenance.md`, `grading_kit/manifest.yaml`, `grading_kit/tasks.jsonl`, `configs/design_choices.md`, `reports/pipeline_diagram.md` — these are all `[author]` tasks. You're not writing a function that generates them; you're just directly typing the correct values/documentation into the file yourself, based on your project's real decisions (your axes, your corpus, your model choices).

**How it differs from the other tags:**
- **`[fill]`** = write the body of a Python function that already has a signature/stub (like `load_pages()`) — this is what you did for `loader.py`.
- **`[wire]`** = write logic AND connect it to a hook seam via `wiring.py` (like the PII redaction or tracing example from earlier — `hooks.register(hooks.AFTER_OCR, pii.redact)`).
- **`[author]`** = write config/docs/tests by hand, no code logic needed — just correct content.
- **`[read-only]`** = never touch it at all (`contracts.py`, `pipeline.py`'s stage order, `settings.py`).

So when you see a file tagged `[author]` in the codebase guide, that's your signal: don't hunt for a function stub to implement — just fill in the file directly with your project's real values, same as you did for `task.yaml`.

from **future** import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from pdf2image import convert\_from\_path

from ..contracts import Page

RAW\_DIR = Path("data/raw")
PAGE\_DIR = Path("data/interim/pages")

def load\_pages(cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
"""Read data/raw/ and return scanned pages as Page objects."""

```
pdf_files = sorted(RAW_DIR.glob("*.pdf"))

if not pdf_files:
    raise FileNotFoundError(
        f"No PDF files found in {RAW_DIR.resolve()}"
    )

if len(pdf_files) > 1:
    raise ValueError(
        f"Expected one PDF in {RAW_DIR}, "
        f"but found {len(pdf_files)}. "
        "Process one issue at a time."
    )

pdf_path = pdf_files[0]
doc_id = pdf_path.stem

output_dir = PAGE_DIR / doc_id
output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

pages: list[Page] = []

page_count = 1

while True:
    image_path = output_dir / f"page_{page_count:04d}.png"

    if image_path.exists():
        pages.append(
            Page(
                id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_count:04d}",
                image_path=str(image_path),
                doc_id=doc_id,
            )
        )
        page_count += 1
        continue

    try:
        images = convert_from_path(
            str(pdf_path),
            dpi=150,
            first_page=page_count,
            last_page=page_count,
            fmt="png",
        )
    except Exception as exc:
        if page_count == 1:
            raise RuntimeError(
                f"Failed to convert the first page of {pdf_path}"
            ) from exc
        break

    if not images:
        break

    image = images[0]
    image.save(image_path, "PNG")

    pages.append(
        Page(
            id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_count:04d}",
            image_path=str(image_path),
            doc_id=doc_id,
        )
    )

    page_count += 1

return pages
```

what is this doing 

AI: Ah yes — this cell is basically **editing your `loader.py` file from inside the Kaggle notebook**. It is **not running the loader** yet.

Think of it as:

```text
Kaggle notebook
      ↓
writes this code
      ↓
src/doc_agent/ingest/loader.py
```

### What each part does

```python
loader_path = Path("src/doc_agent/ingest/loader.py")
```

Finds the `loader.py` file in your cloned repository.

Then:

```python
loader_path.write_text(''' ... ''')
```

**replaces the entire contents of `loader.py`** with the Python code between the triple quotes.

So after this cell runs, your actual file contains the loader implementation.

---

### What the loader itself does

The code inside the triple quotes does:

```text
data/raw/
    ↓
find PDFs
    ↓
make sure exactly ONE exists
    ↓
take that PDF
    ↓
convert page 1 → PNG
    ↓
save PNG
    ↓
create Page object
    ↓
convert page 2 → PNG
    ↓
...
```

This part:

```python
pdf_files = sorted(RAW_DIR.glob("*.pdf"))
```

finds all PDFs in:

```text
data/raw/
```

This:

```python
if len(pdf_files) > 1:
    raise ValueError(...)
```

makes sure you don't accidentally process multiple issues at once.

Then:

```python
pdf_path = pdf_files[0]
doc_id = pdf_path.stem
```

selects the PDF and derives its document ID from the filename.

For example:

```text
1975_09_BYTE_00-01_The_Worlds_Greatest_Toy.pdf
```

becomes:

```text
doc_id =
1975_09_BYTE_00-01_The_Worlds_Greatest_Toy
```

Then:

```python
output_dir = PAGE_DIR / doc_id
```

creates:

```text
data/interim/pages/
└── 1975_09_BYTE_00-01_The_Worlds_Greatest_Toy/
```

---

### The important bit

This:

```python

    str(pdf_path),

    first_page=page_count,
```

means:

> "Give me **only this one PDF page** as an image."

So instead of converting the whole magazine at once, it does:

```text
page 1 → convert → save
page 2 → convert → save
page 3 → convert → save
...
```

That's why this implementation is much more memory-friendly.
