AI: Yep — **3 pages is a much better sanity test**. If all 3 convert, save correctly, and come back as valid `Page` objects, we have much more confidence in the loader before letting it loose on the whole issue.

You only need to change **two things**: the `loader.py` cell and the test cell. Your last two test cells are duplicates, so you only need **one**.

### 1. Replace your `loader.py` notebook cell with this

```python
from pathlib import Path

loader_path = Path("src/doc_agent/ingest/loader.py")

loader_path.write_text('''"""Stage 1 — load scanned page-images."""

from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from pdf2image import convert_from_path

from ..contracts import Page


RAW_DIR = Path("data/raw")
PAGE_DIR = Path("data/interim/pages")


def load_pages(cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
    """Read data/raw/ and load the first three pages as Page objects."""

    pdf_files = sorted(RAW_DIR.glob("*.pdf"))

    if not pdf_files:
        raise FileNotFoundError(
            f"No PDF files found in {RAW_DIR.resolve()}"
        )

    if len(pdf_files) > 1:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Expected one PDF in {RAW_DIR}, "
            f"but found {len(pdf_files)}. "
            "Process one issue at a time."
        )

    pdf_path = pdf_files[0]
    doc_id = pdf_path.stem

    output_dir = PAGE_DIR / doc_id
    output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    pages: list[Page] = []

    for page_number in range(1, 4):
        image_path = output_dir / f"page_{page_number:04d}.png"

        if not image_path.exists():
            images = convert_from_path(
                str(pdf_path),
                dpi=150,
                first_page=page_number,
                last_page=page_number,
                fmt="png",
            )

            if not images:
                raise RuntimeError(
                    f"Failed to convert page {page_number} of {pdf_path}"
                )

            images[0].save(image_path, "PNG")

        pages.append(
            Page(
                id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_number:04d}",
                image_path=str(image_path),
                doc_id=doc_id,
            )
        )

    return pages
''')

print(f"Updated {loader_path}")
```

This will do:

```text
PDF
 ├── page 1 → PNG → Page
 ├── page 2 → PNG → Page
 └── page 3 → PNG → Page
```

and **not** convert the entire PDF.

Me: okay this works, so should i update the loader.py in my main repo, but pls tell me exactly which part is responsible for fixing the range in that code 

AI: Yep — **now that the 3-page test works, you should update the actual `loader.py` in your repo**, but I would **not** copy the 3-page version into the repo. That version has a hard-coded test range.

The exact line responsible for limiting the test to 3 pages is:

```python
for page_number in range(1, 4):
```

### For your 3-page test

```python
for page_number in range(1, 4):
```

means:

```text
1, 2, 3
```

because Python's `range` excludes the ending number.

### For the real full-issue loader

You **shouldn't change it to something like `range(1, 200)`**, because you don't know the number of pages in advance.

Instead, use the incremental `while True` approach we had earlier, where:

```python
page_number = 1

while True:
    ...
    images = convert_from_path(
        ...,
        first_page=page_number,
        last_page=page_number,
    )

    if not images:
        break

    ...
    page_number += 1
```

The important part is:

```python
first_page=page_number,
last_page=page_number,
```

That means **only one PDF page is rendered at a time**.

And:

```python
page_number += 1
```

moves to the next page.

When Poppler eventually returns no image:

```python
if not images:
    break
```

the loader knows it has reached the end of the PDF.

### So your final architecture should be

```text
PDF
 │
 ├── page_number = 1
 │      ↓
 │   render page 1
 │      ↓
 │   save page_0001.png
 │      ↓
 │   Page(...)
 │
 ├── page_number = 2
 │      ↓
 │   render page 2
 │      ↓
 │   save page_0002.png
 │      ↓
 │   Page(...)
 │
 ├── ...
 │
 └── no more pages
        ↓
       break
        ↓
   return list[Page]
```

**So yes: update the repo's `loader.py`, but use the incremental `while True` version, not the `range(1, 4)` test version.**

AI: Yep — here's the **full production version**. This removes the `range(1, 4)` limitation and processes the PDF **one page at a time until the PDF ends**.

```python
from pathlib import Path

loader_path = Path("src/doc_agent/ingest/loader.py")

loader_path.write_text('''"""Stage 1 — load scanned page-images."""

from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from pdf2image import convert_from_path

from ..contracts import Page

RAW_DIR = Path("data/raw")
PAGE_DIR = Path("data/interim/pages")


def load_pages(cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
    """Read data/raw/ and return scanned pages as Page objects."""

    pdf_files = sorted(RAW_DIR.glob("*.pdf"))

    if not pdf_files:
        raise FileNotFoundError(
            f"No PDF files found in {RAW_DIR.resolve()}"
        )

    if len(pdf_files) > 1:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Expected one PDF in {RAW_DIR}, "
            f"but found {len(pdf_files)}. "
            "Process one issue at a time."
        )

    pdf_path = pdf_files[0]
    doc_id = pdf_path.stem

    output_dir = PAGE_DIR / doc_id
    output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    pages: list[Page] = []
    page_number = 1

    while True:
        image_path = output_dir / f"page_{page_number:04d}.png"

        # Reuse an already-converted page if it exists.
        if image_path.exists():
            pages.append(
                Page(
                    id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_number:04d}",
                    image_path=str(image_path),
                    doc_id=doc_id,
                )
            )
            page_number += 1
            continue

        try:
            images = convert_from_path(
                str(pdf_path),
                dpi=150,
                first_page=page_number,
                last_page=page_number,
                fmt="png",
            )
        except Exception as exc:
            if page_number == 1:
                raise RuntimeError(
                    f"Failed to convert the first page of {pdf_path}"
                ) from exc
            break

        # No image means we have reached the end of the PDF.
        if not images:
            break

        images[0].save(image_path, "PNG")

        pages.append(
            Page(
                id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_number:04d}",
                image_path=str(image_path),
                doc_id=doc_id,
            )
        )

        page_number += 1

    return pages
''')

print(f"Updated {loader_path}")
```

### The key difference

Your test version had:

```python
for page_number in range(1, 4):
```

The production version has:

```python
page_number = 1

while True:
```

and increments:

```python
page_number += 1
```

until:

```python
if not images:
    break
```

So you **don't need to know how many pages the issue has**.

Also, this line:

```python
if image_path.exists():
```

means if the loader gets interrupted halfway through, rerunning it can **reuse pages that were already converted** instead of unnecessarily rendering those pages again. That's particularly useful for your large BYTE corpus.

AI: Yes — **the logic is fine for your VS Code repo**, and it's actually better suited to the real project than the 3-page test version.

There is just **one important thing to consider**: your pasted code has formatting artifacts from the chat (`**future**`, `convert\_from\_path`). In the actual `.py` file, they must be normal Python syntax.

Your actual `loader.py` should look exactly like this:

```python
"""Stage 1 — load scanned page-images."""

from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from pdf2image import convert_from_path

from ..contracts import Page

RAW_DIR = Path("data/raw")
PAGE_DIR = Path("data/interim/pages")


def load_pages(cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
    """Read data/raw/ and return scanned pages as Page objects."""

    pdf_files = sorted(RAW_DIR.glob("*.pdf"))

    if not pdf_files:
        raise FileNotFoundError(
            f"No PDF files found in {RAW_DIR.resolve()}"
        )

    if len(pdf_files) > 1:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Expected one PDF in {RAW_DIR}, "
            f"but found {len(pdf_files)}. "
            "Process one issue at a time."
        )

    pdf_path = pdf_files[0]
    doc_id = pdf_path.stem

    output_dir = PAGE_DIR / doc_id
    output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    pages: list[Page] = []
    page_number = 1

    while True:
        image_path = output_dir / f"page_{page_number:04d}.png"

        # Reuse an already-converted page if it exists.
        if image_path.exists():
            pages.append(
                Page(
                    id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_number:04d}",
                    image_path=str(image_path),
                    doc_id=doc_id,
                )
            )
            page_number += 1
            continue

        try:
            images = convert_from_path(
                str(pdf_path),
                dpi=150,
                first_page=page_number,
                last_page=page_number,
                fmt="png",
            )
        except Exception as exc:
            if page_number == 1:
                raise RuntimeError(
                    f"Failed to convert the first page of {pdf_path}"
                ) from exc
            break

        # No image means we have reached the end of the PDF.
        if not images:
            break

        images[0].save(image_path, "PNG")

        pages.append(
            Page(
                id=f"{doc_id}_p{page_number:04d}",
                image_path=str(image_path),
                doc_id=doc_id,
            )
        )

        page_number += 1

    return pages
```

### One VS Code-specific thing ⚠️

You'll need **Poppler installed on your local machine** for `pdf2image` to work.

On Ubuntu, for example:

```bash
sudo apt update
sudo apt install poppler-utils
```

Then verify:

```bash
which pdftoppm
```

You should get something like:

```text
/usr/bin/pdftoppm
```

And make sure your Python environment has:

```bash
pip install pdf2image
```

### One architectural note

Your current loader deliberately enforces:

```python
if len(pdf_files) > 1:
    raise ValueError(...)
```

That's **consistent with your current "one issue at a time" workflow**.

So:

```text
data/raw/
└── one_issue.pdf
        ↓
loader
        ↓
data/interim/pages/
└── one_issue/
    ├── page_0001.png
    ├── page_0002.png
    └── ...
```

That's perfectly reasonable for A2.

Later, when you want to ingest multiple issues into the corpus incrementally, we can modify the loader to process **each PDF independently** rather than forcing exactly one PDF in `data/raw/`. That change is separate from the page-by-page rendering logic.

```python

```

So preprocessing should **modify/create processed page images and return new `Page` objects**. We shouldn't invent new fields.

## 1. Kaggle: implement `preprocess.py`

Since you're editing the repository from the Kaggle notebook, use this cell:

```python
from pathlib import Path

preprocess_path = Path("src/doc_agent/ingest/preprocess.py")

preprocess_path.write_text('''"""Stage 1 — deskew / denoise / binarize / augment."""

from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from PIL import Image, ImageEnhance, ImageFilter

from ..contracts import Page


PROCESSED_DIR = Path("data/interim/preprocessed")


def run(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
    """Apply lightweight preprocessing and return processed Page objects."""

    PROCESSED_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    processed_pages: list[Page] = []

    for page in pages:
        input_path = Path(page.image_path)

        output_dir = PROCESSED_DIR / page.doc_id
        output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

        output_path = output_dir / input_path.name

        # Reuse an already-preprocessed page.
        if not output_path.exists():
            image = Image.open(input_path).convert("L")

            # Light contrast enhancement.
            image = ImageEnhance.Contrast(image).enhance(1.2)

            # Mild denoising.
            image = image.filter(ImageFilter.MedianFilter(size=3))

            image.save(output_path, "PNG")

        processed_pages.append(
            Page(
                id=page.id,
                image_path=str(output_path),
                doc_id=page.doc_id,
            )
        )

    return processed_pages
''')

print(f"Updated {preprocess_path}")
```

This is intentionally **lightweight** for the first test.

Me: but there isnt really anything to deskew as the scans appear pretty straight, there is need for denoise, but as the magazine is a colorful corpus, do you think binarizing will be an ideal approach, pls argue and state valid points, and also debate with your own self and find out what might be wrong with this approach 
and what is augment in this case?

AI: Yeah — I would **not make binarization the default preprocessing step** for your BYTE corpus. Your instinct about the scans being already straight is also reasonable.

Let's actually argue both sides, because this is one of those cases where "standard OCR preprocessing" can quietly make a colorful historical corpus worse.

### 1. Deskew: probably unnecessary

If your scans are consistently straight, adding deskew doesn't buy you much.

Deskew is useful when:

* the physical page was scanned at an angle,
* text baselines are visibly tilted,
* OCR struggles because of rotation.

But your loader is already producing the page image as scanned. If the pages look straight, running a deskew algorithm over **hundreds/thousands of pages** adds computation and introduces another transformation that can potentially:

* slightly resample text,
* blur fine characters,
* crop edges incorrectly,
* misinterpret large graphics as text orientation.

So I'd make deskew **optional**, not mandatory.

---

# 2. Denoising: YES, but gently

This is probably the most defensible preprocessing step for your corpus.

Old magazine scans can have:

* paper texture,
* scanner noise,
* speckles,
* printing artifacts,
* bleed-through,
* compression artifacts.

A **mild denoise** can help OCR distinguish:

```text
actual character
      ↓
from
      ↓
random scan noise
```

But there's a trap.

If you denoise too aggressively:

```text
original:
    i   l   1   !
```

can start becoming:

```text
    |   |   |
```

And tiny punctuation/code characters can disappear.

That matters **a LOT** for BYTE because you're dealing with technical material and code listings.

So I'd favor something like a mild median filter or similarly conservative denoising, then validate visually.

---

# 3. Binarization: here's where I'd push back

The traditional OCR pipeline often looks like:

```text
scan
 ↓
grayscale
 ↓
denoise
 ↓
binarize
 ↓
OCR
```

So you might reasonably think:

> "Why not do that?"

Because your corpus isn't a collection of clean black-and-white documents.

It's **BYTE magazine**.

You have:

* colored pages
* colored backgrounds
* advertisements
* diagrams
* screenshots
* illustrations
* highlighted text
* possibly colored headings
* boxes and sidebars
* different typography

Binarization turns something like:

```text
🟨 yellow background
black text
```

into something closer to:

```text
████████████
██ TEXT ████
████████████
```

depending on the threshold.

That can be helpful for OCR **if the only thing you care about is text recognition**.

But it destroys information.

---

# The strongest argument FOR binarization

There *is* a legitimate reason to use it.

OCR models ultimately need to recognize characters. A good adaptive threshold can sometimes make:

```text
faded black text
+
yellow-ish paper
+
scanner noise
```

much easier to recognize.

For example:

```text
original
      ↓
grayscale
      ↓
adaptive threshold
      ↓
high-contrast text
```

could improve OCR on particularly poor scans.

So binarization isn't inherently bad.

The problem is making it **the canonical representation of your corpus**.

---

# The strongest argument AGAINST it

Your system isn't simply:

> image → OCR

Your project is:

> scanned page → layout understanding → OCR → chunks → embeddings → retrieval → grounded answer

And your `layout.detect()` stage comes **before OCR**:

```python
pages = loader.load_pages(cfg)
pages = preprocess.run(pages, cfg)
pages = enhance.run(pages, cfg)

regions = layout.detect(pages, cfg)
text = ocr.transcribe(regions, cfg)
```

That's important.

A layout model may benefit from seeing:

> **the actual page**

rather than a heavily thresholded black-and-white representation.

A colored advertisement, a figure, a boxed code listing, a heading, and a normal paragraph may have useful visual distinctions.

If you binarize first, you potentially throw away those cues.

And there's another nasty problem:

### Colored text

Imagine:

```text
dark blue text
on
light blue background
```

A global threshold might decide:

```text
blue ≈ background
```

and make the text nearly disappear.

Or:

```text
red heading
on
yellow background
```

may have poor grayscale contrast even though the colors are visually very distinct.

So:

**binarization can improve some text OCR while simultaneously destroying information useful to layout detection and other visual understanding.**

That's a bad trade for your particular pipeline.

---

# 4. What about grayscale?

This is much more defensible than binarization.

You could preserve the original scan:

```text
original RGB
     │
     ├──────────────→ preserved original
     │
     ↓
grayscale copy
     ↓
OCR
```

That way you don't destroy the original.

But even grayscale isn't necessarily ideal for every VLM/layout model.

So I'd actually consider **keeping the original RGB image as the canonical input** and creating a processed derivative only when needed.

---

# 5. And what the hell does "augment" mean here? 😭

This is another thing I'd question in your stub.

**Data augmentation** normally means artificially modifying training examples to create additional training data.

For example:

```text
original page
    │
    ├── rotated 1°
    ├── slightly blurred
    ├── brightness changed
    ├── contrast changed
    └── cropped
```

You'd use those during **model training** to make the model robust.

For your inference/indexing pipeline, though, you generally **don't want to create five versions of every page**.

Otherwise:

```text
1 page
 ↓
5 augmented pages
 ↓
OCR
 ↓
5× chunks
 ↓
5× embeddings
 ↓
5× index entries
```

Now you've made your corpus larger and potentially introduced duplicate/redundant retrieval results.

For BYTE Retriever, that would be pretty pointless unless you specifically have a training stage where augmentation is needed.

So I'd say the `augment` comment in your stub is probably **too broad for the actual ingestion pipeline**.

---

# My recommendation for YOUR pipeline

I'd structure preprocessing like this:

```text
             ORIGINAL SCAN
                  │
                  │
          ┌───────┴────────┐
          │                │
          ↓                ↓
   preserve RGB       preprocessing
   original image          │
                           ↓
                    mild denoise
                           │
                           ↓
                  optional grayscale
                           │
                           ↓
                     layout/OCR
```

And **do not permanently replace the original scan**.

I'd also make preprocessing configurable:

```yaml
preprocess:
  enabled: true
  denoise: true
  grayscale: false
  binarize: false
  deskew: false
```

Although your current `config.yaml` doesn't have a `preprocess` section, so **we should not invent one casually** if the assignment says the config is fixed. We can either keep the preprocessing decisions as implementation defaults or discuss whether adding configuration is permitted.

### My ranking for your corpus

| Technique    | Recommendation     | Why                                       |
| ------------ | ------------------ | ----------------------------------------- |
| Preserve RGB | **Definitely**     | Don't destroy source information          |
| Mild denoise | **Yes**            | Historical scan noise can hurt OCR        |
| Deskew       | **Probably off**   | Your pages already appear straight        |
| Grayscale    | **Maybe**          | Useful for OCR, but test against RGB      |
| Binarization | **Off by default** | Risks destroying color/layout information |
| Augmentation | **No**             | Not useful for normal corpus ingestion    |

### One thing I'd change from the preprocessing code I gave you earlier

I previously suggested:

```python
image = Image.open(input_path).convert("L")
```

That **automatically throws away the color information**.

Given what you've now told me about the corpus, **I would NOT use that as the default implementation.** That's exactly the kind of assumption we should catch before committing it to your repo.

I'd instead preserve RGB and apply only conservative denoising initially.

Then we can empirically compare:

**A. Original RGB → OCR**

vs.

**B. RGB + mild denoise → OCR**

vs.

**C. Grayscale + mild denoise → OCR**

and only introduce binarization if it demonstrably improves OCR on representative BYTE pages.

That is much more defensible academically too: you're not saying *"we used binarization because OCR preprocessing usually does that"*; you're saying *"we evaluated preprocessing transformations against the characteristics of the corpus and avoided irreversible transformations that could degrade visual/layout information."*

from __future__ import annotations

    for page in pages:

        output_path = output_dir / input_path.name

        # Reuse an already-preprocessed page if it exists.
        if not output_path.exists():

                # Preserve the original RGB/color information.
                image = image.convert("RGB")

                # Apply mild denoising to reduce scan noise
                # without aggressively removing fine text details.

Me: but this does not really enhance the image 

AI: Yeah — that's the key distinction. **Denoising is not the same thing as enhancement.** Your current preprocessing only removes small noise; visually, it may barely change the page.

For your corpus, though, I would **not automatically add aggressive "enhancement"** either. The safest approach is:

* Preserve RGB.
* Mild denoising.
* Mild contrast enhancement **only if it demonstrably improves text readability**.
* No deskew.
* No binarization.
* No augmentation.

For BYTE scans, I'd test **mild contrast + mild sharpness + denoising** rather than just denoising.

Try this version on your 3 pages:

```python
from pathlib import Path

preprocess_path = Path("src/doc_agent/ingest/preprocess.py")

preprocess_path.write_text('''"""Stage 1 — mild enhancement and denoising for scanned page-images."""

from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from PIL import Image, ImageEnhance, ImageFilter

from ..contracts import Page


PROCESSED_DIR = Path("data/interim/preprocessed")


def run(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
    """Apply mild image enhancement while preserving page colors."""

    PROCESSED_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    processed_pages: list[Page] = []

    for page in pages:
        input_path = Path(page.image_path)

        output_dir = PROCESSED_DIR / page.doc_id
        output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

        output_path = output_dir / input_path.name

        if not output_path.exists():
            with Image.open(input_path) as image:
                # Preserve the original color information.
                image = image.convert("RGB")

                # Mild denoising.
                image = image.filter(
                    ImageFilter.MedianFilter(size=3)
                )

                # Mild contrast enhancement.
                image = ImageEnhance.Contrast(image).enhance(1.1)

                # Mild sharpening to recover fine text edges.
                image = ImageEnhance.Sharpness(image).enhance(1.1)

                image.save(output_path, "PNG")

        processed_pages.append(
            Page(
                id=page.id,
                image_path=str(output_path),
                doc_id=page.doc_id,
            )
        )

    return processed_pages
''')

print(f"Updated {preprocess_path}")
```

### Why only `1.1`?

Because we're not trying to make the scan look dramatically prettier. We're trying to make the **information easier for the downstream layout/OCR models to read**.

Think:

```text
Original
   ↓
tiny amount of denoising
   ↓
slightly stronger text/background separation
   ↓
slightly sharper character edges
   ↓
OCR
```

rather than:

```text
Original
   ↓
HEAVY enhancement
   ↓
"wow this looks processed"
   ↓
OCR gets confused by artifacts 💀
```

### And PLEASE delete the old processed files first

Because your code deliberately does:

```python
if not output_path.exists():
```

So it won't overwrite your previous black-and-white outputs.

Run:

```python
import shutil
from pathlib import Path

processed_dir = Path("data/interim/preprocessed")

if processed_dir.exists():
    shutil.rmtree(processed_dir)

print("Old preprocessed images removed.")
```

Then rerun the preprocessing test.

I'd also compare **original vs processed side-by-side** before committing this to the repo. If the processed image looks only subtly different but text edges are cleaner, that's actually a good result.

convert this code for preprocess.py in the git repo



and also mention in the comment why we didnt do binarize and deskew

AI: Yep. For the **actual Git repo**, `preprocess.py` should contain the Python code itself — not the notebook's `Path.write_text(...)` wrapper.

I'd also make the comments explicit about why we're **not** doing deskew, binarization, or augmentation, so your implementation rationale is visible in the repo.

```python
"""Stage 1 — mild enhancement and denoising for scanned page-images."""

from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from PIL import Image, ImageEnhance, ImageFilter

from ..contracts import Page


PROCESSED_DIR = Path("data/raw/preprocessed")


def run(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
    """Apply mild image enhancement while preserving page colors."""

    PROCESSED_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    processed_pages: list[Page] = []

    for page in pages:
        input_path = Path(page.image_path)

        output_dir = PROCESSED_DIR / page.doc_id
        output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

        output_path = output_dir / input_path.name

        # Reuse an already-preprocessed page if it exists.
        if not output_path.exists():
            with Image.open(input_path) as image:
                # Preserve the original RGB/color information because
                # BYTE is a colorful magazine corpus containing figures,
                # advertisements, diagrams, headings, and other visual
                # layout information useful to downstream models.
                image = image.convert("RGB")

                # Mild denoising to reduce scan noise while preserving
                # fine text and code characters.
                image = image.filter(
                    ImageFilter.MedianFilter(size=3)
                )

                # Mild contrast enhancement to improve text/background
                # separation without aggressively altering the scan.
                image = ImageEnhance.Contrast(image).enhance(1.1)

                # Mild sharpening to improve fine character edges.
                image = ImageEnhance.Sharpness(image).enhance(1.1)

                image.save(output_path, "PNG")

        processed_pages.append(
            Page(
                id=page.id,
                image_path=str(output_path),
                doc_id=page.doc_id,
            )
        )

    return processed_pages
```

### Why we're deliberately not doing these

I would keep these comments in the code/documentation:

* **Deskew:** The scanned BYTE pages are already sufficiently straight, so deskewing would add unnecessary processing and could introduce resampling/cropping artifacts.
* **Binarization:** BYTE is a colorful corpus. Converting pages to binary black/white images can destroy color, background, illustration, and layout information that may be useful for layout detection and VLM/OCR.
* **Augmentation:** This pipeline is preparing the corpus for inference/indexing, not training the OCR model, so generating rotated/altered copies would unnecessarily increase the corpus and create duplicate retrieval content.

One small thing: I kept your chosen:

```python
PROCESSED_DIR = Path("data/raw/preprocessed")
```

exactly as you specified. **But architecturally, I'd recommend `data/interim/preprocessed` instead**, because `data/raw` should ideally remain untouched source data. If your assignment's expected directory structure allows it, I'd change that before committing.

Me: # THE place you set models & parameters. Code reads only from here.

seed: 42
device: cuda

enhance:   {enabled: true,  model: "unet\_small", type: "diffusion"}   # or "vae" / "none"
layout:    {model: "detectron2\:layout", score\_thr: 0.5}
ocr:       {model: "microsoft/trocr-base-printed", finetune: true}    # BASELINE = pretrained OCR
embed:     {model: "sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2", dim: 384}
index:     {type: "faiss\:hnsw", chunk\_tokens: 256, overlap: 32}
retrieve:  {k: 10, k\_step: 10, k\_max: 40, weak\_threshold: 0.35, rerank: true, reranker: "cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L6-v2"}   # k\_step/k\_max/weak\_threshold drive evidence-gated re-search
agent:     {max\_steps: 8, autonomy: "act-with-approval", budget\_usd: 0.05}
rl:        {algo: "ppo", train\_policy: false}                         # bonus: set true
serve:     {batch: true}
deploy:    {target: "hf-spaces"}





so if the enabled is false , then i dont need to change anything in enhance.py do i?

AI: Exactly. **If you set `enabled: false`, you don't need to change the existing `enhance.py` at all.** Your existing `run()` already handles this:

```python
def run(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Page]:
    if not cfg["enhance"]["enabled"]:
        return pages
    return Enhancer(cfg).apply(pages)
```

So with:

```yaml
enhance: {enabled: false, model: "unet_small", type: "diffusion"}
```

the execution is simply:

```text
preprocess.run(pages, cfg)
        ↓
enhance.run(pages, cfg)
        ↓
enabled == false
        ↓
return pages
        ↓
layout.detect(...)
```

The `Enhancer` class is **never instantiated**, so these:

```python
def train(...):
    raise NotImplementedError(...)

def apply(...):
    raise NotImplementedError(...)
```

will never execute.

### So your changes are just:

**`config.yaml`:**

```yaml
enhance: {enabled: false, model: "unet_small", type: "diffusion"}
```

**`enhance.py`:** leave it exactly as the provided stub.

And honestly, that's preferable here because **the stub was provided to you and you're not required to implement a feature you're explicitly disabling**. Keep the architectural seam intact.

| Option                                      | Why I'd consider it                                          |   Kaggle pain | Fit for BYTE |

### 🥇 I'd pick DocLayout-YOLO

There are currently multiple YOLO-based models trained on **DocLayNet**, including YOLO11/YOLO26 variants. DocLayNet itself has **80,863 pages and 11 layout classes**, so it's substantially more diverse than simply training on generic natural images. ([Hugging Face][1])

For example, the YOLO11 document-layout implementation explicitly targets:

* text
* tables
* figures
* titles
* other document regions

and is designed for relatively efficient inference. ([Hugging Face][2])

That's much closer to what you need than fighting with an old Detectron2 stack.

### 🥈 PaddleOCR is also VERY interesting

I'd actually put **PP-DocLayoutV3 / PP-StructureV3** on your shortlist.

PaddleOCR's current document-analysis stack is designed specifically for complex document parsing, and PP-StructureV3 is intended to preserve document layout/hierarchy. ([GitHub][3])

More importantly for **your specific problem**, their document pipeline explicitly describes the first stage as:

> layout analysis → locate elements → determine reading order → crop elements

That's basically the problem you're trying to solve manually with `_sort_reading_order()`. ([GitHub][4])

So PaddleOCR could potentially eliminate some of the custom reading-order work.

I **wouldn't blindly replace your friend's `layout.py` yet**.

Your current contract is:


and:

```python

    page_id=...,
    bbox=(x0, y0, x1, y1),
    kind="text" | "table" | "figure" | "heading"
```

That's actually great because it means **the rest of your pipeline doesn't care what model performs the detection**.

You could have:

```text
                 ┌── Detectron2
                 │

                 │
                 └── PaddleOCR
                       ↓
                 list[Region]
                       ↓
                     OCR
```

As long as the model adapter produces your `Region` objects, everything downstream stays the same.

and have `layout.py` select the appropriate backend.

AI: Yes — there **is a defensible argument for keeping Detectron2**, especially given *your exact assignment*. But I wouldn't argue that Detectron2 is universally better than DocLayout-YOLO. The stronger argument is that **your existing Detectron2 + PubLayNet implementation may be the safer choice for this particular pipeline**, despite the horrible Kaggle installation experience. 😭

### The strongest case for Detectron2

**1. Your teammate already implemented the required interface around it.**

Your `layout.py` isn't merely using Detectron2; it already handles:

```text
Detectron2
    ↓
PubLayNet labels
    ↓
your _LABEL_MAP
    ↓
your Region contract
    ↓
your custom multi-column reading order
```

So replacing the model means you have to revalidate the entire adapter.

With Detectron2, you're mostly validating an implementation you already have.

---

**2. PubLayNet is a very established document-layout baseline.**

Your friend's model is:

```text
Faster R-CNN
ResNet-50
FPN
PubLayNet
```

PubLayNet was created specifically for document layout analysis and contains **over 360,000 document images**. It was designed around elements such as text, title, list, table, and figure. ([arXiv][1])

That's a very natural match for your contract:

```python
text
heading
table
figure
```

And LayoutParser explicitly provides this Detectron2/PubLayNet model through its model zoo. ([layout-parser.github.io][2])

So you aren't using some random object detector and hoping it understands documents.

---

### 3. Detectron2 gives you a mature object-detection architecture

Faster R-CNN + FPN is a **two-stage detector**.

Conceptually:

```text
page image
    ↓
backbone + FPN
    ↓
candidate regions
    ↓
classification + bounding-box refinement
    ↓
layout regions
```

That's useful when you care about **precise region boundaries**, rather than merely wanting extremely fast approximate detection.

For BYTE, that matters because your OCR stage will eventually operate on these regions.

If your bounding box is slightly wrong:

```text
┌─────────────────────┐
│ "The amazing world  │
│ of personal comput..."│
└─────────────────────┘
```

versus:

```text
┌───────────────────────────────┐
│ "The amazing world of ...     │
│ [part of neighboring column]  │
└───────────────────────────────┘
```

you can contaminate the OCR input with another column.

That is particularly nasty for your corpus.

---

### 4. Your actual reading-order problem is NOT solved simply by changing the detector

This is probably the biggest point.

DocLayout-YOLO is specifically designed to improve document-layout detection and reports strong speed/accuracy results. ([arXiv][3])

But your assignment's special problem is:

> **2–3 column BYTE pages + sidebars + boxed code listings → correct reading order**

Your friend's implementation explicitly addresses that:

```python
_sort_reading_order(...)
```

The pipeline is therefore:

```text
Detectron2
   ↓
bounding boxes
   ↓
group into columns
   ↓
left → right
   ↓
top → bottom
```

Switching to DocLayout-YOLO **doesn't automatically solve that problem**.

You'd still need to implement and validate equivalent reading-order logic.

So if your friend has already done that correctly, replacing the detector doesn't necessarily buy you much.

---

### 5. Your assignment values an auditable baseline

This is another subtle advantage.

Your project isn't:

> "Find the fanciest layout model available."

It's:

> build a complete retrieval/agentic pipeline with measurable NFRs.

A very defensible engineering choice is:

> **We use a pretrained PubLayNet Faster R-CNN baseline for layout detection and apply deterministic post-processing for BYTE's multi-column reading order.**

That's easy to explain and audit.

DocLayout-YOLO is newer and potentially better, but that doesn't automatically make it the better *project decision*. DocLayout-YOLO's paper itself frames the problem as a speed/accuracy tradeoff and introduces additional document-specific pretraining and architectural modifications. ([arXiv][3])

---

## BUT here's where I would push back against Detectron2

This is important.

### PubLayNet isn't particularly well matched to BYTE.

PubLayNet is heavily based on scientific articles. Its authors themselves describe its source as PubMed Central articles. ([arXiv][1])

And this is exactly the weakness of your current implementation.

BYTE contains:

* colorful magazine layouts
* advertisements
* sidebars
* unusual typography
* code listings
* decorative headings
* multiple columns
* magazine-specific visual structures

That's **not a scientific paper**.

And DocLayNet was explicitly created partly because existing datasets such as PubLayNet had **limited layout variability**, causing performance to drop on more diverse layouts. DocLayNet contains 80,863 manually annotated pages across diverse document sources and 11 layout classes. ([arXiv][4])

So if we were choosing purely based on **domain generalization**, I'd actually give DocLayNet-based models a serious advantage.

---

## And DocLayout-YOLO has another advantage

It's newer.

The DocLayout-YOLO paper specifically targets document layout analysis and introduces document-specific pretraining on **DocSynth-300K** plus a global-to-local perception mechanism. The authors report strong speed/accuracy performance across document types. ([arXiv][3])

So:

|                                 | Detectron2 + PubLayNet | DocLayout-YOLO   |
| ------------------------------- | ---------------------- | ---------------- |
| Mature baseline                 | 🟢                     | 🟡               |
| Existing implementation         | 🟢🟢                   | 🔴               |
| Your current reading-order code | 🟢                     | 🔴 needs adapter |
| Document-specific               | 🟢                     | 🟢🟢             |
| Diverse layouts                 | 🟡                     | 🟢               |
| Magazine suitability            | 🟡                     | 🟢 potentially   |
| Bounding-box detection          | 🟢                     | 🟢               |
| Speed                           | 🟡                     | 🟢               |
| Kaggle installation             | 💀                     | 🟢               |
| Easy to defend as baseline      | 🟢🟢                   | 🟢               |

### So my actual verdict

If you asked me:

> **"Which model is theoretically better for BYTE?"**

I'd say **I'd investigate DocLayout-YOLO/DocLayNet first**, because BYTE is much more diverse than scientific papers.

But if you ask:

> **"Should I abandon my teammate's already-working Detectron2 implementation right now?"**

**No.**

I'd first get the Detectron2 implementation running on **3 representative BYTE pages** and inspect:

1. Are text blocks detected?
2. Are columns separated correctly?
3. Are figures detected?
4. Are headings detected?
5. Are tables detected?
6. Does `_sort_reading_order()` actually produce sane order?

If it works reasonably well, **keep it**.

The Kaggle installation headache alone isn't a sufficient reason to replace a model that performs adequately.

And one particularly important point: **don't confuse model detection quality with reading-order quality.** Your project has a custom E2 requirement around multi-column reading order. That deterministic post-processing may matter more to your downstream OCR quality than whether the detector is Detectron2 or YOLO.

So I'd treat this as an **empirical decision**, not a "newer model = better model" decision.

i mean follow the flow of loader.py and preprocess.py 

AI: Yes. For your project, I’d make the DocLayout-YOLO implementation an **adapter** around the model so that the rest of your pipeline still sees exactly the same `Region` contract:

`Page → layout.detect() → list[Region]`

The current DocLayout-YOLO ecosystem has DocStructBench/DocLayNet checkpoints, and the models output bounding boxes, classes, and confidence scores. ([Hugging Face][1])

One caveat: **DocLayout-YOLO has several variants/checkpoints**, so I recommend using the `doclayout_yolo` package/checkpoint rather than inventing a model URL or weight filename. The official demo uses `YOLOv10` with a DocStructBench checkpoint. ([Hugging Face][2])

## 1. `layout.py`

Replace the stub in your repo with this:

```python
"""Stage 2 — layout detection / segmentation."""

from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

from PIL import Image

from ..contracts import Page, Region


# Map model-specific labels to our fixed Region contract.
#
# DocLayout-YOLO / DocStructBench has more classes than our contract,
# so several model classes are intentionally mapped to the closest
# supported Region kind.
_LABEL_MAP = {
    "title": "heading",
    "section-header": "heading",
    "plain_text": "text",
    "text": "text",
    "list-item": "text",
    "table": "table",
    "picture": "figure",
    "figure": "figure",
    "figure-caption": "text",
    "table-caption": "text",
    "page-header": "text",
    "page-footer": "text",
    "footnote": "text",
    "formula": "text",
}


_model = None


def _get_model(cfg: dict):
    """Lazy-load the DocLayout-YOLO model once."""

    global _model

    if _model is not None:
        return _model

    from doclayout_yolo import YOLOv10

    model_path = cfg.get("layout", {}).get(
        "model_path",
        "doclayout_yolo_docstructbench_imgsz1024.pt",
    )

    _model = YOLOv10(model_path)

    return _model


def _sort_reading_order(
    boxes: list[dict],
    page_width: int,
) -> list[dict]:
    """
    Sort detected regions into approximate multi-column reading order.

    BYTE commonly uses 2–3 running columns. We therefore group regions
    by their horizontal position first, then sort top-to-bottom inside
    each column.
    """

    if not boxes:
        return boxes

    # Tolerance for grouping regions into the same column.
    gap_px = 0.04 * page_width

    columns: list[list[dict]] = []

    for box in sorted(
        boxes,
        key=lambda b: b["bbox"][0],
    ):
        x0 = box["bbox"][0]
        placed = False

        for column in columns:
            column_x0 = min(
                b["bbox"][0]
                for b in column
            )

            if abs(x0 - column_x0) <= gap_px * 3:
                column.append(box)
                placed = True
                break

        if not placed:
            columns.append([box])

    # Left column → right column.
    columns.sort(
        key=lambda column: min(
            b["bbox"][0]
            for b in column
        )
    )

    # Top → bottom within each column.
    ordered: list[dict] = []

    for column in columns:
        column.sort(
            key=lambda b: b["bbox"][1]
        )
        ordered.extend(column)

    return ordered


def detect(
    pages: list[Page],
    cfg: dict,
) -> list[Region]:
    """
    Detect text/table/figure/heading regions.

    Returns regions in approximate BYTE multi-column reading order.
    """

    model = _get_model(cfg)

    regions: list[Region] = []

    score_thr = cfg.get("layout", {}).get(
        "score_thr",
        0.5,
    )

    for page in pages:

        image_path = Path(page.image_path)

        with Image.open(image_path) as image:
            image = image.convert("RGB")
            page_width, page_height = image.size

            # Run DocLayout-YOLO inference.
            results = model.predict(
                str(image_path),
                imgsz=1024,
                conf=score_thr,
                verbose=False,
            )

        boxes: list[dict] = []

        for result in results:

            if result.boxes is None:
                continue

            xyxy = result.boxes.xyxy.tolist()
            confidences = result.boxes.conf.tolist()
            classes = result.boxes.cls.tolist()

            for bbox, confidence, class_id in zip(
                xyxy,
                confidences,
                classes,
            ):
                confidence = float(confidence)

                if confidence < score_thr:
                    continue

                class_id = int(class_id)

                # Get model class name.
                if hasattr(result, "names"):
                    label = result.names[class_id]
                else:
                    label = str(class_id)

                label = label.lower().replace("_", "-")

                kind = _LABEL_MAP.get(
                    label,
                    "text",
                )

                x0, y0, x1, y1 = map(
                    int,
                    bbox,
                )

                # Clamp coordinates to page boundaries.
                x0 = max(0, min(x0, page_width))
                y0 = max(0, min(y0, page_height))
                x1 = max(0, min(x1, page_width))
                y1 = max(0, min(y1, page_height))

                if x1 <= x0 or y1 <= y0:
                    continue

                boxes.append(
                    {
                        "bbox": (x0, y0, x1, y1),
                        "kind": kind,
                        "score": confidence,
                    }
                )

        ordered_boxes = _sort_reading_order(
            boxes,
            page_width=page_width,
        )

        for box in ordered_boxes:
            regions.append(
                Region(
                    page_id=page.id,
                    bbox=box["bbox"],
                    kind=box["kind"],
                )
            )

    return regions
```

### Why I kept `_sort_reading_order()`

This part is **not the detector**.

It's your BYTE-specific post-processing:

```text
DocLayout-YOLO
      ↓
detected boxes
      ↓
column grouping
      ↓
left → right
      ↓
top → bottom
      ↓
Region objects
```

That's important because your corpus's 2–3-column layout is one of your data-speciality requirements.

---

Change it to something like:

**But:** the exact model filename depends on which checkpoint you download. Don't put a made-up filename into the repo.

The official DocLayout-YOLO demo uses a DocStructBench checkpoint named along the lines of `doclayout_yolo_docstructbench_imgsz1024.pt`. ([Hugging Face][3])

# 4. Kaggle test — follow your loader → preprocess flow

Since you've already tested those stages, your notebook should now look like this.

### Cell 1 — imports

```python
import importlib

from doc_agent import config
from doc_agent.ingest import loader, preprocess
```

## 5. Then check each page separately

This is the test I'd care about most:

```python
for page in processed_pages:
    page_regions = [
        r for r in regions
        if r.page_id == page.id
    ]

    print(
        f"{page.id}: "
        f"{len(page_regions)} regions"
    )

    for i, region in enumerate(page_regions, 1):
        print(
            f"  {i:02d}. "
            f"{region.kind:8s} "
            f"{region.bbox}"
        )
```

You want to see whether the ordering makes sense:

```text
PAGE 1

01 heading
02 text       ← left column, top
03 text
04 text
05 figure
06 text       ← left column, bottom
07 text       ← next column, top
08 text
09 table
...
```

rather than jumping:

```text
left column top
right column top
middle column middle
left column bottom
...
```

because **that reading order is what ultimately affects your OCR/chunking quality**.

from ..contracts import *  # noqa

class Reader:
    """Model set by cfg['ocr']. Baseline: pretrained TrOCR/Donut/Tesseract."""

AI: Yep. Now we're at **Stage 3**, and the current pipeline gives us a very clear input:

```text
PDF
 ↓
loader
 ↓
Page objects
 ↓
mild preprocessing
 ↓
processed Page objects
 ↓
DocLayout-YOLO
 ↓
Region objects (bbox + kind + page_id)
 ↓
OCR
 ↓
Chunk objects
```

One important point: **TrOCR is a text-recognition model, not a document-layout model.** So our layout detector gives us the region crop, and TrOCR reads the text inside that crop.

For the first experiment, I would **not fine-tune anything yet**. Let's get the pretrained baseline working on a few regions, inspect the output, and save it.

## 1. First: install the OCR dependencies in Kaggle

Run this cell:

```python
!pip install -q transformers sentencepiece
```

---

# 2. Edit `ocr.py`

For Kaggle, use the actual repo path like we did for `layout.py`.

```python
from pathlib import Path

ocr_path = Path(
    "/kaggle/working/doc-agent-G21/src/doc_agent/vision/ocr.py"
)

ocr_path.write_text('''"""Stage 3 — OCR using pretrained TrOCR."""

from __future__ import annotations

import json
from pathlib import Path

import torch
from PIL import Image

from ..contracts import Page, Region, Chunk


# Saved OCR output.
OCR_DIR = Path(
    "/kaggle/working/doc-agent-G21/data/interim/ocr"
)


class Reader:
    """Pretrained TrOCR reader for text regions."""

    def __init__(self, cfg: dict) -> None:
        self.cfg = cfg["ocr"]

        model_name = self.cfg.get(
            "model",
            "microsoft/trocr-base-printed",
        )

        from transformers import (
            TrOCRProcessor,
            VisionEncoderDecoderModel,
        )

        self.device = (
            "cuda"
            if torch.cuda.is_available()
            else "cpu"
        )

        print(f"Loading OCR model: {model_name}")
        print(f"OCR device: {self.device}")

        self.processor = (
            TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained(model_name)
        )

        self.model = (
            VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained(
                model_name
            )
        )

        self.model.to(self.device)
        self.model.eval()

        print("OCR model loaded successfully.")

    def transcribe_region(
        self,
        region: Region,
        page_images: dict[str, str],
    ) -> str:
        """Crop one detected region and transcribe its text."""

        image_path = page_images.get(region.page_id)

        if image_path is None:
            raise KeyError(
                f"No image found for page_id={region.page_id}"
            )

        with Image.open(image_path) as image:
            image = image.convert("RGB")

            x0, y0, x1, y1 = region.bbox

            # Keep coordinates inside the image.
            x0 = max(0, x0)
            y0 = max(0, y0)
            x1 = min(image.width, x1)
            y1 = min(image.height, y1)

            if x1 <= x0 or y1 <= y0:
                return ""

            crop = image.crop(
                (x0, y0, x1, y1)
            )

            pixel_values = self.processor(
                images=crop,
                return_tensors="pt",
            ).pixel_values.to(self.device)

            with torch.no_grad():
                generated_ids = self.model.generate(
                    pixel_values,
                    max_new_tokens=512,
                )

            text = self.processor.batch_decode(
                generated_ids,
                skip_special_tokens=True,
            )[0]

        return text.strip()


def transcribe(
    regions: list[Region],
    cfg: dict,
    pages: list[Page],
) -> list[Chunk]:
    """Convert detected regions into OCR text chunks."""

    if not regions:
        return []

    # Map page IDs to their processed image paths.
    page_images = {
        page.id: page.image_path
        for page in pages
    }

    reader = Reader(cfg)

    chunks: list[Chunk] = []

    for index, region in enumerate(regions):

        text = reader.transcribe_region(
            region,
            page_images,
        )

        # Skip completely empty OCR results.
        if not text:
            continue

        chunks.append(
            Chunk(
                id=f"{region.page_id}_r{index:04d}",
                doc_id=pages[0].doc_id,
                text=text,
                page_ids=[region.page_id],
            )
        )

    # Save OCR results.
    OCR_DIR.mkdir(
        parents=True,
        exist_ok=True,
    )

    doc_id = pages[0].doc_id

    output_path = (
        OCR_DIR / f"{doc_id}.json"
    )

    with output_path.open(
        "w",
        encoding="utf-8",
    ) as f:
        json.dump(
            [
                chunk.model_dump()
                for chunk in chunks
            ],
            f,
            indent=2,
            ensure_ascii=False,
        )

    print(
        f"Saved {len(chunks)} OCR chunks to "
        f"{output_path}"
    )

    return chunks
''')

print(f"Updated {ocr_path}")
```

### One thing to notice

I'm deliberately **not modifying `contracts.py`**.

Your existing:

```python
class Chunk(BaseModel):
    id: str
    doc_id: str
    text: str
    page_ids: list[str]
    score: float = 0.0
```

is enough for the OCR stage.

## ⚠️ One BIG caveat with TrOCR

This is important for your BYTE corpus.

**TrOCR-base-printed is primarily designed for printed text recognition, but a BYTE "region" can be an entire multi-line/multi-column block.**

For example, DocLayout-YOLO might give us:

```text
┌─────────────────────────┐
│ This is paragraph 1     │
│ This is paragraph 2     │
│ This is paragraph 3     │
│ This is paragraph 4     │
└─────────────────────────┘
```

and we're currently feeding that **whole block** to TrOCR.

That's not necessarily ideal.

TrOCR is much happier with something closer to:

```text
one text line
```

rather than a giant article region.

So **don't commit to TrOCR as the final OCR solution yet**.

Our immediate experiment should be:

```text
3–5 regions
       ↓
TrOCR
       ↓
inspect actual text
       ↓
compare against original crop
       ↓
decide whether TrOCR is adequate
```

If the output is terrible on BYTE's multi-line regions—which honestly wouldn't surprise me—we should consider a **line/paragraph-aware OCR approach or your VLM-based OCR idea** instead of forcing TrOCR into a job it wasn't really designed for.

And once the baseline works, we can modify the OCR stage to process **all 100 pages and persist the chunks**.

chunks = []
start_time = time.time()
for i, region in enumerate(regions, start=1):

        text = reader.transcribe_region(region)
        if text:
            doc_id = region.page_id.rsplit(
                "_p", 1
            )[0]

                doc_id=doc_id,

            f"region {i}/{len(regions)}: {exc}"

    # Checkpoint
    # --------------------------------------------------

                    chunk.model_dump()
                    for chunk in chunks
                ],
                f,
                ensure_ascii=False,
                indent=2,
            )
        elapsed = time.time() - start_time
        rate = i / elapsed if elapsed > 0 else 0
        print(
            f"\n[{i}/{len(regions)}] "
            f"OCR complete"
        )
        print(
            f"Chunks saved: {len(chunks)}"
        )
        print(
            f"Speed: {rate:.2f} regions/sec"
        )
        print(
            f"Output: {output_path}"
        )

    f"\nFinished OCR: "
    f"{len(chunks)} chunks generated."
)
print(
    f"Final output: "
    f"{output_path.resolve()}"
)

AI: Good instinct pushing this to Kaggle — and yes, **JSON is exactly the right format**, your reasoning was already correct. `Chunk` is a pydantic `BaseModel` with only plain types (`str`, `list[str]`, `float`) — `model_dump()` → JSON round-trips perfectly, and reloading is just `Chunk(**item)` or `Chunk.model_validate(item)`. That doesn't conflict with the contract at all — the contract governs what `transcribe()` **returns in memory** (`list[Chunk]`); how you persist it to disk along the way is entirely your call.

**The real fix: move the checkpointing *into* `transcribe()` itself**, not a separate Kaggle scratch script. Your existing `ocr.py` already defines `OCR_DIR = Path("data/interim/ocr")` but never uses it — that's the gap. Building it into the actual function gives you two things your standalone script doesn't: it becomes part of your graded repo (satisfies the Section 5 "OCR text on disk for evaluation" need from earlier), and it's automatically resumable if a Kaggle session dies mid-run — which matters a lot given how much environment instability you've already hit this week.

```python
def transcribe(
    regions: list[Region],
    cfg: dict,
) -> list[Chunk]:
    """Convert layout regions into OCR text chunks.
    Checkpoints incrementally to OCR_DIR/chunks.json and resumes from it
    on restart (skips regions whose chunk_id was already OCR'd) -- Kaggle
    sessions can die mid-run, this avoids losing completed work."""

    OCR_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    output_path = OCR_DIR / "chunks.json"
    checkpoint_every = cfg["ocr"].get("checkpoint_every", 25)

    # Resume: load any chunks already OCR'd from a previous run.
    chunks: list[Chunk] = []
    already_done_ids: set[str] = set()
    if output_path.exists():
        with output_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            existing = json.load(f)
        chunks = [Chunk(**item) for item in existing]
        already_done_ids = {c.id for c in chunks}
        print(f"Resuming: {len(chunks)} chunks already OCR'd, skipping those.")

    reader = Reader(cfg)

    for index, region in enumerate(regions):
        doc_id = region.page_id.rsplit("_p", 1)[0]
        chunk_id = f"{region.page_id}_region_{index:04d}"

        if chunk_id in already_done_ids:
            continue  # already OCR'd in a previous (interrupted) run

        try:
            text = reader.transcribe_region(region)
        except Exception as exc:
            print(f"WARNING: OCR failed for region {index}/{len(regions)} ({chunk_id}): {exc}")
            continue

        if not text:
            continue

        chunks.append(Chunk(
            id=chunk_id, doc_id=doc_id, text=text,
            page_ids=[region.page_id], score=0.0,
        ))

        if (index + 1) % checkpoint_every == 0 or (index + 1) == len(regions):
            with output_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
                json.dump([c.model_dump() for c in chunks], f, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)
            print(f"[{index + 1}/{len(regions)}] checkpoint saved -- {len(chunks)} chunks total")

    return chunks
```

Add `import json` at the top alongside your existing imports.

**Two things worth deciding now:**
1. **`checkpoint_every`** — I made it configurable via `cfg["ocr"].get("checkpoint_every", 25)` rather than hardcoded, so you can tune it from `config.yaml` without touching code (matches the "configs are the only place you configure the project" principle from earlier).
2. **Resume-by-`chunk_id`, not by index count** — this only works correctly if `regions` (the list `layout.detect()` produces) comes back in the *same order* across reruns. Since your layout model runs deterministically in `eval()` mode with no sampling, that should hold — but if you ever add randomness upstream (data augmentation, etc.), resume could silently misalign. Worth keeping in mind, not an issue today.

**On local CUDA/storage**: don't fight it — keep developing/editing `.py` files locally (that part's fine, no GPU needed to write code), but always run the actual OCR on Kaggle, exactly like you're doing. This checkpoint file gives you a clean way to pull partial results back down (`data/interim/ocr/chunks.json`) even if a session cuts out mid-run.

import time
import torch
from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel

# Reuse the existing crop/page-lookup helpers from your real ocr.py --
# these are format-agnostic (just image cropping), no need to duplicate them.
from doc_agent.vision.ocr import _crop_region, _find_page_image

TROCR_MODEL_ID = "microsoft/trocr-base-printed"

print(f"Loading {TROCR_MODEL_ID}...")
trocr_processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained(TROCR_MODEL_ID)
trocr_model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained(TROCR_MODEL_ID).to("cuda").eval()
print("TrOCR loaded.")


def transcribe_region_trocr(region) -> str:
    """Temporary TrOCR path -- NOT part of committed ocr.py. TrOCR is a
    line-level recognizer, so feeding it a full multi-line region (as
    detected by layout.py) is a mismatch -- this is here specifically to
    SEE that mismatch, not as a drop-in replacement for Reader."""
    crop = _crop_region(region)
    pixel_values = trocr_processor(images=crop, return_tensors="pt").pixel_values.to("cuda")
    with torch.inference_mode():
        generated_ids = trocr_model.generate(pixel_values)
    text = trocr_processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0]
    return text.strip()

import time
import torch
from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel
# Reuse the existing crop/page-lookup helpers from your real ocr.py --
# these are format-agnostic (just image cropping), no need to duplicate them.
from doc_agent.vision.ocr import _crop_region, _find_page_image

print(f"Loading {TROCR_MODEL_ID}...")
trocr_processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained(TROCR_MODEL_ID)
trocr_model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained(TROCR_MODEL_ID).to("cuda").eval()
print("TrOCR loaded.")
def transcribe_region_trocr(region) -> str:
    """Temporary TrOCR path -- NOT part of committed ocr.py. TrOCR is a
    line-level recognizer, so feeding it a full multi-line region (as
    detected by layout.py) is a mismatch -- this is here specifically to
    SEE that mismatch, not as a drop-in replacement for Reader."""
    crop = _crop_region(region)
    pixel_values = trocr_processor(images=crop, return_tensors="pt").pixel_values.to("cuda")
    with torch.inference_mode():
        generated_ids = trocr_model.generate(pixel_values)
    text = trocr_processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0]

why is the output of all the texts in capital 

AI: That's not a bug in your code — it's a known property of `microsoft/trocr-base-printed` specifically.

**Why:** TrOCR's printed-text checkpoints were fine-tuned on the SROIE dataset (scanned receipts), and SROIE's ground-truth annotations are **all uppercase**. So the model learned to output uppercase regardless of the actual case in the source image — this is a training-data artifact, not something wrong with your crop/inference code.

**Fix options:**

1. **Switch to a different TrOCR checkpoint** trained on mixed-case text:
   ```python
   TROCR_MODEL_ID = "microsoft/trocr-base-str"  # scene-text recognition, mixed case
   # or
   TROCR_MODEL_ID = "microsoft/trocr-large-printed"  # still uppercase-biased, larger
   ```
   `trocr-base-str` is the more reliable mixed-case option, though it's tuned more for scene/street text than document scans — worth testing against your held-out labels either way.

2. **If you're just checking general OCR accuracy/speed for now** (not final text casing), you can ignore this for the moment — it doesn't affect character-level correctness in a way that would invalidate a quick speed/quality comparison, just cosmetically ugly output.

3. **If casing matters for your actual F1 metric** — worth checking how `eval/metrics.py`'s F1 calculation handles case: if it's case-sensitive, TrOCR's uppercase output will tank its score artificially, making the comparison against Qwen2-VL/olmOCR unfair (they'll naturally preserve original casing). Either lowercase both sides before comparing, or make the F1 computation case-insensitive, so you're comparing genuine transcription accuracy rather than a case-convention mismatch.

Given you're already leaning toward VLM-based OCR (Qwen2-VL/olmOCR) based on the earlier multi-column/table evidence, and TrOCR's line-level mismatch issue we're about to see in the visualization — this uppercase quirk is one more reason TrOCR is looking like the weaker fit for BYTE specifically, though worth still finishing the comparison for a documented, evidence-based `design_choices.md` entry rather than dropping it based on this alone.

Me: what are some other fast options to explore? 
what about donut or tessarect?

AI: **Donut (Document Understanding Transformer):**
- OCR-free — it doesn't do character-level recognition at all; it's an encoder-decoder that reads pixels directly and outputs *structured* extraction (JSON-like fields), not free-form transcription.
- This means it's designed for extraction tasks it's been fine-tuned for (receipts → totals/dates, invoices → line items), not "transcribe everything on this page." Using it for open-ended full-page transcription would likely need fine-tuning on BYTE-style output first — not a drop-in "fast alternative" the way TrOCR/Tesseract are.
- Fast at inference (single forward pass, no separate detection+recognition), but the mismatch with your actual task (full transcription, not field extraction) makes it a poor fit without extra work you don't have time for this week.

**Tesseract:**
- Genuinely fast, runs on CPU (no GPU needed at all — real advantage given your Kaggle GPU-hour constraints).
- Same fundamental limitation as TrOCR: no built-in reading-order intelligence for multi-column layouts. It has page-segmentation modes (`--psm`) that can help, but on real magazine layouts with sidebars/code boxes/ads breaking columns, it needs the same explicit layout-detection-first pipeline you're already testing for Qwen2-VL-2B — it doesn't solve the reading-order problem on its own.
- Weak on historical/degraded fonts and unusual typefaces relative to modern deep models — 1970s-80s print fonts plus scan artifacts is exactly the harder end of what it handles.
- Worth including in your benchmark specifically *because* it's free/fast/CPU-only — if it scores acceptably on your held-out F1 even with its weaknesses, it's a legitimate low-cost fallback option worth documenting, even if you don't end up using it as primary.

**A couple more genuinely fast options worth adding to your comparison, given your multi-column/table requirement:**

- **PaddleOCR (PP-OCR family)** — purpose-built for exactly this: detection + recognition + **layout analysis** in one lightweight pipeline, actively used in the pipeline-tool category from our earlier research (competitive with much larger VLMs on multi-column benchmarks). Runs fast, has reasonable multilingual/table support out of the box. Worth testing — this is architecturally closest to the "layout detection + small recognizer" pattern that performed well earlier.
- **Florence-2** (Microsoft, ~230M-770M params) — a genuinely tiny VLM with real OCR capability built in, much smaller than even Qwen2-VL-2B, worth a quick test given your speed constraints — but like Donut, it's more of a general vision-task model, so verify its OCR-specific mode handles dense multi-column text before betting on it.

**Practical recommendation given your 4-day clock:** don't chase every option — you've already got a solid 3-4 way comparison (Qwen2-VL-2B raw, Qwen2-VL-2B+layout, olmOCR-7B, TrOCR). Adding **PaddleOCR** as a 5th is the highest-value addition here since it directly tests the "purpose-built layout+recognition pipeline" hypothesis at a genuinely fast/lightweight tier — Tesseract is worth a quick cheap test too since it costs nothing, but I'd stop there rather than also chasing Donut/Florence-2, which need more adaptation work than your remaining time really allows.

Me: I am already using layout.py to detect the regions so i think a not so fancy ocr thing will do the job now

AI: That reasoning holds — since `layout.py` already solves reading order for you, you no longer need a full-page-aware VLM like olmOCR-7B. A lighter region-level recognizer becomes a much fairer contest now.

**One nuance worth catching before you commit, though:** this logic helps **Tesseract**, but doesn't actually fix **TrOCR**'s problem. TrOCR is still *line*-level, not *region*-level — even with layout already isolating a paragraph/column block, that block still has multiple lines in it, and TrOCR expects one line per call. So TrOCR would still need an extra line-splitting step on top of your region crop; it doesn't become "simple" just because layout ran first.

**Tesseract, by contrast, genuinely benefits from this** — it natively handles multi-line blocks (`--psm 6` = "assume a uniform block of text" is exactly your region shape after layout crops it). So: **Tesseract is your actual "not-so-fancy, fast" candidate now**, not TrOCR.
