If it comes back clean on your test regions, great, fast and free. If it garbles specifically on older issues (1975-1980) versus cleaner later ones (1988+), that's a real, documentable finding for `design_choices.md` — "Tesseract works for later scans, VLM needed for earlier/degraded ones" is a legitimate, evidence-based split decision, not a failure.

Me: """Stage 4 — chunk text"""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
from ..contracts import Chunk

CHUNK_DIR = Path("data/interim/chunks")


def split(chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> list[Chunk]:
    """Re-chunk raw per-region OCR chunks to cfg['index'] size/overlap.
    Input: small, region-sized Chunks from ocr.transcribe() (one per detected
    region — text/table/heading blocks), already in correct multi-column
    reading order from layout.py. This function regroups them, never reorders.
    """
    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})
    target_tokens = index_cfg.get("chunk_tokens", 256)
    overlap_tokens = index_cfg.get("overlap", 32)

    by_doc: dict[str, list[Chunk]] = {}
    for c in chunks:
        by_doc.setdefault(c.doc_id, []).append(c)

    output: list[Chunk] = []

    for doc_id, doc_chunks in by_doc.items():
        buffer_words: list[str] = []
        buffer_page_ids: list[str] = []
        chunk_idx = 0

        def flush():
            nonlocal chunk_idx
            if not buffer_words:
                return
            text = " ".join(buffer_words)
            seen = set()
            ordered_pages = [p for p in buffer_page_ids if not (p in seen or seen.add(p))]
            output.append(Chunk(
                id=f"{doc_id}_c{chunk_idx:04d}",
                doc_id=doc_id,
                text=text,
                page_ids=ordered_pages,
            ))
            chunk_idx += 1

        for c in doc_chunks:
            words = c.text.split()
            for w in words:
                buffer_words.append(w)
                if c.page_ids:
                    buffer_page_ids.extend(c.page_ids)

                if len(buffer_words) >= target_tokens:
                    flush()
                    carry = buffer_words[-overlap_tokens:] if overlap_tokens > 0 else []
                    carry_pages = buffer_page_ids[-1:] if buffer_page_ids else []
                    buffer_words = list(carry)
                    buffer_page_ids = list(carry_pages)

        flush()

    # Checkpoint, matching layout.py/ocr.py's pattern — lets embed.py (or you,
    # re-running this notebook later) reload without recomputing.
    CHUNK_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    out_path = CHUNK_DIR / "chunks.json"
    with out_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump([c.model_dump() for c in output], f, indent=2, ensure_ascii=False)
    print(f"Saved {len(output)} final chunks to {out_path}")

    return output


how do i change the name of the output file according to the input file name? 

AI: Yep. Since `split()` only receives `chunks` and `cfg`, it doesn't directly know the original OCR filename. The cleanest approach is to pass the **issue ID / output name through `cfg`** for now.

For your current setup, if the OCR input is:

```text
data/interim/ocr/byte-magazine-1975-09.txt
```

you want:

```text
data/interim/chunks/byte-magazine-1975-09.json
```

### Change this part of `chunk.py`

Replace:

```python
CHUNK_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
out_path = CHUNK_DIR / "chunks.json"
```

with:

```python
CHUNK_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

output_name = cfg.get("chunk_output_name", "chunks.json")
out_path = CHUNK_DIR / output_name
```

So the bottom becomes:

```python
# Checkpoint
CHUNK_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

output_name = cfg.get(
    "chunk_output_name",
    "chunks.json",
)

out_path = CHUNK_DIR / output_name

with out_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    json.dump(
        [c.model_dump() for c in output],
        f,
        indent=2,
        ensure_ascii=False,
    )

print(
    f"Saved {len(output)} final chunks to {out_path}"
)

return output
```

### Then in your notebook

Set:

```python
cfg = {
    "index": {
        "chunk_tokens": 256,
        "overlap": 32,
    },
    "chunk_output_name": "byte-magazine-1975-09.json",
}
```

Then:

```python
final_chunks = chunk.split(
    ocr_chunks,
    cfg,
)
```

You'll get:

```text
data/interim/chunks/
└── byte-magazine-1975-09.json
```

### Even better: automatically derive it

If you want `chunk.py` to **automatically name the output based on the document being processed**, you don't actually need to put the filename in `cfg`.

Since every `Chunk` already has `doc_id`, you can do:

```python
doc_ids = {c.doc_id for c in chunks}

if len(doc_ids) == 1:
    doc_id = next(iter(doc_ids))
    output_name = f"{doc_id}.json"
else:
    output_name = "chunks.json"

out_path = CHUNK_DIR / output_name
```

For your OCR:

```text
doc_id = byte-magazine-1975-09
```

that produces:

```text
data/interim/chunks/byte-magazine-1975-09.json
```

**I'd use this second approach.** Your pipeline already carries `doc_id` in the `Chunk` contract, so you don't need to introduce another config field just to name the checkpoint.

Me: Whenever i run this code on notebook the output is saved by creating these folders -- data/interim/chunks inside the notebook folder but i want them to be saved on the data/interim/chunks and the data folder is outside the notebook , what to edit  

AI: Ah yep — this is because `chunk.py` uses a **relative path**:

```python
CHUNK_DIR = Path("data/interim/chunks")
```

Relative paths are resolved from the **current working directory**, which in your notebook is probably:

```text
doc-agent-starter/notebook/
```

So Python creates:

```text
notebook/data/interim/chunks/
```

instead of:

```text
doc-agent-starter/data/interim/chunks/
```

### Best fix

Since your project root is already being determined in the notebook, make `CHUNK_DIR` relative to the project root.

In `chunk.py`, change:

```python
CHUNK_DIR = Path("data/interim/chunks")
```

to:

```python
PROJECT_ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[3]
CHUNK_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "data" / "interim" / "chunks"
```

Given your structure:

```text
doc-agent-starter/
├── data/
│   └── interim/
│       ├── ocr/
│       └── chunks/
├── src/
│   └── doc_agent/
│       └── index/
│           └── chunk.py
└── notebook/
    └── test_chunk.ipynb
```

`__file__` points to:

```text
src/doc_agent/index/chunk.py
```

and `.parents[3]` gets you to:

```text
doc-agent-starter/
```

So the output will correctly go to:

```text
doc-agent-starter/data/interim/chunks/
```

### Even better for your whole project

You should eventually do the same thing for `ocr.py`, `layout.py`, `embed.py`, `store.py`, etc. Otherwise you'll keep getting this annoying **"why did Python create another data folder inside notebook 😭"** problem.

For example:

```python
PROJECT_ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[3]
OCR_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "data" / "interim" / "ocr"
```

and:

```python
PROJECT_ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[3]
CHUNK_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "data" / "interim" / "chunks"
```

That makes the modules independent of whether you run them from:

```text
notebook/
```

or:

```text
project root/
```

or another working directory. Much safer for your actual pipeline.

Me: i dont think that will be the same for all my friends 


i have doc../doc../data 
but they have doc../data

isnt there a generalized way to point to the data directory ? using ".."?

AI: YES — and this is actually the better way to think about it. 😭

But there are **two different directory structures** you're dealing with:

### Yours

```text
doc-agent-template/
└── doc-agent-starter/
    ├── data/
    ├── src/
    └── notebooks/
```

### Your friends'

```text
doc-agent-template/
├── data/
├── src/
└── ...
```

So hardcoding:

```python
Path(__file__).resolve().parents[3]
```

is **not portable**.

And simply doing:

```python
Path("../data")
```

is also not reliable, because `..` is relative to the **current working directory**, not relative to `chunk.py`.

---

## The clean generalized solution

Since your `chunk.py` is inside the Python package, you can **walk upward until you find the `data` directory**.

Use:

```python
from pathlib import Path


def _find_data_dir() -> Path:
    current = Path(__file__).resolve()

    for parent in current.parents:
        data_dir = parent / "data"

        if data_dir.is_dir():
            return data_dir

    raise RuntimeError(
        "Could not find project data directory."
    )


DATA_DIR = _find_data_dir()
CHUNK_DIR = DATA_DIR / "interim" / "chunks"
```

Now look what happens.

### Your structure

```text
doc-agent-template/
└── doc-agent-starter/
    ├── data/
    └── src/doc_agent/index/chunk.py
```

The function walks upward:

```text
chunk.py
   ↓
index/
   ↓
doc_agent/
   ↓
src/
   ↓
doc-agent-starter/
       └── data/  ← FOUND
```

So:

```python
CHUNK_DIR
```

becomes:

```text
doc-agent-starter/data/interim/chunks
```

### Your friend's structure

```text
doc-agent-template/
├── data/
└── doc-agent-starter/
    └── src/doc_agent/index/chunk.py
```

It walks upward and finds:

```text
doc-agent-template/data/
```

So it becomes:

```text
doc-agent-template/data/interim/chunks
```

**Same code. No hardcoded number of `..`.**

---

### And importantly

Don't do:

```python
Path("../data")
```

inside `chunk.py`.

Because if you run your notebook from:

```text
doc-agent-starter/notebooks/
```

then `../data` works.

But if someone runs:

```bash
cd doc-agent-starter
python ...
```

then `../data` points somewhere completely different.

Your modules should ideally resolve project resources **relative to the module/project**, not relative to wherever the user happens to launch Python.

So I'd use the `find_data_dir()` approach for your project. It's much more portable across you and your teammates.

Me: why are the changes i made in chunk.py not being reflected in the notebook ?

AI: Because **Python imports a module once and caches it**. 😭

So if your notebook did:

```python
from doc_agent.index import chunk
```

Python loads `chunk.py` into memory. If you then edit the file on disk, the notebook's `chunk` object **doesn't automatically update**.

### Do this after every edit to `chunk.py`

```python
import importlib
from doc_agent.index import chunk

importlib.reload(chunk)
```

Then verify:

```python
print(chunk.__file__)
print(chunk.CHUNK_DIR)
```

You should see the **new** value.

---

### Even safer while you're developing

Use:

```python
import importlib
import doc_agent.index.chunk as chunk

importlib.reload(chunk)
```

Then call:

```python
final_chunks = chunk.split(
    ocr_chunks,
    cfg,
)
```

---

### One important gotcha

If you did this earlier:

```python
from doc_agent.index.chunk import split
```

then **reloading `chunk` does NOT update your `split` variable**.

You'd need to reload and re-import:

```python
import importlib
import doc_agent.index.chunk as chunk

importlib.reload(chunk)

split = chunk.split
```

But honestly, during development, I'd avoid importing individual functions. Do:

```python
from doc_agent.index import chunk
import importlib

importlib.reload(chunk)

chunk.split(...)
```

That way you're always explicitly using the reloaded module.

### Also check you're editing the same file you're importing

Run:

```python
from doc_agent.index import chunk

print(chunk.__file__)
```

Earlier yours showed:

```text
/home/anisa/Downloads/doc-agent-template/doc-agent-starter/src/doc_agent/index/chunk.py
```

So **that's the exact `chunk.py` you need to edit**. If you're editing another copy of `chunk.py` somewhere else, the notebook won't see those changes no matter how many times you reload.

"""Stage 4 — vector store"""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np
import faiss
from ..contracts import Chunk

INDEX_DIR = Path("data/index")


def build(chunks: list[Chunk], vectors: np.ndarray, cfg: dict) -> None:
    """Persist a vector index (cfg['index']['type'])."""
    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})
    dim = vectors.shape[1]

    # HNSW ("Hierarchical Navigable Small World") — an approximate index.
    # M = how many links each vector keeps to its neighbors in the graph.
    # Bigger M = more accurate search, slower to build, bigger file on disk.
    m = index_cfg.get("hnsw_m", 32)

    index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)

    # efConstruction: how hard the graph tries to find good neighbors while
    # BUILDING the index. Higher = better quality index, slower one-time build.
    index.hnsw.efConstruction = index_cfg.get("ef_construction", 200)

    # efSearch: how hard it searches at QUERY time. This is your main lever
    # for the scalability NFR — higher efSearch = more accurate but slower
    # per query; lower = faster but risks missing the true best match.
    # Tune this against your P95 <= 1.5s target directly.
    index.hnsw.efSearch = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

    vectors = np.ascontiguousarray(vectors.astype("float32"))
    index.add(vectors)

    INDEX_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    faiss.write_index(index, str(INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"))

    # FAISS only stores vectors + an internal integer id (0, 1, 2...) — it
    # knows nothing about your Chunk objects. We save this side-table so
    # retriever.py can map "FAISS returned internal id 47" back to a real
    # Chunk (its text, doc_id, page_ids) for citations.
    metadata = [c.model_dump() for c in chunks]
    with open(INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json", "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(metadata, f, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)

    # These are exactly the numbers your A2 Section 4 report needs.
    size_mb = (INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss").stat().st_size / (1024 ** 2)
    print(f"✅ Built HNSW index: {len(chunks)} chunks, dim={dim}, "
          f"M={m}, size={size_mb:.2f} MB → {INDEX_DIR / 'index.faiss'}")


def load(cfg: dict):
    """Load the persisted index + its chunk metadata."""
    index_path = INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"
    meta_path = INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json"

    if not index_path.exists():
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"No index found at {index_path} — run build() first.")

    index = faiss.read_index(str(index_path))

    # efSearch doesn't get saved with the index — reset it here in case
    # something changed in cfg since build() ran.
    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})
    index.hnsw.efSearch = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

    with open(meta_path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        chunk_dicts = json.load(f)

    chunks = [Chunk(**d) for d in chunk_dicts]

    print(f"Loaded index: {index.ntotal} vectors, dim={index.d}")
    return index, chunks

Me: """FIXED end-to-end order (Stages 0-9) + cross-cutting seams.Do not reorder stages or remove hooks.run()/register_all() calls."""from future import annotationsfrom . import config, hooks, wiring  # noqa: F401from .ingest import loader, preprocess, enhancefrom .vision import layout, ocrfrom .index import chunk, embed, storefrom .retrieval import retrieverfrom .agent import agent

def build_knowledge_base(cfg: dict) -> None:wiring.register_all(cfg)                        # wire cross-cutting featurespages = loader.load_pages(cfg)pages = preprocess.run(pages, cfg)pages = enhance.run(pages, cfg)                 # Stage 1 - enhancement (VAE/diffusion)hooks.run(hooks.AFTER_INGEST, {"pages": pages})regions = layout.detect(pages, cfg)             # Stage 2text = ocr.transcribe(regions, cfg)             # Stage 3hooks.run(hooks.AFTER_OCR, {"chunks": text})    # e.g. PII redaction on extracted textchunks = chunk.split(text, cfg)                 # Stage 4hooks.run(hooks.BEFORE_INDEX, {"chunks": chunks})vectors = embed.encode(chunks, cfg)store.build(chunks, vectors, cfg)

def answer(query_text: str, cfg: dict):wiring.register_all(cfg)r = retriever.Retriever(cfg)                    # Stage 5return agent.Agent(cfg, r).run(query_text)      # Stage 6 (seams run inside the loop)

the chunk.py file is --- 

"""Stage 4 — chunk text"""from future import annotationsimport jsonfrom pathlib import Pathfrom ..contracts import Chunk

from pathlib import Path

from pathlib import Path

def _find_project_root() -> Path:current = Path(file).resolve()

for parent in current.parents:
    if (
        (parent / "src").is_dir()
        and (parent / "data").is_dir()
    ):
        return parent

raise RuntimeError(
    "Could not find project root containing both 'src' and 'data'."
)

PROJECT_ROOT = _find_project_root()CHUNK_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "data" / "interim" / "chunks"

def split(chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> list[Chunk]:"""Re-chunk raw per-region OCR chunks to cfg['index'] size/overlap.Input: small, region-sized Chunks from ocr.transcribe() (one per detectedregion — text/table/heading blocks), already in correct multi-columnreading order from layout.py. This function regroups them, never reorders."""index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})target_tokens = index_cfg.get("chunk_tokens", 256)overlap_tokens = index_cfg.get("overlap", 32)

by_doc: dict[str, list[Chunk]] = {}
for c in chunks:
    by_doc.setdefault(c.doc_id, []).append(c)

output: list[Chunk] = []

for doc_id, doc_chunks in by_doc.items():
    buffer_words: list[str] = []
    buffer_page_ids: list[str] = []
    chunk_idx = 0

    def flush():
        nonlocal chunk_idx
        if not buffer_words:
            return
        text = " ".join(buffer_words)
        seen = set()
        ordered_pages = [p for p in buffer_page_ids if not (p in seen or seen.add(p))]
        output.append(Chunk(
            id=f"{doc_id}_c{chunk_idx:04d}",
            doc_id=doc_id,
            text=text,
            page_ids=ordered_pages,
        ))
        chunk_idx += 1

    for c in doc_chunks:
        words = c.text.split()
        for w in words:
            buffer_words.append(w)
            if c.page_ids:
                buffer_page_ids.extend(c.page_ids)

            if len(buffer_words) >= target_tokens:
                flush()
                carry = buffer_words[-overlap_tokens:] if overlap_tokens > 0 else []
                carry_pages = buffer_page_ids[-1:] if buffer_page_ids else []
                buffer_words = list(carry)
                buffer_page_ids = list(carry_pages)

    flush()

# Checkpoint, matching layout.py/ocr.py's pattern — lets embed.py (or you,
# re-running this notebook later) reload without recomputing.
CHUNK_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
doc_ids = {c.doc_id for c in chunks}

if len(doc_ids) == 1:
    doc_id = next(iter(doc_ids))
    output_name = f"{doc_id}.json"
else:
    output_name = "chks.json"

out_path = CHUNK_DIR / output_name
with out_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    json.dump([c.model_dump() for c in output], f, indent=2, ensure_ascii=False)
print(f"Saved {len(output)} final chunks to {out_path}")

return output



and then we got the hooks.py --- 

"""FIXED — cross-cutting seam. Horizontal features register handlers here; the pipeline and agentcall run() at fixed seams. Do NOT add/remove seams or the hooks.run() calls that use them."""from future import annotationsfrom collections import defaultdictfrom typing import Callable

The only points where cross-cutting code runs.

AFTER_INGEST = "after_ingest"AFTER_OCR = "after_ocr"BEFORE_INDEX = "before_index"AFTER_RETRIEVE = "after_retrieve"ON_STEP = "on_step"ON_TOOL_CALL = "on_tool_call"BEFORE_ANSWER = "before_answer"AFTER_ANSWER = "after_answer"ON_LOG = "on_log"SEAMS = [AFTER_INGEST, AFTER_OCR, BEFORE_INDEX, AFTER_RETRIEVE, ON_STEP,ON_TOOL_CALL, BEFORE_ANSWER, AFTER_ANSWER, ON_LOG]

_handlers: dict[str, list[Callable]] = defaultdict(list)

def register(seam: str, handler: Callable) -> None:"""Attach a handler to a seam. Called by each feature's register() via wiring.py."""assert seam in SEAMS, f"unknown seam {seam}"_handlers[seam].append(handler)

def run(seam: str, ctx: dict) -> dict:"""Run every handler registered at seam, threading ctx through. Called at fixed points only."""for h in _handlers[seam]:ctx = h(ctx) or ctxreturn ctx

def clear() -> None:_handlers.clear()





and then the embed.py 

"""Stage 4 — embed chunks"""from future import annotationsimport jsonfrom pathlib import Pathimport numpy as npfrom ..contracts import Chunk

EMBED_DIR = Path("data/interim/embed")_model = None

"""Stage 4 — vector store"""from future import annotationsimport jsonfrom pathlib import Pathimport numpy as npimport faissfrom ..contracts import Chunk

INDEX_DIR = Path("data/index")

def build(chunks: list[Chunk], vectors: np.ndarray, cfg: dict) -> None:"""Persist a vector index (cfg['index']['type'])."""index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})dim = vectors.shape[1]

# HNSW ("Hierarchical Navigable Small World") — an approximate index.
# M = how many links each vector keeps to its neighbors in the graph.
# Bigger M = more accurate search, slower to build, bigger file on disk.
m = index_cfg.get("hnsw_m", 32)

index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)

# efConstruction: how hard the graph tries to find good neighbors while
# BUILDING the index. Higher = better quality index, slower one-time build.
index.hnsw.efConstruction = index_cfg.get("ef_construction", 200)

# efSearch: how hard it searches at QUERY time. This is your main lever
# for the scalability NFR — higher efSearch = more accurate but slower
# per query; lower = faster but risks missing the true best match.
# Tune this against your P95 <= 1.5s target directly.
index.hnsw.efSearch = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

vectors = np.ascontiguousarray(vectors.astype("float32"))
index.add(vectors)

INDEX_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
faiss.write_index(index, str(INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"))

# FAISS only stores vectors + an internal integer id (0, 1, 2...) — it
# knows nothing about your Chunk objects. We save this side-table so
# retriever.py can map "FAISS returned internal id 47" back to a real
# Chunk (its text, doc_id, page_ids) for citations.
metadata = [c.model_dump() for c in chunks]
with open(INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json", "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    json.dump(metadata, f, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)

# These are exactly the numbers your A2 Section 4 report needs.
size_mb = (INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss").stat().st_size / (1024 ** 2)
print(f"✅ Built HNSW index: {len(chunks)} chunks, dim={dim}, "
      f"M={m}, size={size_mb:.2f} MB → {INDEX_DIR / 'index.faiss'}")

    raise FileNotFoundError(f"No index found at {index_path} — run build() first.")

# efSearch doesn't get saved with the index — reset it here in case
# something changed in cfg since build() ran.

now i have the following questions --- 

since the size of our corpus is huge and we are not done ocr ing all the issues, can you pls tell me whether the store.py creates the vectored db in an incremental way ?

what i understood is that the vectored db will be stored in the data/index which wont be furthered committed to the git repo so even then does this support the incremental addition? 

AI: Yep — and there’s an important distinction here:

### 1. Does your current `store.py` support incremental addition?

**No.** Not as currently written.

This part:

```python
index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)
...
index.add(vectors)
...
faiss.write_index(index, str(INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"))
```

**creates a brand-new FAISS index every time `store.build()` is called.**

So imagine you have:

```text
Issue A → 10,000 chunks → embeddings
Issue B → 12,000 chunks → embeddings
Issue C → 8,000 chunks → embeddings
```

If you run:

```python
store.build(chunks_A, vectors_A, cfg)
```

you get:

```text
index.faiss = A
```

Then later:

```python
store.build(chunks_B, vectors_B, cfg)
```

you **do not get A + B**.

You get:

```text
index.faiss = B
```

because this line creates a fresh index:

```python
index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)
```

and this:

```python
index.add(vectors)
```

only adds the vectors from **this particular call**.

---

## 2. Does not committing `data/index` to Git prevent incremental addition?

**No. Those are two completely separate things.**

Git has nothing to do with whether FAISS can incrementally add vectors.

You can have:

```text
Git repository
│
├── src/
│   └── doc_agent/
│       └── index/
│           └── store.py       ← committed
│
└── data/
    └── index/
        ├── index.faiss       ← NOT committed
        └── chunks_meta.json  ← NOT committed
```

The FAISS files can live on your local machine/Kaggle/server without being tracked by Git.

The important thing is that **the existing `index.faiss` must be loaded before adding new vectors.**

For example, conceptually:

```python
index = faiss.read_index("data/index/index.faiss")

index.add(new_vectors)

faiss.write_index(index, "data/index/index.faiss")
```

That **would** be incremental.

---

# And this matters A LOT for your project

Because you're saying:

> We have a huge corpus and we're not done OCR-ing all the issues.

You **should not need to wait until all OCR is finished** to create your final vector DB.

Your intended workflow can be:

```text
BYTE Issue 1
   ↓
OCR
   ↓
chunks
   ↓
embeddings
   ↓
add to FAISS
   ↓
SAVE index

BYTE Issue 2
   ↓
OCR
   ↓
chunks
   ↓
embeddings
   ↓
LOAD existing FAISS
   ↓
ADD new vectors
   ↓
SAVE index

BYTE Issue 3
   ↓
...
```

So the index gradually becomes:

```text
After Issue 1:
[A]

After Issue 2:
[A B]

After Issue 3:
[A B C]

...
```

### Your current `store.py`, however, does this:

```text
Issue 1 → CREATE index → SAVE [A]

Issue 2 → CREATE NEW index → SAVE [B]

Issue 3 → CREATE NEW index → SAVE [C]
```

So **you need to modify `store.py` if incremental indexing is required.**

---

## One more issue: `embed.py`

Your current `embed.py` also does:

```python
np.save(EMBED_DIR / "vectors.npy", vectors)
```

and

```python
json.dump([c.id for c in chunks], f)
```

So it **overwrites**:

```text
vectors.npy
chunk_ids.json
```

every time you run it.

That's not necessarily a problem if you're processing one issue at a time, because you can immediately send those vectors to FAISS.

You don't actually need to maintain one giant `vectors.npy`.

A much cleaner large-corpus workflow is:

```text
OCR outputs
     ↓
Issue A chunks
     ↓
Issue A embeddings
     ↓
ADD to FAISS
     ↓
discard Issue A embedding array

Issue B chunks
     ↓
Issue B embeddings
     ↓
ADD to FAISS
     ↓
discard Issue B embedding array
```

That is **much more memory-friendly**.

---

# What I would recommend for your project

Change `store.build()` so that it behaves like:

```python
if index.faiss exists:
    load existing index
else:
    create new index

index.add(new_vectors)

save index
```

And similarly update the metadata:

```python
existing chunks
        +
new chunks
        ↓
chunks_meta.json
```

Then you can safely process the corpus issue-by-issue.

### Result

You could have:

```text
data/
├── interim/
│   ├── ocr/
│   │   ├── issue1.txt
│   │   ├── issue2.txt
│   │   └── ...
│   ├── chunks/
│   │   ├── issue1.json
│   │   ├── issue2.json
│   │   └── ...
│   └── embed/
│       └── ...
│
└── index/
    ├── index.faiss
    └── chunks_meta.json
```

while Git only contains the **code/config**, not those generated artifacts.

**So your understanding is correct about `data/index` not needing to be committed — but your current `store.py` itself is NOT incremental yet.** That's the thing I'd fix before you start feeding all those remaining BYTE issues into it.

AI: Yep. You only need to replace the **body of `build()`**. `load()` can stay as it is.

Replace everything inside `build()` with this:

```python
def build(chunks: list[Chunk], vectors: np.ndarray, cfg: dict) -> None:
    """Incrementally add new vectors/chunks to the existing HNSW index.

    The corpus is too large to process all at once, and OCR is being completed
    issue-by-issue. Therefore, we reuse the existing FAISS index when it exists
    and add only the newly generated vectors.

    This avoids rebuilding the entire vector database every time a new issue
    is processed.
    """
    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})
    
    INDEX_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    index_path = INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"
    meta_path = INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json"

    vectors = np.ascontiguousarray(vectors.astype("float32"))
    dim = vectors.shape[1]

    m = index_cfg.get("hnsw_m", 32)
    ef_construction = index_cfg.get("ef_construction", 200)
    ef_search = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 1. Load the existing index OR create a new one
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # The project processes a very large corpus incrementally.
    # If an index already exists, we MUST load it instead of
    # creating a new IndexHNSWFlat, otherwise previously indexed
    # issues would be lost.
    #
    # First issue:
    #     no index → create new HNSW index
    #
    # Later issues:
    #     existing index → load it and append new vectors
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    if index_path.exists():
        print(f"Loading existing FAISS index from {index_path}")

        index = faiss.read_index(str(index_path))

        if index.d != dim:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Embedding dimension mismatch: "
                f"existing index has dim={index.d}, "
                f"but new vectors have dim={dim}."
            )

        print(
            f"Existing index contains {index.ntotal} vectors. "
            f"Adding {len(vectors)} new vectors."
        )

    else:
        print("No existing FAISS index found. Creating a new HNSW index.")

        index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)

        # efConstruction controls how thoroughly HNSW searches for
        # good neighbors while constructing the graph.
        index.hnsw.efConstruction = ef_construction

    # efSearch controls the search effort at query time.
    # It is reset here because this value is a runtime setting and
    # may not be preserved exactly when the index is saved/reloaded.
    index.hnsw.efSearch = ef_search

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 2. Add ONLY the new vectors
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # FAISS supports adding vectors to an existing HNSW index.
    # Therefore, we do not need to recompute embeddings for
    # previously processed issues.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    index.add(vectors)

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 3. Save the updated FAISS index
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # The index now contains:
    #
    #     previous vectors + newly added vectors
    #
    # Saving it means the next issue can load this updated index
    # and continue from where we stopped.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    faiss.write_index(index, str(index_path))

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 4. Update chunk metadata
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # FAISS only stores vectors and their internal integer IDs.
    # It does NOT know the actual Chunk objects.
    #
    # Therefore chunks_meta.json must contain metadata for BOTH:
    #     - previously indexed chunks
    #     - newly added chunks
    #
    # The ordering must match FAISS's vector IDs.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    if meta_path.exists():
        with meta_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            existing_metadata = json.load(f)
    else:
        existing_metadata = []

    existing_metadata.extend(
        c.model_dump() for c in chunks
    )

    with meta_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(
            existing_metadata,
            f,
            ensure_ascii=False,
            indent=2,
        )

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 5. Report the updated index size
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    size_mb = index_path.stat().st_size / (1024 ** 2)

    print(
        f"✅ Added {len(chunks)} chunks to HNSW index. "
        f"Total vectors: {index.ntotal}, "
        f"dim={dim}, M={m}, "
        f"size={size_mb:.2f} MB → {index_path}"
    )
```

### One important thing

This gives you:

```text
Issue 1
  ↓
embed
  ↓
store.build()
  ↓
FAISS = Issue 1

Issue 2
  ↓
embed
  ↓
store.build()
  ↓
FAISS = Issue 1 + Issue 2

Issue 3
  ↓
embed
  ↓
store.build()
  ↓
FAISS = Issue 1 + Issue 2 + Issue 3
```

And `chunks_meta.json` grows correspondingly.

**One caveat:** this assumes you never accidentally feed the *same issue/chunks* to `build()` twice. If you do, it will add duplicate vectors. For your issue-by-issue workflow, that's manageable, but we can add duplicate-issue protection later if needed.

Me: what measures can be taken to prevent the issue of accidentally feeding the same issue twice?

AI: Yep — and given your corpus is being processed **issue-by-issue**, I'd definitely add protection. Otherwise one accidental notebook rerun could silently duplicate an entire issue in the FAISS index 😭.

There are a few levels of protection.

### Best option for your project: track `doc_id`s already indexed

Your `Chunk` already has:

```python
c.doc_id
```

So before adding anything, inspect `chunks_meta.json` and determine which `doc_id`s are already present.

For example, before `index.add(vectors)`:

```python
# Check whether any of these documents have already been indexed.
new_doc_ids = {c.doc_id for c in chunks}

if meta_path.exists():
    with meta_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        existing_metadata = json.load(f)

    existing_doc_ids = {
        item["doc_id"]
        for item in existing_metadata
    }

    duplicate_doc_ids = new_doc_ids & existing_doc_ids

    if duplicate_doc_ids:
        raise ValueError(
            "These documents have already been indexed: "
            f"{sorted(duplicate_doc_ids)}. "
            "Refusing to add them again."
        )
else:
    existing_metadata = []
```

Then only proceed to:

```python
index.add(vectors)
```

if there are no duplicates.

---

### Why I prefer **raising an error** instead of silently skipping

Suppose you accidentally run:

```text
1975_09 → already indexed
```

You *could* make the code say:

> "Already exists, skipping."

But that's potentially dangerous because you might have intentionally regenerated the OCR/chunks for that issue and actually want to replace the old version.

Instead:

```text
❌ Duplicate document detected.
Refusing to modify vector DB.
```

is safer.

Then **you decide** what to do.

---

### Even better: track issues separately

You could have:

```text
data/index/
├── index.faiss
├── chunks_meta.json
└── indexed_docs.json
```

where:

```json
[
  "1975_09_BYTE_00-01_The_Worlds_Greatest_Toy",
  "1985_11_BYTE_10-11_Inside_the_IBM_PCs"
]
```

Then checking whether an issue has already been processed becomes extremely straightforward.

However, you **don't actually need this extra file** because `chunks_meta.json` already contains `doc_id`.

So I'd avoid unnecessary bookkeeping for now.

---

### One more protection I'd add

Check that the number of vectors being added equals the number of chunks:

```python
if len(chunks) != len(vectors):
    raise ValueError(
        f"Chunk/vector mismatch: "
        f"{len(chunks)} chunks but {len(vectors)} vectors."
    )
```

This is important because FAISS assigns IDs based on vector order:

```text
FAISS ID 0 → chunks_meta[0]
FAISS ID 1 → chunks_meta[1]
FAISS ID 2 → chunks_meta[2]
...
```

If those ever get out of sync, retrieval citations can become **wrong** even though the system appears to work.

---

### So I'd use these 3 safeguards

```text
1. len(chunks) == len(vectors)
          ↓
2. doc_id hasn't already been indexed
          ↓
3. only then index.add(vectors)
```

That gives you a pretty robust issue-by-issue pipeline:

```text
OCR issue
   ↓
chunk
   ↓
embed
   ↓
check chunk/vector count
   ↓
check doc_id isn't already indexed
   ↓
LOAD existing FAISS
   ↓
ADD vectors
   ↓
append metadata
   ↓
SAVE FAISS + metadata
```

For your situation, **`doc_id`-based duplicate protection is the most sensible approach**. You already have the identifier in your `Chunk`, so there's no need to invent another tracking system.

Me: could you pls give the build function's code when it also deals with the accidental duplicate issue input based on the previous response?

AI: Absolutely. I’d make the `build()` **fail safely** if an already-indexed `doc_id` is passed again, rather than silently skipping it. I’d also include the chunk/vector count check.

Replace your current `build()` with this:

```python
def build(chunks: list[Chunk], vectors: np.ndarray, cfg: dict) -> None:
    """Incrementally add new vectors to the existing HNSW index.

    New issues are appended to the existing FAISS index instead of rebuilding
    the entire vector database.

    Safety checks:
    1. Number of chunks must equal number of vectors.
    2. A document/issue (doc_id) must not already exist in the index.
       This prevents accidental duplicate indexing if the same issue is
       processed twice.
    """

    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})

    INDEX_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    index_path = INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"
    meta_path = INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json"

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 1. Basic chunk/vector consistency check
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # FAISS assigns vector IDs based on their position:
    #
    #   FAISS ID 0 -> chunks_meta[0]
    #   FAISS ID 1 -> chunks_meta[1]
    #   ...
    #
    # Therefore these two lists MUST have the same length.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    if len(chunks) != len(vectors):
        raise ValueError(
            f"Chunk/vector mismatch: "
            f"{len(chunks)} chunks but {len(vectors)} vectors."
        )

    if len(chunks) == 0:
        print("No chunks supplied. Nothing to add.")
        return

    vectors = np.ascontiguousarray(vectors.astype("float32"))
    dim = vectors.shape[1]

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 2. Load existing metadata and detect duplicate issues
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # chunks_meta.json contains the metadata for every vector
    # currently stored in FAISS.
    #
    # We use doc_id to determine whether an entire issue has
    # already been indexed.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    if meta_path.exists():
        with meta_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            existing_metadata = json.load(f)
    else:
        existing_metadata = []

    existing_doc_ids = {
        item["doc_id"]
        for item in existing_metadata
    }

    new_doc_ids = {
        c.doc_id
        for c in chunks
    }

    duplicate_doc_ids = new_doc_ids & existing_doc_ids

    if duplicate_doc_ids:
        raise ValueError(
            "\n❌ Duplicate issue detected!\n"
            f"The following doc_id(s) are already indexed:\n"
            + "\n".join(
                f"  - {doc_id}"
                for doc_id in sorted(duplicate_doc_ids)
            )
            + "\n\nRefusing to modify the FAISS index."
            "\nThis prevents accidental duplicate indexing."
        )

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 3. Load existing FAISS index OR create a new one
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    m = index_cfg.get("hnsw_m", 32)
    ef_construction = index_cfg.get("ef_construction", 200)
    ef_search = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

    if index_path.exists():

        print(f"Loading existing FAISS index from {index_path}")

        index = faiss.read_index(str(index_path))

        # The embedding model must produce the same dimensionality
        # as the vectors already stored in the index.
        if index.d != dim:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Embedding dimension mismatch: "
                f"existing index has dim={index.d}, "
                f"but new vectors have dim={dim}."
            )

        print(
            f"Existing index contains {index.ntotal} vectors."
        )
        print(
            f"Adding {len(vectors)} vectors from "
            f"{len(new_doc_ids)} new issue(s)."
        )

    else:

        print(
            "No existing FAISS index found. "
            "Creating a new HNSW index."
        )

        index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)

        # Controls how thoroughly HNSW searches for good neighbors
        # while constructing the graph.
        index.hnsw.efConstruction = ef_construction

    # efSearch controls search effort at query time.
    index.hnsw.efSearch = ef_search

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 4. Add ONLY the new vectors
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # Existing vectors remain untouched.
    #
    # Example:
    #
    # First run:
    #   FAISS = Issue A
    #
    # Second run:
    #   FAISS = Issue A + Issue B
    #
    # Third run:
    #   FAISS = Issue A + Issue B + Issue C
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    index.add(vectors)

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 5. Save the updated FAISS index
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    faiss.write_index(index, str(index_path))

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 6. Append metadata for the newly indexed chunks
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    #
    # The order here MUST match the order in which vectors were
    # added to FAISS.
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    existing_metadata.extend(
        c.model_dump()
        for c in chunks
    )

    with meta_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(
            existing_metadata,
            f,
            ensure_ascii=False,
            indent=2,
        )

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 7. Report final index status
    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    size_mb = index_path.stat().st_size / (1024 ** 2)

    print(
        f"\n✅ Successfully updated HNSW index."
        f"\n   Added chunks : {len(chunks)}"
        f"\n   Total vectors: {index.ntotal}"
        f"\n   Dimensions   : {dim}"
        f"\n   HNSW M       : {m}"
        f"\n   Index size   : {size_mb:.2f} MB"
        f"\n   Index path   : {index_path}"
    )
```

### One important behavior

If you accidentally run the same issue twice:

```text
1975_09 → first run
             ↓
       267 chunks added
             ↓
       FAISS index saved
```

Then accidentally:

```text
1975_09 → second run
             ↓
       duplicate doc_id detected
             ↓
       ❌ ValueError
             ↓
       FAISS is NOT modified
```

That's exactly what you want. **Don't silently skip it**, because if you later intentionally re-OCR an issue with a better model, you'll want an explicit way to replace/re-index it rather than having the system quietly do nothing.

One small caveat: this treats `doc_id` as the unit of duplication. That's appropriate for your BYTE issues because an issue should be indexed as one document, even though it produces many chunks.

# Part C — Once the test works: full dataset

Now you can switch to the full version.

There is one thing I strongly recommend:

**Process one issue at a time.**

Don't do:

```text
all OCR
   ↓
all chunks
   ↓
all embeddings
   ↓
FAISS
```

because your corpus is huge and you're already hitting memory limitations.

Instead:

```text
OCR issue 1
 ↓
chunks
 ↓
embeddings
 ↓
FAISS ADD
 ↓
OCR issue 2
 ↓
chunks
 ↓
embeddings
 ↓
FAISS ADD
 ↓
...
```

This is much safer.

# Full Cell 4 — Process ALL issues

This is the main cell you can eventually run with **Save Version**.

```python
for issue_number, ocr_file in enumerate(ocr_files, start=1):

    print("\n" + "=" * 100)
    print(
        f"[{issue_number}/{len(ocr_files)}] "
        f"Processing: {ocr_file.name}"
    )
    print("=" * 100)

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 1. Read OCR text
    # ---------------------------------------------------------

    text = ocr_file.read_text(
        encoding="utf-8"
    )

    if not text.strip():
        print("⚠️ Empty OCR file. Skipping.")
        continue

    # The filename identifies the BYTE issue.
    doc_id = ocr_file.stem

    print(
        f"OCR text: "
        f"{len(text):,} characters / "
        f"{len(text.split()):,} words"
    )

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 2. Convert OCR output into the Chunk contract
    # ---------------------------------------------------------

    raw_chunks = [
        Chunk(
            id=f"{doc_id}_ocr",
            doc_id=doc_id,
            text=text,
            page_ids=[],
            score=0.0,
        )
    ]

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 3. Stage 4 — chunk
    # ---------------------------------------------------------

    final_chunks = chunk.split(
        raw_chunks,
        cfg
    )

    print(
        f"Generated {len(final_chunks)} chunks."
    )

    if not final_chunks:
        print("⚠️ No chunks generated. Skipping.")
        continue

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 4. BEFORE_INDEX hook
    # ---------------------------------------------------------

    hook_result = hooks.run(
        hooks.BEFORE_INDEX,
        {"chunks": final_chunks}
    )

    final_chunks = hook_result["chunks"]

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 5. Embed
    # ---------------------------------------------------------

    vectors = embed.encode(
        final_chunks,
        cfg
    )

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 6. Add to vector database
    # ---------------------------------------------------------

    store.build(
        final_chunks,
        vectors,
        cfg
    )

    # ---------------------------------------------------------
    # 7. Free large objects before processing next issue
    # ---------------------------------------------------------

    del text
    del raw_chunks
    del final_chunks
    del vectors

    print(
        f"✅ Finished issue {doc_id}"
    )
```

## ⚠️ One thing I want you to fix before running the full version

Your current `embed.py` has:

```python
EMBED_DIR = Path("data/interim/embed")
```

and `store.py` has:

```python
INDEX_DIR = Path("data/index")
```

These are **relative paths**.

Since you're running from:

```text
/kaggle/working/doc-agent-G21
```

they'll normally resolve to:

```text
/kaggle/working/doc-agent-G21/data/interim/embed
/kaggle/working/doc-agent-G21/data/index
```

That's actually what you want for the Kaggle run.

And **you do not need to commit these generated files to GitHub**. For the Kaggle run, they're generated artifacts.

The important thing is that your final Kaggle notebook saves them under `/kaggle/working/...`, because that's what Kaggle's **Save Version / output preservation** can capture.

### One bigger issue, though

Your current OCR `.txt` → `Chunk` conversion does this:

```python
page_ids=[]
```

That means your final `chunks_meta.json` won't know which BYTE **pages** produced a chunk.

Since your project is supposed to answer questions grounded in scanned pages, **I'd strongly recommend checking the format of one of your OCR `.txt` files before doing the full run**. If it contains page/region boundaries, we should parse those into `page_ids` rather than throwing that information away.

If the `.txt` is literally just one giant text dump per issue, then the pipeline above will work technically, but your citation/grounding quality will be weaker.

AI: AH — okay, that changes things significantly, and actually **makes your life easier**. 😭

Your OCR `.txt` files are actually **JSON files stored with a `.txt` extension**, and they already contain the exact `Chunk` information that `chunk.py` expects.

So **do NOT create a new `Chunk` using the filename**. You should simply load the JSON and reconstruct the existing `Chunk` objects.

### The part that needs changing

In my previous Kaggle code, I gave you this:

```python
text = ocr_file.read_text(encoding="utf-8")

doc_id = ocr_file.stem

raw_chunks = [
    Chunk(
        id=f"{doc_id}_ocr",
        doc_id=doc_id,
        text=text,
        page_ids=[],
        score=0.0,
    )
]
```

❌ **Delete that entire part.**

Replace it with:

```python
import json

with ocr_file.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    data = json.load(f)

raw_chunks = [
    Chunk(**item)
    for item in data
]

print(
    f"Loaded {len(raw_chunks)} OCR chunks "
    f"from {ocr_file.name}"
)

print(
    f"Document: {raw_chunks[0].doc_id}"
)
```

Now your pipeline becomes:

```text
OCR .txt
   ↓
JSON list
   ↓
Chunk(**item)
   ↓
raw_chunks
   ↓
chunk.split()
   ↓
BEFORE_INDEX hook
   ↓
embed.encode()
   ↓
store.build()
```

And **crucially**, your existing metadata is preserved:

```text
id
doc_id
text
page_ids
score
```

So when `chunk.py` creates the larger chunks, it can preserve the page information.

### Your full per-issue section should therefore look like this

Replace the corresponding section in the full Kaggle loop with:

```python
# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 1. Load OCR chunks from JSON-formatted .txt file
# ---------------------------------------------------------

with ocr_file.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    data = json.load(f)

raw_chunks = [
    Chunk(**item)
    for item in data
]

if not raw_chunks:
    print("⚠️ Empty OCR file. Skipping.")
    continue

doc_id = raw_chunks[0].doc_id

print(
    f"Loaded {len(raw_chunks)} OCR chunks"
)
print(
    f"Document: {doc_id}"
)

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 2. Stage 4 — re-chunk
# ---------------------------------------------------------

final_chunks = chunk.split(
    raw_chunks,
    cfg
)

print(
    f"Generated {len(final_chunks)} final chunks."
)

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 3. BEFORE_INDEX hook
# ---------------------------------------------------------

hook_result = hooks.run(
    hooks.BEFORE_INDEX,
    {"chunks": final_chunks}
)

final_chunks = hook_result["chunks"]

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 4. Embed
# ---------------------------------------------------------

vectors = embed.encode(
    final_chunks,
    cfg
)

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 5. Incrementally add to FAISS
# ---------------------------------------------------------

store.build(
    final_chunks,
    vectors,
    cfg
)
```

### And your page information is retained 🎉

For example, your input:

```json
{
  "id": "..._p0001_region_0000",
  "doc_id": "...",
  "text": "Issue #1",
  "page_ids": [
    "..._p0001"
  ],
  "score": 0.0
}
```

becomes an input to:

```python
chunk.split(raw_chunks, cfg)
```

Your friend's `chunk.py` collects the `page_ids` while building the larger chunks, so the resulting final chunks can still contain something like:

```json
{
  "id": "..._c0000",
  "doc_id": "...",
  "text": "...",
  "page_ids": [
    "..._p0001",
    "..._p0002"
  ]
}
```

That is **much better** than the approach I initially suggested where I would have thrown away `page_ids`.

So basically: **your OCR outputs are already serialized `Chunk` objects. Just deserialize them. Don't reconstruct them from the filename.**

AI: Yep — both of those are easy to account for. And the `faiss` error is separate from your OCR-file handling.

### 1. Kaggle OCR dataset path

Use:

```python
OCR_DIR = Path(
    "/kaggle/input/datasets/thesisworkb7/byte-magazine-ocr-v1"
)
```

Since you have **both `.txt` and `.json`**, don't filter for only one extension.

Use:

```python
ocr_files = sorted(
    p for p in OCR_DIR.iterdir()
    if p.suffix.lower() in {".txt", ".json"}
)

print(f"Found {len(ocr_files)} OCR files")

for p in ocr_files[:10]:
    print(p.name)
```

Your files can then be loaded identically because both contain JSON:

```python
with ocr_file.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    data = json.load(f)

raw_chunks = [
    Chunk(**item)
    for item in data
]
```

So the **extension doesn't actually matter** here; `json.load()` cares about the contents, not whether the filename ends in `.json` or `.txt`.

rl:        {algo: "ppo", train\_policy: false}                         # bonus: set true
serve:     {batch: true}
deploy:    {target: "hf-spaces"}

AI: Mostly yes **for the code you showed**, but there are a few mismatches I'd fix before running the full pipeline.

### Main mismatch: `embed.py` vs `config.yaml`

Your `embed.py` expects:

```yaml
embed:
  model: ...
  dim: ...
```

So structurally that's correct.

But your config says:

```yaml
embed:
  model: "sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2"
  dim: 384
```

and your `embed.py` default is:

```python
"jinaai/jina-embeddings-v5-text-small"
```

That's fine because **config overrides the default**. Therefore you'll actually use:

```text
sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2
```

and it produces 384-dimensional vectors, matching `dim: 384`. ✅

---

### `store.py` vs config

Your `store.py` looks for:

```python
index_cfg.get("hnsw_m", 32)
index_cfg.get("ef_construction", 200)
index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)
```

But your config currently has:

```yaml
index:
  type: "faiss:hnsw"
  chunk_tokens: 256
  overlap: 32
```

So the HNSW parameters **aren't actually specified**.

That's not an error because `store.py` has defaults:

```text
M              = 32
efConstruction = 200
efSearch       = 64
```

But if these are supposed to be configurable for your NFR experiments, I'd change it to:

```yaml
index:
  type: "faiss:hnsw"
  chunk_tokens: 256
  overlap: 32
  hnsw_m: 32
  ef_construction: 200
  ef_search: 64
```

That makes your experiment parameters explicit and reproducible.

---

### Bigger issue: `device: cuda`

You have:

```yaml
device: cuda
```

This is okay **for Kaggle GPU**, but remember your `embed.py` doesn't actually read `cfg["device"]`.

`SentenceTransformer` will automatically decide its device unless you explicitly give one.

So:

```yaml
device: cuda
```

doesn't currently guarantee that `embed.py` uses CUDA.

If you're testing on Kaggle GPU, check:

```python
import torch

print(torch.cuda.is_available())
print(torch.cuda.get_device_name(0) if torch.cuda.is_available() else "CPU")
```

For your OCR code, however, `Qwen2VLForConditionalGeneration.from_pretrained(... device_map="auto")` handles device placement itself.

---

### `retrieve.reranker`

Your config says:

```yaml
rerank: true
reranker: "cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L6-v2"
```

That's fine **if `retriever.py` actually reads those keys**. We haven't checked that file yet, so I wouldn't claim this part is verified.

---

### One important thing for your current Kaggle task

Since you're **NOT running OCR now**, you don't need to worry about:

```yaml
ocr:
  model: "Qwen/Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct"
```

Your current workflow is:

```text
Downloaded OCR JSON/TXT
        ↓
Chunk
        ↓
BEFORE_INDEX hooks
        ↓
Embedding
        ↓
FAISS HNSW
```

So the relevant config for this Kaggle run is primarily:

```yaml
embed:
  model: "sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2"
  dim: 384

index:
  type: "faiss:hnsw"
  chunk_tokens: 256
  overlap: 32
  hnsw_m: 32
  ef_construction: 200
  ef_search: 64
```

### One more thing I'd change

Your current config says:

```yaml
seed: 42
device: cuda
```

but your `embed.py` doesn't use `seed` or `device`.

That's **not necessarily wrong**—it just means those settings are currently unused by that module.

So overall:

| Config               | Code consistency                                            |
| -------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
| `embed.model`        | ✅                                                           |
| `embed.dim: 384`     | ✅                                                           |
| `index.chunk_tokens` | ✅                                                           |
| `index.overlap`      | ✅                                                           |
| `index.type`         | ✅ conceptually                                              |
| `index.hnsw_m`       | ⚠️ missing → uses default                                   |
| `ef_construction`    | ⚠️ missing → uses default                                   |
| `ef_search`          | ⚠️ missing → uses default                                   |
| `device: cuda`       | ⚠️ not used by `embed.py`                                   |
| OCR settings         | ✅ for `ocr.py`, but irrelevant to current post-OCR pipeline |

**So yes, your config is compatible enough to run, but I'd add the three HNSW parameters before you start the full Kaggle run.**

AI: Yep — and this comparison matters **a lot** for your BYTE Retriever because you're doing semantic retrieval over a huge OCR corpus.

## 1. Core difference

The biggest difference is basically:

> **MiniLM = small, fast, older, short-text English embedding model.**
> **Jina v5 small = much larger, newer, retrieval-focused, multilingual, long-context embedding model.**

|                          | `all-MiniLM-L6-v2`                    | `jina-embeddings-v5-text-small`                 |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------- |
| Parameters               | ~22M                                  | **677M**                                        |
| Embedding size           | **384**                               | **1024**                                        |
| Max input                | **256 word pieces**                   | **32,768 tokens**                               |
| Languages                | Primarily English                     | **119+ languages**                              |
| Main use                 | Sentence similarity / semantic search | Retrieval, matching, clustering, classification |
| Architecture             | MiniLM                                | Qwen3-0.6B based                                |
| Retrieval specialization | General-purpose                       | **Explicit retrieval training + task adapters** |
| Matryoshka dimensions    | No                                    | **32–1024**                                     |
| Memory/speed             | 🟢 Excellent                          | 🔴 Much heavier                                 |
| Age                      | Older                                 | Released Feb 2026                               |

MiniLM's model card explicitly describes it as a sentence/short-paragraph encoder and says inputs beyond 256 word pieces are truncated. ([Hugging Face][1])

Jina v5 small is a 677M-parameter model with 32K-token context, 1024-dimensional embeddings and retrieval-specific training/adapters. ([Hugging Face][2])

---

# 2. Do the pip commands change?

### If you use MiniLM

Your current code:

```python
from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer

model = SentenceTransformer(
    "sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2"
)
```

Install:

```bash
pip install sentence-transformers
```

That's it.

### If you use Jina v5 small

You can **still use SentenceTransformers**, so your existing `embed.py` architecture doesn't have to change:

```python
from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer

model = SentenceTransformer(
    "jinaai/jina-embeddings-v5-text-small",
    trust_remote_code=True
)
```

The Jina model specifically supports the SentenceTransformers interface. ([Hugging Face][2])

However, its documented requirements include newer versions of:

```bash
pip install -U \
    "transformers>=4.57.0" \
    "torch>=2.8.0" \
    "peft>=0.15.2" \
    sentence-transformers
```

Flash Attention is recommended but optional. ([Hugging Face][2])

So on Kaggle, **don't immediately install random versions of everything**. Check what's already installed first:

```python
import torch
import transformers
import sentence_transformers

print("torch:", torch.__version__)
print("transformers:", transformers.__version__)
print("sentence-transformers:", sentence_transformers.__version__)
```

Then install only what's missing/outdated.

---

# 3. Why Jina could be BETTER for your project

This is where I lean toward **Jina for BYTE Retriever**.

### Reason #1 — Your task is retrieval

Your actual task is:

> Given a question, retrieve the relevant passage from BYTE magazine.

That's not merely "are these two sentences similar?"

Jina v5 small has a specific **retrieval task adapter**, alongside adapters for text matching, clustering and classification. ([Jina][3])

That's much more aligned with:

```text
query
  ↓
embedding
  ↓
FAISS
  ↓
relevant BYTE chunk
```

than a generic sentence embedding model.

---

### Reason #2 — Your chunks are 256 tokens

You currently have:

```yaml
chunk_tokens: 256
```

This is interesting.

MiniLM's model card says text beyond **256 word pieces is truncated**. ([Hugging Face][1])

Your chunker is therefore sitting **right at MiniLM's input ceiling**.

Imagine your chunk contains:

```text
BYTE magazine article...

"The Apple II was introduced..."
...
[256 tokens]
```

You're okay-ish.

But tokenization isn't identical to `text.split()`.

Your chunker says:

```python
len(buffer_words)
```

while MiniLM's limit is measured in **word pieces/subword tokens**.

So a 256-"word" chunk can actually exceed MiniLM's 256-token input limit and get truncated.

Jina supports up to **32K tokens**, so 256-token chunks are nowhere near its ceiling. ([Hugging Face][2])

---

### Reason #3 — BYTE has weird technical vocabulary

This is important.

BYTE isn't:

> "The cat sat on the mat."

It has stuff like:

```text
8086
6502
CP/M
RS-232
BASIC
FORTRAN
microprocessor
DMA
interrupt vectors
hexadecimal
ASCII
```

A stronger modern embedding model may produce better semantic representations for technical queries such as:

> "Which processor architecture was used by this machine?"

versus a passage containing:

> "The system employs an 8086 CPU..."

That's exactly the kind of semantic gap retrieval models are intended to bridge.

I'd **expect Jina to have an advantage**, although you should verify this experimentally on your actual BYTE queries rather than assuming benchmark scores transfer perfectly.

---

### Reason #4 — Long-context capability gives you room later

Today:

```text
chunk = 256 tokens
```

Tomorrow you might discover:

> "Oh crap, our OCR regions are tiny and we're losing context."

You could increase chunk size to:

```text
384
512
768
1024
```

MiniLM becomes increasingly problematic because of its short input limit.

Jina gives you enormous headroom with its 32K context. ([Jina][3])

---

### Reason #5 — Matryoshka embeddings

Jina supports:

```text
1024
 ↓
768
 ↓
512
 ↓
256
 ↓
128
 ↓
64
 ↓
32
```

dimensions. ([Jina][3])

That's useful for your **scalability NFR**.

For example, you could potentially experiment with:

```text
1024-d → maximum quality
512-d  → smaller FAISS index
256-d  → even smaller/faster
```

without having to completely change embedding models.

That's pretty useful when your corpus grows massively.

---

# 4. Why Jina could be WORSE

And this is where I don't want you to blindly switch just because "new model = better." 😭

### Reason #1 — It's ~30× larger

MiniLM:

```text
~22M parameters
```

Jina:

```text
677M parameters
```

That's a **massive** difference. ([Hugging Face][1])

Your embedding stage:

```text
1805 chunks
```

might be perfectly manageable.

But imagine:

```text
500,000 chunks
```

You're doing hundreds of thousands of forward passes.

Jina will consume substantially more compute and memory.

---

### Reason #2 — Your FAISS index becomes larger

MiniLM:

```text
384 dimensions
```

Jina:

```text
1024 dimensions
```

So, ignoring FAISS overhead:

**one million vectors**

MiniLM:

```text
1,000,000 × 384 × 4 bytes
≈ 1.54 GB
```

Jina:

```text
1,000,000 × 1024 × 4 bytes
≈ 4.10 GB
```

That's roughly **2.67× more vector storage**.

And you're specifically trying to demonstrate a scalability NFR.

So this matters.

---

### Reason #3 — Your actual corpus is English

BYTE is overwhelmingly English.

Jina's multilingual capability is fantastic, but you're potentially paying for capabilities you don't need.

MiniLM is designed around English sentence/paragraph embeddings. ([Hugging Face][1])

So if your evaluation says:

> "Our corpus is English and our queries are English."

MiniLM has a very legitimate argument.

---

### Reason #4 — Your chunks are already small

The biggest Jina advantage is long context.

But you're doing:

```text
256-token chunks
```

So you're not really exploiting its:

```text
32,768-token context
```

capability.

That's like buying a truck because it can carry 2 tons and then transporting a sandwich.

😂

---

### Reason #5 — Jina introduces more dependency complexity

Your MiniLM pipeline is wonderfully boring:

```python
SentenceTransformer(...)
```

Jina's current documentation calls for newer Transformers/PyTorch/PEFT versions, with optional Flash Attention. ([Hugging Face][2])

For a project where **you have very little time**, boring can be a feature.

---

# So which would I choose for BYTE Retriever?

Honestly?

### If your priority is:

**"I need something that works reliably and quickly."**

→ **MiniLM**

### If your priority is:

**"I want the strongest retrieval model I can reasonably run on Kaggle and I want a technically defensible research choice."**

→ **Jina v5 small**

And there's an especially nice argument for Jina in your project:

> BYTE Retriever is a **retrieval system**, not merely a sentence similarity system.

Jina v5 small was explicitly trained for retrieval and reports 71.7 MTEB English v2 and 67.7 MMTEB averages, though those benchmark numbers should not be treated as a guarantee on your specific BYTE corpus. ([Hugging Face][2])

**But:** if you switch to Jina, your `embed.dim` needs to change from:

```yaml
dim: 384
```

to:

```yaml
dim: 1024
```

unless you deliberately use one of Jina's Matryoshka truncated dimensions. ([Hugging Face][2])

# 5. Now the FAISS settings — these are actually pretty important

Your config:

```yaml
type: "faiss:hnsw"
chunk_tokens: 256
overlap: 32
hnsw_m: 32
ef_construction: 200
ef_search: 64
```

These are doing **different jobs**.

Think of the whole thing as:

```text
BYTE articles
     ↓
split into chunks
     ↓
convert chunks → vectors
     ↓
put vectors into a graph
     ↓
search graph for nearest vectors
```

Let's take them one by one.

---

## `type: "faiss:hnsw"`

This says:

> Use FAISS with an **HNSW** vector index.

HNSW = **Hierarchical Navigable Small World** graph.

Imagine you have 1,000,000 BYTE chunks.

Each chunk becomes a vector:

```text
Chunk A → [0.12, -0.04, 0.88, ...]
Chunk B → [0.10, -0.01, 0.91, ...]
Chunk C → [-0.71, 0.22, ...]
```

You want to search:

> "What computers used the 8086 processor?"

Instead of comparing your query against **every single vector**, HNSW creates a graph that lets FAISS navigate toward nearby vectors.

Very roughly:

```text
             A
           /   \
          B     C
        / |     | \
       D  E     F  G
          |
          H
```

Search starts somewhere and moves through increasingly promising neighbors.

That's why HNSW is useful for large-scale approximate nearest-neighbor search.

---

# `chunk_tokens: 256`

This belongs to your **chunking stage**, not FAISS.

It says:

> Try to make each final retrieval chunk approximately 256 words/tokens according to your chunker's counting logic.

For example:

```text
Original OCR:

BYTE magazine article
─────────────────────

word word word word
...
256 words
```

becomes:

```text
Chunk 0
~256 words
```

Then:

```text
Chunk 1
~256 words
```

etc.

Why not make everything one giant document?

Because a query like:

> "What processor does the Altair use?"

should retrieve the **small relevant passage**, rather than a 10-page article.

---

# `overlap: 32`

This prevents information from being chopped awkwardly between chunks.

Suppose:

```text
Chunk 0:
A B C D E ... X Y Z
```

and the next chunk starts immediately:

```text
Chunk 1:
AA BB CC DD ...
```

What if the important sentence is split exactly at the boundary?

Instead, with overlap:

```text
Chunk 0:
A B C ... W X Y Z

Chunk 1:
Y Z AA BB CC ...
```

So the last 32 tokens of one chunk are repeated at the beginning of the next.

### Why?

Imagine:

```text
Chunk 0:
"The IBM PC was introduced in 1981 and used the Intel"

Chunk 1:
"8088 processor..."
```

Without overlap, your embedding might have:

```text
"The IBM PC ... used the Intel"
```

and:

```text
"8088 processor..."
```

Neither chunk completely represents:

> IBM PC → Intel 8088

With overlap, the boundary has a better chance of preserving context.

---

# `hnsw_m: 32`

This is **graph connectivity**.

Each vector gets connections to approximately `M` neighbors in the HNSW graph.

So:

```yaml
hnsw_m: 32
```

roughly means:

> Give each node a fairly rich neighborhood of connections.

Think:

```text
M = 4

        A
      / | \
     B  C  D
```

versus:

```text
M = 32

        A
   / / / | \ \ \
  B C D E F G H ...
```

Higher `M`:

✅ potentially better recall
✅ richer graph
❌ more memory
❌ larger index
❌ slower construction

Lower `M`:

✅ smaller
✅ faster construction
❌ potentially poorer search quality

`32` is a reasonable starting point for experimentation.

---

# `ef_construction: 200`

This affects **building the HNSW graph**.

Think of it as:

> "How hard should FAISS work while constructing the graph to find good connections?"

Imagine adding a new vector:

```text
New chunk → ?
```

FAISS considers possible neighbors.

With:

```yaml
ef_construction: 20
```

it does a relatively limited search.

With:

```yaml
ef_construction: 200
```

it explores a much larger candidate set.

So:

```text
ef_construction ↑
       ↓
better graph construction
       ↓
potentially better recall
       ↓
more build time
```

The important thing:

### This is mostly a one-time cost.

You're not paying `200` every time somebody asks a query.

---

# `ef_search: 64`

THIS one matters to your NFR.

It controls how hard HNSW searches **during retrieval**.

Suppose you ask:

> "Which processor was used in the IBM PC?"

The query enters the HNSW graph.

With:

```yaml
ef_search: 10
```

FAISS explores relatively few candidates.

With:

```yaml
ef_search: 64
```

it considers more candidates.

With:

```yaml
ef_search: 200
```

it searches harder.

So:

```text
ef_search ↑
      ↓
better probability of finding true nearest neighbors
      ↓
higher recall
      ↓
slower query
```

And:

```text
ef_search ↓
      ↓
faster query
      ↓
potentially lower recall
```

Which connects **directly** to your project's:

> P95 end-to-end query latency ≤ 1.5 seconds.

That's why your friend's comment about `efSearch` being the main scalability lever isn't random.

---

# Put everything together

Your system is basically doing:

```text
                 BYTE OCR
                    │
                    ▼
        ┌─────────────────────┐
        │ Chunk into ~256     │
        │ token pieces        │
        └──────────┬──────────┘
                   │
                   │ 32-token overlap
                   ▼
        ┌─────────────────────┐
        │ Chunk 0             │
        │ Chunk 1             │
        │ Chunk 2             │
        │ ...                 │
        └──────────┬──────────┘
                   │
                   ▼
             Embedding model
                   │
                   ▼
             Vector 0
             Vector 1
             Vector 2
                ...
                   │
                   ▼
          ┌──────────────────┐
          │   HNSW Graph     │
          │                  │
          │ M = 32           │
          │ efConstruction   │
          │      = 200       │
          └────────┬─────────┘
                   │
             User query
                   │
                   ▼
             efSearch = 64
                   │
                   ▼
             Top-k chunks
```

### And for your project specifically

I'd start with:

```yaml
embed:
  model: "jinaai/jina-embeddings-v5-text-small"
  dim: 1024

index:
  type: "faiss:hnsw"
  chunk_tokens: 256
  overlap: 32
  hnsw_m: 32
  ef_construction: 200
  ef_search: 64
```

**BUT** I would not blindly declare Jina "better." Your A2 evaluation should ideally compare **MiniLM vs Jina on a small fixed set of BYTE questions** using something like Recall@k / Hit@k and latency. That gives you an actual project-specific justification rather than "the model card says it's better."

And honestly, that comparison could make a **very nice little experiment for your report**: same chunks → two embedding models → same HNSW settings → compare retrieval quality + embedding time + index size.

[1]: https://huggingface.co/sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2?utm_source=chatgpt.com "sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2 · Hugging Face"
[2]: https://huggingface.co/jinaai/jina-embeddings-v5-text-small?utm_source=chatgpt.com "jinaai/jina-embeddings-v5-text-small · Hugging Face"
[3]: https://jina.ai/models/jina-embeddings-v5-text-small/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "jina-embeddings-v5-text-small - Search Foundation Models"

Me: 1. what does Matryoshka dimensions mean , short example pls 
2. as this is an agentic RAG project, i think miniLM will not perform well but jina v5 small will take too long to generate the embedding, isnt there any middle ground options as in english based small retrieval model ?

AI: Yep. There **is** a pretty sensible middle ground, and for your BYTE Retriever I think it's worth considering.

## 1. Matryoshka dimensions — tiny example

Think of a Matryoshka embedding as a vector designed so that the **first part still contains a useful representation** of the whole thing.

Suppose the full embedding is:

```text
[0.12, -0.31, 0.88, 0.42, -0.17, 0.63, ... 1024 numbers ...]
```

A Matryoshka model can let you use:

```text
1024 dimensions → maximum representation
512 dimensions  → smaller but still useful
256 dimensions  → smaller again
128 dimensions  → even smaller
```

without training a completely separate model for every dimension.

So, roughly:

```text
Full embedding
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ 1024 dimensions                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
             ↓ truncate
┌───────────────────────────┐
│ first 512 dimensions      │
└───────────────────────────┘
             ↓
┌───────────────┐
│ first 256     │
└───────────────┘
```

It's called *Matryoshka* because it's like Russian nesting dolls: a smaller useful representation is nested inside the larger one.

For your project, the appeal is:

> **Jina quality/model + potentially much smaller FAISS vectors.**

---

# 2. And YES — I think you should consider a middle ground

Your reasoning is actually pretty reasonable:

### MiniLM

```text
22M parameters
384 dimensions
```

Very cheap.

But it wasn't specifically designed as a modern retrieval model, and its input is limited to 256 word pieces. ([Hugging Face][1])

### Jina v5 small

```text
677M parameters
1024 dimensions
32K context
retrieval-oriented
```

Much more capable, but potentially overkill for your workload.

So I'd look at **small retrieval-specific English models**.

---

# My first choice: BGE-small-en-v1.5

BAAI's:

```text
BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5
```

is basically sitting in the sweet spot you're looking for.

It has:

* **384 dimensions**
* **512-token input**
* English-focused
* designed for embedding/retrieval
* works directly with SentenceTransformers
* only **~33M parameters** (roughly, depending on implementation)
* MIT license

Its published MTEB results show:

```text
Overall:    62.17
Retrieval:  51.68
```

compared with:

```text
all-MiniLM-L6-v2
```

which is older and primarily intended as a general sentence/paragraph embedding model. BGE-small's model card explicitly positions the BGE family around retrieval and reports the above benchmark numbers. ([Hugging Face][1])

### This is the important part:

Your current pipeline:

```python
model = SentenceTransformer(
    "sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2"
)
```

could basically become:

```python
model = SentenceTransformer(
    "BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5"
)
```

and your dimensionality remains:

```yaml
dim: 384
```

So your FAISS storage doesn't suddenly explode from 384 → 1024.

That's **very attractive for your scalability requirement**.

---

# Another good candidate: E5-small-v2

Another model I'd seriously consider is:

```text
intfloat/e5-small-v2
```

It's:

* English
* 384-dimensional
* 512-token maximum
* explicitly trained for retrieval
* relatively small
* SentenceTransformers-compatible. ([Hugging Face][2])

Its model card specifically tells you to use:

```text
query: ...
```

for queries and:

```text
passage: ...
```

for documents/passages because that's how it was trained. ([Hugging Face][2])

For example:

```python
query = "query: Which processor was used in the IBM PC?"
passage = "passage: The IBM PC was introduced with an Intel 8088 processor..."
```

That distinction is **very appropriate for your RAG architecture**, because you have asymmetric retrieval:

```text
USER QUESTION
     ↓
   query
     ↓
retrieve
     ↑
  passage
     ↑
BYTE CHUNK
```

Its published MTEB numbers are lower than BGE-small-v1.5's:

```text
                    Overall    Retrieval
```

# One important catch with BGE/E5

There's a small change I'd make to your `embed.py` if you choose either retrieval model.

For **BGE**, the model card recommends a retrieval instruction for queries, e.g.:

```text
Represent this sentence for searching relevant passages:
```

while passages can be encoded normally. ([Hugging Face][1])

For **E5**, you explicitly use:

```text
query: ...
passage: ...
```

([Hugging Face][2])

That's different from your current:

```python
texts = [c.text for c in chunks]
vectors = model.encode(texts)
```

And more importantly, your **query embedding code in `retriever.py` would also need to use the corresponding query format**.

So don't switch models merely by changing the YAML name and calling it done.
