Me: Would you still recommend BGE-small-en-v1.5 despite it not having the Matryoshka property?

AI: Yes — **I would still recommend BGE-small-en-v1.5 for your current project**, despite it not having Matryoshka dimensions.

In fact, for *your* constraints, I wouldn't treat Matryoshka as a deciding factor.

### Why?

Matryoshka is mainly useful when you want to say:

> "I want this model's embeddings, but I may later shrink 1024 → 512 → 256 dimensions to trade retrieval quality for storage/speed."

That's genuinely useful for Jina v5 small, which supports 32–1024 dimensions. ([Hugging Face][1])

But BGE-small **already starts at only 384 dimensions**. Its published configuration is 384-dimensional with a 512-token sequence length. ([Hugging Face][2])

So your situation is basically:

```text
Jina v5 small
677M parameters
1024 dimensions
        ↓
      Matryoshka
        ↓
512 / 256 / 128 ...
```

versus:

```text
BGE-small
~small model
384 dimensions
        ↓
already compact
```

You don't necessarily *need* to shrink BGE.

---

### And BGE is much more aligned with your immediate constraints

Your project has:

* huge corpus
* potentially hundreds of thousands of chunks
* Kaggle GPU availability
* limited time
* FAISS HNSW
* P95 latency NFR
* English BYTE corpus
* 256-ish token chunks

BGE-small gives you **384-dimensional vectors** and a 512-token maximum, while its published MTEB retrieval score is 51.68. ([Hugging Face][2])

That's a very reasonable compromise.

The alternative isn't:

> "BGE has no Matryoshka, therefore it's inferior."

It's:

> **"BGE starts compact enough that we may not need Matryoshka at all."**

AI: Yep. If you're switching to **BGE-small-en-v1.5** and using the HNSW settings from your `store.py`, I'd change those lines to:

```yaml
embed:     {model: "BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5", dim: 384}
index:     {type: "faiss:hnsw", chunk_tokens: 256, overlap: 32, hnsw_m: 32, ef_construction: 200, ef_search: 64}
```

### What each HNSW setting is doing

* `hnsw_m: 32` → each vector keeps up to 32 graph connections. Higher = potentially better recall, but larger/slower index.
* `ef_construction: 200` → effort spent **building** the HNSW graph. Higher = better graph quality, slower construction.
* `ef_search: 64` → effort spent **searching** the graph at query time. Higher = better recall, slower retrieval.

For your initial baseline, I'd keep:

```yaml
hnsw_m: 32
ef_construction: 200
ef_search: 64
```

They're reasonable starting values for your scalability experiment. You can later benchmark `ef_search` (e.g. 32, 64, 128) against your **P95 ≤ 1.5 s** requirement.

One other thing: because BGE is a retrieval model, you'll eventually want to modify the **query embedding** side in `retriever.py` to use BGE's recommended query instruction. Your document/chunk embeddings can remain normal passage embeddings. That's important for getting the benefit of the model.

Me: """Stage 5 — dense retrieval"""
from **future** import annotations
from ..contracts import \*  # noqa

class Retriever:
def **init**(self, cfg: dict) -> None:
self.cfg = cfg["retrieve"]
def retrieve(self, query: str, k: int | None = None) -> list[Chunk]:
"""Top-k dense retrieval. Set chunk.score (relevance) on every result so decide() can judge
whether the evidence is weak. IMPLEMENT."""
raise NotImplementedError("Stage 5: retrieve")

# --- evidence-strength policy: read by agent.decide() for evidence-gated re-search ---

def top\_score(chunks: list[Chunk]) -> float:
"""Strength of the current evidence = best chunk score (0.0 if empty)."""
return max((c.score for c in chunks), default=0.0)

def is\_weak(chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> bool:
"""Weak evidence = best score below cfg.retrieve.weak\_threshold."""
return top\_score(chunks) < cfg["retrieve"]["weak\_threshold"]

def next\_k(k: int, cfg: dict) -> int | None:
"""Widen the net: k + k\_step, or None once it would exceed k\_max (signal to ABSTAIN)."""
nk = k + cfg["retrieve"]["k\_step"]
return nk if nk <= cfg["retrieve"]["k\_max"] else None





this is the retriever.py for the next stage of this assignment, will using BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5 work? 

AI: Yes — **BGE-small-en-v1.5 will work with this `Retriever` design**, but there is an important distinction:

The `retriever.py` you showed is currently just a **stub**. It doesn't actually load the FAISS index or generate query embeddings yet.

Your eventual flow will be:

```text
User query
   ↓
BGE-small-en-v1.5
   ↓
384-dimensional query vector
   ↓
FAISS HNSW
   ↓
top-k chunk IDs
   ↓
chunks_meta.json
   ↓
Chunk objects with scores
```

### The important BGE-specific change

For BGE-small, you should ideally encode the **query** with BGE's retrieval instruction, while your document chunks are encoded as passages.

For example, conceptually:

```python
query = "Represent this sentence for searching relevant passages: " + query
```

Then:

```python
query_vector = model.encode([query])
```

while your chunk embedding stage uses the chunk text normally.

So your `embed.py` and `retriever.py` need to agree on the **same BGE model and 384-dimensional space**.

### Your config is already fine

```yaml
embed: {model: "BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5", dim: 384}

index:
  type: "faiss:hnsw"
  chunk_tokens: 256
  overlap: 32
  hnsw_m: 32
  ef_construction: 200
  ef_search: 64
```

### One thing I'd flag now

Your `top_score()` / `weak_threshold` assumes that the FAISS similarity score has a meaningful range where something like:

```yaml
weak_threshold: 0.35
```

makes sense.

That depends on **how you normalize embeddings and what FAISS index is actually calculating**.

Your current:

```python
faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)
```

uses **L2 distance**, not cosine similarity.

For BGE, cosine similarity is generally what you want. A common solution is:

```text
normalize embeddings
        ↓
IndexHNSWFlat
        ↓
METRIC_INNER_PRODUCT
```

because normalized inner product ≈ cosine similarity.

So **before implementing `Retriever.retrieve()`**, I'd actually fix/check `store.py` and `embed.py` so the entire pipeline is consistent:

```text
BGE chunk embedding
       ↓
L2 normalize
       ↓
FAISS inner-product HNSW
       ↓
BGE query embedding
       ↓
L2 normalize
       ↓
search
       ↓
cosine-like scores
```

Otherwise your `weak_threshold: 0.35` may not mean what you think it means.

**So: yes, BGE works — but I'd make the FAISS metric + normalization consistent with BGE before implementing Stage 5.**

Me: this is the store.py : 

"""Stage 4 — vector store"""
from **future** import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np
import faiss
from ..contracts import Chunk

INDEX\_DIR = Path("data/index")

def build(chunks: list[Chunk], vectors: np.ndarray, cfg: dict) -> None:
"""Incrementally add new vectors to the existing HNSW index.

```
New issues are appended to the existing FAISS index instead of rebuilding
the entire vector database.

Safety checks:
1. Number of chunks must equal number of vectors.
2. A document/issue (doc_id) must not already exist in the index.
   This prevents accidental duplicate indexing if the same issue is
   processed twice.
"""

index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})

INDEX_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

index_path = INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"
meta_path = INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json"

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 1. Basic chunk/vector consistency check
# ---------------------------------------------------------
#
# FAISS assigns vector IDs based on their position:
#
#   FAISS ID 0 -> chunks_meta[0]
#   FAISS ID 1 -> chunks_meta[1]
#   ...
#
# Therefore these two lists MUST have the same length.
# ---------------------------------------------------------
if len(chunks) != len(vectors):
    raise ValueError(
        f"Chunk/vector mismatch: "
        f"{len(chunks)} chunks but {len(vectors)} vectors."
    )

if len(chunks) == 0:
    print("No chunks supplied. Nothing to add.")
    return

vectors = np.ascontiguousarray(vectors.astype("float32"))
dim = vectors.shape[1]

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 2. Load existing metadata and detect duplicate issues
# ---------------------------------------------------------
#
# chunks_meta.json contains the metadata for every vector
# currently stored in FAISS.
#
# We use doc_id to determine whether an entire issue has
# already been indexed.
# ---------------------------------------------------------
if meta_path.exists():
    with meta_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        existing_metadata = json.load(f)
else:
    existing_metadata = []

existing_doc_ids = {
    item["doc_id"]
    for item in existing_metadata
}

new_doc_ids = {
    c.doc_id
    for c in chunks
}

duplicate_doc_ids = new_doc_ids & existing_doc_ids

if duplicate_doc_ids:
    raise ValueError(
        "\n❌ Duplicate issue detected!\n"
        f"The following doc_id(s) are already indexed:\n"
        + "\n".join(
            f"  - {doc_id}"
            for doc_id in sorted(duplicate_doc_ids)
        )
        + "\n\nRefusing to modify the FAISS index."
        "\nThis prevents accidental duplicate indexing."
    )

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 3. Load existing FAISS index OR create a new one
# ---------------------------------------------------------
m = index_cfg.get("hnsw_m", 32)
ef_construction = index_cfg.get("ef_construction", 200)
ef_search = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

if index_path.exists():

    print(f"Loading existing FAISS index from {index_path}")

    index = faiss.read_index(str(index_path))

    # The embedding model must produce the same dimensionality
    # as the vectors already stored in the index.
    if index.d != dim:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Embedding dimension mismatch: "
            f"existing index has dim={index.d}, "
            f"but new vectors have dim={dim}."
        )

    print(
        f"Existing index contains {index.ntotal} vectors."
    )
    print(
        f"Adding {len(vectors)} vectors from "
        f"{len(new_doc_ids)} new issue(s)."
    )

else:

    print(
        "No existing FAISS index found. "
        "Creating a new HNSW index."
    )

    index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)

    # Controls how thoroughly HNSW searches for good neighbors
    # while constructing the graph.
    index.hnsw.efConstruction = ef_construction

# efSearch controls search effort at query time.
index.hnsw.efSearch = ef_search

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 4. Add ONLY the new vectors
# ---------------------------------------------------------
#
# Existing vectors remain untouched.
#
# Example:
#
# First run:
#   FAISS = Issue A
#
# Second run:
#   FAISS = Issue A + Issue B
#
# Third run:
#   FAISS = Issue A + Issue B + Issue C
# ---------------------------------------------------------
index.add(vectors)

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 5. Save the updated FAISS index
# ---------------------------------------------------------
faiss.write_index(index, str(index_path))

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 6. Append metadata for the newly indexed chunks
# ---------------------------------------------------------
#
# The order here MUST match the order in which vectors were
# added to FAISS.
# ---------------------------------------------------------
existing_metadata.extend(
    c.model_dump()
    for c in chunks
)

with meta_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    json.dump(
        existing_metadata,
        f,
        ensure_ascii=False,
        indent=2,
    )

# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 7. Report final index status
# ---------------------------------------------------------
size_mb = index_path.stat().st_size / (1024 ** 2)

print(
    f"\n✅ Successfully updated HNSW index."
    f"\n   Added chunks : {len(chunks)}"
    f"\n   Total vectors: {index.ntotal}"
    f"\n   Dimensions   : {dim}"
    f"\n   HNSW M       : {m}"
    f"\n   Index size   : {size_mb:.2f} MB"
    f"\n   Index path   : {index_path}"
)
```

def load(cfg: dict):
"""Load the persisted index + its chunk metadata."""
index\_path = INDEX\_DIR / "index.faiss"
meta\_path = INDEX\_DIR / "chunks\_meta.json"

```
if not index_path.exists():
    raise FileNotFoundError(f"No index found at {index_path} — run build() first.")

index = faiss.read_index(str(index_path))

# efSearch doesn't get saved with the index — reset it here in case
# something changed in cfg since build() ran.
index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})
index.hnsw.efSearch = index_cfg.get("ef_search", 64)

with open(meta_path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    chunk_dicts = json.load(f)

chunks = [Chunk(**d) for d in chunk_dicts]

print(f"Loaded index: {index.ntotal} vectors, dim={index.d}")
return index, chunks
and this is the embed.py: """Stage 4 — embed chunks"""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np
from ..contracts import Chunk

EMBED_DIR = Path("data/interim/embed")
_model = None


def _get_model(cfg: dict):
    global _model
    if _model is not None:
        return _model
    from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
    model_name = cfg.get("embed", {}).get("model", "jinaai/jina-embeddings-v5-text-small")
    _model = SentenceTransformer(model_name, trust_remote_code=True)
    return _model


def encode(chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> np.ndarray:
    """Embed with cfg['embed']['model']."""
    model = _get_model(cfg)
    texts = [c.text for c in chunks]

    vectors = model.encode(
        texts,
        batch_size=32,
        show_progress_bar=True,
        convert_to_numpy=True,
    )

    expected_dim = cfg.get("embed", {}).get("dim")
    if expected_dim and vectors.shape[1] != expected_dim:
        print(f"Warning: got dim {vectors.shape[1]}, config expected {expected_dim}")

    EMBED_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    np.save(EMBED_DIR / "vectors.npy", vectors)
    with open(EMBED_DIR / "chunk_ids.json", "w") as f:
        json.dump([c.id for c in chunks], f)
    print(f"Saved {vectors.shape[0]} vectors (dim={vectors.shape[1]}) to {EMBED_DIR}")

    return vectors
```

AI: Yep — seeing both files makes the situation much clearer.

**Your `store.py` is already incremental**, but there is one important issue if you're switching to **BGE-small**: your current `embed.py` + `store.py` use **raw embeddings + `IndexHNSWFlat`**, which means FAISS is currently doing **L2-distance search**, not cosine similarity.

For BGE, I'd recommend making the embedding/indexing pipeline cosine-compatible.

### 1. Modify `embed.py`

Change the `model.encode()` call to normalize the embeddings:

```python
vectors = model.encode(
    texts,
    batch_size=32,
    show_progress_bar=True,
    convert_to_numpy=True,
    normalize_embeddings=True,   # BGE: normalize for cosine similarity
)
```

That's the main embedding-side change.

---

### 2. Modify `store.py`

Change:

```python
index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)
```

to:

```python
index = faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(
    dim,
    m,
    faiss.METRIC_INNER_PRODUCT,
)
```

Because you've normalized the vectors:

```text
normalized vector A · normalized vector B
                 ↓
            cosine similarity
```

So now your FAISS scores are cosine-similarity-like scores rather than L2 distances.

**This matters a lot for your `weak_threshold: 0.35`.**

With the current L2 index, a score of `0.35` doesn't mean "35% similarity." With normalized vectors + inner product, it has a much more intuitive interpretation as cosine similarity.

---

### 3. Your incremental logic is otherwise good

Your current `store.py` does:

```text
Issue A
 ↓
index.faiss
chunks_meta.json

Issue B
 ↓
load existing index
 ↓
add B's vectors
 ↓
save updated index

Issue C
 ↓
load A+B
 ↓
add C
 ↓
save A+B+C
```

And this part:

```python
duplicate_doc_ids = new_doc_ids & existing_doc_ids

if duplicate_doc_ids:
    raise ValueError(...)
```

protects you against accidentally feeding the same **issue** twice.

So you don't need to redesign that part.

---

### 4. One thing you MUST be careful about

Once you create an index with:

```python
faiss.METRIC_INNER_PRODUCT
```

**you cannot append vectors to an old L2 index.**

So if you already generated an `index.faiss` using:

```python
faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m)
```

you should **delete/rebuild that index once** after making this change.

After that, incremental additions are perfectly fine:

```text
NEW BGE index
      │
      ├── Issue 1
      ├── Issue 2
      ├── Issue 3
      │
      └── later → Issue 4
                  ↓
             append vectors
```

You don't need to rebuild the whole database every time.

---

### 5. And your config should be

```yaml
embed: {model: "BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5", dim: 384}

index:
  type: "faiss:hnsw"
  chunk_tokens: 256
  overlap: 32
  hnsw_m: 32
  ef_construction: 200
  ef_search: 64
```

One final thing: **BGE's query-side encoding also needs to be handled consistently** when you implement `retriever.py`. Your document embeddings can be normalized as above, but the query embedding must also be normalized before the inner-product search. Otherwise the cosine-similarity assumption breaks.

Me: 1. but why is the normalize\_embeddings needed? short answer with an example 
2. where did you get the weak\_threshold from? 
3. why is the faiss.METRIC\_INNER\_PRODUCT required, short ans with an example 

AI: Yep, short version:

### 1. Why `normalize_embeddings=True`?

It makes every embedding have **length 1**, so comparing two vectors depends on their **direction/meaning**, not their magnitude.

Example:

```text
A = [3, 4]       → length = 5
B = [6, 8]       → length = 10
```

They're pointing in exactly the same direction, so they're semantically identical for our purposes.

After normalization:

```text
A → [0.6, 0.8]
B → [0.6, 0.8]
```

Now their dot product is:

```text
0.6×0.6 + 0.8×0.8 = 1
```

→ maximum cosine similarity.

---

### 2. Where did `weak_threshold` come from?

**Your own `config.yaml`**, not from me:

```yaml
retrieve:
  k: 10
  k_step: 10
  k_max: 40
  weak_threshold: 0.35
```

And your `retriever.py` explicitly uses it:

```python
return top_score(chunks) < cfg["retrieve"]["weak_threshold"]
```

So `0.35` is currently **your project's configured threshold**.

One caveat: after switching to cosine similarity, you should **experimentally validate whether 0.35 is a good threshold** for your BYTE corpus rather than assuming it's optimal.

---

### 3. Why `faiss.METRIC_INNER_PRODUCT`?

Because after normalization:

**dot product = cosine similarity.**

Example:

```text
Query       = [0.6, 0.8]
Document A  = [0.6, 0.8]

dot product = 0.36 + 0.64 = 1.0
```

Very similar → score **1.0**.

Whereas:

```text
Query       = [0.6, 0.8]
Document B  = [-0.8, 0.6]

dot product = -0.48 + 0.48 = 0
```

Unrelated direction → score **0**.

So:

```python
faiss.IndexHNSWFlat(dim, m, faiss.METRIC_INNER_PRODUCT)
```

tells FAISS:

> "Find vectors with the **largest dot product**."

And because we normalized them, that's effectively:

> "Find vectors with the **highest cosine similarity**."

That's exactly what we want for semantic retrieval with BGE.

### C15 — Test incremental addition with a second OCR file

```python

from doc_agent.index import chunk, embed, store

# ---------------------------------------------------------

    p for p in OCR_DIR.rglob("*.json")
    if p != test_ocr_path
]

print(second_ocr_path)


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 2. Load the second OCR output
# ---------------------------------------------------------

    data = json.load(f)

    Chunk(**item)
    for item in data
]

    c.doc_id for c in second_ocr_chunks
}

    second_ocr_chunks,
    cfg
)

# 4. Generate embeddings for ONLY the second issue
# ---------------------------------------------------------

second_vectors = embed.encode(
    second_chunks,
    cfg
)

print("\nEmbedding shape:", second_vectors.shape)


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 5. Add ONLY the second issue to the existing FAISS index
# ---------------------------------------------------------

store.build(
    second_chunks,
    second_vectors,
    cfg
)


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 6. Verify that the index grew
# ---------------------------------------------------------

index, stored_chunks = store.load(cfg)

stored_doc_ids = {
    c.doc_id for c in stored_chunks
}

print("\n" + "=" * 60)
print("INCREMENTAL ADDITION TEST")
print("=" * 60)

print("Total vectors :", index.ntotal)
print("Total chunks  :", len(stored_chunks))
print("Total issues  :", len(stored_doc_ids))
print("Issues        :", sorted(stored_doc_ids))
```

### C7 — Check what each file actually contains

Since you said the filenames **don't necessarily contain the doc ID**, we'll extract the `doc_id` from inside the OCR data.

```python
import json

def load_ocr_chunks(path):
    """
    Load OCR output and convert it into Chunk objects.

    Expected OCR JSON structure:
    [
        {
            "id": "...",
            "doc_id": "...",
            "text": "...",
            "page_ids": [...],
            "score": 0.0
        },
        ...
    ]
    """
    with path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        data = json.load(f)

    return [
        Chunk(**item)
        for item in data
    ]


# Test discovery
for path in ocr_files[:10]:
    try:
        file_chunks = load_ocr_chunks(path)

        doc_ids = {
            c.doc_id for c in file_chunks
        }

        print(
            f"{path.name:50s} "
            f"chunks={len(file_chunks):5d} "
            f"issues={len(doc_ids)} "
            f"doc_ids={list(doc_ids)[:2]}"
        )

    except Exception as e:
        print(f"❌ {path.name}: {e}")
```

This is worth doing before the full run because it lets you catch any weird OCR file that isn't actually JSON/Chunk format.

### C10 — Process every OCR file

This is the main cell.

```python
import json
from doc_agent.contracts import Chunk
from doc_agent.index import chunk, embed, store

successful = []
failed = []

for file_no, ocr_path in enumerate(ocr_files, start=1):

    print("\n" + "=" * 80)
    print(f"[{file_no}/{len(ocr_files)}] Processing:")
    print(ocr_path.name)
    print("=" * 80)

    try:
        # -------------------------------------------------
        # 1. Load OCR chunks
        # -------------------------------------------------

        with ocr_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            data = json.load(f)

        ocr_chunks = [
            Chunk(**item)
            for item in data
        ]

        if not ocr_chunks:
            print("⚠️ Empty OCR file. Skipping.")
            continue

        doc_ids = {
            c.doc_id for c in ocr_chunks
        }

        print(f"OCR chunks : {len(ocr_chunks)}")
        print(f"Doc IDs    : {doc_ids}")

        # -------------------------------------------------
        # Safety: one OCR file should normally represent
        # one issue.
        # -------------------------------------------------

        if len(doc_ids) != 1:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Expected 1 doc_id, found {len(doc_ids)}: {doc_ids}"
            )

        # -------------------------------------------------
        # 2. Chunk
        # -------------------------------------------------

        final_chunks = chunk.split(
            ocr_chunks,
            cfg
        )

        print(f"Final chunks: {len(final_chunks)}")

        if not final_chunks:
            print("⚠️ No final chunks. Skipping.")
            continue

        # -------------------------------------------------
        # 3. Embed
        # -------------------------------------------------

        vectors = embed.encode(
            final_chunks,
            cfg
        )

        print(f"Vectors: {vectors.shape}")

        # -------------------------------------------------
        # 4. Incrementally add to FAISS
        # -------------------------------------------------

        store.build(
            final_chunks,
            vectors,
            cfg
        )

        successful.append(
            {
                "file": str(ocr_path),
                "doc_id": next(iter(doc_ids)),
                "ocr_chunks": len(ocr_chunks),
                "final_chunks": len(final_chunks),
            }
        )

        print("✅ Successfully processed.")

    except Exception as e:

        print(f"❌ FAILED: {e}")

        failed.append(
            {
                "file": str(ocr_path),
                "error": str(e),
            }
        )

print("\n" + "=" * 80)
print("FULL RUN COMPLETE")
print("=" * 80)

print("Successful:", len(successful))
print("Failed    :", len(failed))
```

### C11 — Inspect failures

else:

AI: Yep — that means **`92-03.json` is not valid JSON**, even though it has a `.json` extension.

Specifically:

```text
Invalid control character
line 36824 column 103
```

usually means there is a **literal newline, tab, or other control character inside a JSON string** that wasn't escaped properly. This can easily happen with OCR output.

### Don't modify the OCR file yet

Since you want to process the whole dataset, I'd first identify how many files have this problem.

Replace C7 with this diagnostic version:

```python
import json

valid_files = []
invalid_files = []

for path in ocr_files:
    try:
        with path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            data = json.load(f)

        valid_files.append(path)

    except json.JSONDecodeError as e:
        invalid_files.append((path, e))

print(f"Valid JSON files  : {len(valid_files)}")
print(f"Invalid JSON files: {len(invalid_files)}")

print("\nINVALID FILES")
print("=" * 80)

for path, error in invalid_files:
    print(f"\n{path.name}")
    print(f"  Line   : {error.lineno}")
    print(f"  Column : {error.colno}")
    print(f"  Char   : {error.pos}")
    print(f"  Error  : {error.msg}")
```

This will tell us whether it's **just `92-03.json` or a bunch of OCR files**.

Your OCR output is valuable data. If we blindly do something like:

```python

we could accidentally alter legitimate OCR formatting.

We should first see **what character is actually breaking the JSON**.

Also, your full pipeline currently assumes:

```text
OCR JSON
   ↓

   ↓
Chunk objects
```

So if `92-03.json` is malformed, your full C10 loop would fail that issue—which is actually preferable to silently corrupting it.

AI: Yep. Since you **only want to process the valid OCR files for now**, let's make the notebook explicitly build a `valid_ocr_files` list and exclude `92-03.json` (and any other malformed files) automatically.

From your current cell onward, I'd do this:

### C7 — Load and validate all OCR files

```python
import json
from doc_agent.contracts import Chunk


def load_ocr_chunks(path):
    """
    Load one OCR JSON file and convert it into Chunk objects.
    """
    with path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        data = json.load(f)

    return [Chunk(**item) for item in data]


valid_ocr_files = []
invalid_ocr_files = []

print("Checking OCR files...")
print("=" * 80)

for path in ocr_files:

    try:
        file_chunks = load_ocr_chunks(path)

        if not file_chunks:
            print(f"⚠️ EMPTY: {path.name}")
            continue

        doc_ids = {c.doc_id for c in file_chunks}

        if len(doc_ids) != 1:
            print(
                f"⚠️ MULTIPLE DOC IDs: {path.name} "
                f"→ {doc_ids}"
            )
            invalid_ocr_files.append(path)
            continue

        valid_ocr_files.append(path)

        print(
            f"✅ {path.name:50s} "
            f"chunks={len(file_chunks):5d} "
            f"doc_id={next(iter(doc_ids))}"
        )

    except Exception as e:

        invalid_ocr_files.append(path)

        print(
            f"❌ INVALID: {path.name}\n"
            f"   {type(e).__name__}: {e}"
        )


print("\n" + "=" * 80)
print(f"VALID OCR files  : {len(valid_ocr_files)}")
print(f"INVALID OCR files: {len(invalid_ocr_files)}")
```

So now:

```python
valid_ocr_files
```

contains **only files that successfully load into your `Chunk` model**.

And:

```python
invalid_ocr_files
```

contains things like `92-03.json`.

---

### C8 — Explicitly see what will be processed

Me: i have done the one file test before, just give me the code for creating the index for all the valid files 

AI: Yep — if the **one-file test already passed**, you can now process every valid OCR file sequentially.

Use this as the next cell:

```python
from doc_agent.index import chunk, embed, store

print(f"Processing {len(valid_ocr_files)} valid OCR files...")
print("=" * 80)

for i, path in enumerate(valid_ocr_files, start=1):

    print(f"\n[{i}/{len(valid_ocr_files)}] Processing: {path.name}")
    print("-" * 80)

    try:
        # --------------------------------------------------
        # 1. Load OCR chunks
        # --------------------------------------------------
        ocr_chunks = load_ocr_chunks(path)

        print(f"OCR chunks loaded: {len(ocr_chunks)}")

        # --------------------------------------------------
        # 2. Re-chunk
        # --------------------------------------------------
        final_chunks = chunk.split(
            ocr_chunks,
            cfg
        )

        print(f"Final chunks: {len(final_chunks)}")

        # --------------------------------------------------
        # 3. Generate embeddings
        # --------------------------------------------------
        vectors = embed.encode(
            final_chunks,
            cfg
        )

        print(f"Embedding shape: {vectors.shape}")

        # --------------------------------------------------
        # 4. Add to incremental FAISS index
        # --------------------------------------------------
        store.build(
            final_chunks,
            vectors,
            cfg
        )

        print(f"✅ Successfully processed: {path.name}")

    except Exception as e:

        print(f"❌ FAILED: {path.name}")
        print(f"   {type(e).__name__}: {e}")

        # Continue with the next issue instead of
        # terminating the entire Kaggle run.
        continue

print("\n" + "=" * 80)
print("🏁 Finished processing all valid OCR files.")
```

### One important detail

Because your `store.build()` is now **incremental + duplicate-protected**, the process becomes:

```text
OCR file 1
   ↓
chunk
   ↓
embed
   ↓
FAISS add
   ↓
save index

OCR file 2
   ↓
chunk
   ↓
embed
   ↓
FAISS add to EXISTING index
   ↓
save updated index

OCR file 3
   ↓
...
```

So **do not delete `data/index` between files**.

Also, if Kaggle's session dies halfway through, you don't lose the completed issues. You can rerun the loop, and your duplicate protection will prevent already-indexed issues from being added again. That's exactly the behavior you want for this giant corpus.

AI:Yep — that output looks healthy. You now want to verify **three things**:

1. FAISS can actually load the index.
2. `index.ntotal == len(chunks_meta.json)`.
3. The vector dimension and metadata IDs line up.

Run this in a new Kaggle cell:

```python
import json
import faiss
from pathlib import Path

INDEX_DIR = Path("/kaggle/working/doc-agent-G21/data/index")

index_path = INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"
meta_path = INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json"

# --------------------------------------------------
# 1. Check files exist
# --------------------------------------------------
print("Index exists:", index_path.exists())
print("Metadata exists:", meta_path.exists())

# --------------------------------------------------
# 2. Load FAISS index
# --------------------------------------------------
index = faiss.read_index(str(index_path))

print("\nFAISS INDEX")
print("=" * 50)
print("Vectors :", index.ntotal)
print("Dimension:", index.d)
print("Type    :", type(index).__name__)

# --------------------------------------------------
# 3. Load metadata
# --------------------------------------------------
with meta_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    metadata = json.load(f)

print("\nMETADATA")
print("=" * 50)
print("Chunks:", len(metadata))

# --------------------------------------------------
# 4. Critical consistency check
# --------------------------------------------------
print("\nCONSISTENCY CHECK")
print("=" * 50)

if index.ntotal == len(metadata):
    print("✅ PASS: Every FAISS vector has corresponding metadata.")
else:
    print(
        f"❌ FAIL: FAISS has {index.ntotal} vectors "
        f"but metadata has {len(metadata)} chunks."
    )

# --------------------------------------------------
# 5. Check metadata structure
# --------------------------------------------------
required_fields = {
    "id",
    "doc_id",
    "text",
    "page_ids",
    "score",
}

bad_entries = []

for i, item in enumerate(metadata):
    missing = required_fields - set(item.keys())

    if missing:
        bad_entries.append((i, missing))

if not bad_entries:
    print("✅ PASS: All metadata entries have required fields.")
else:
    print(f"❌ FAIL: {len(bad_entries)} metadata entries are malformed.")
    print("Examples:", bad_entries[:5])

# --------------------------------------------------
# 6. Check duplicate chunk IDs
# --------------------------------------------------
chunk_ids = [item["id"] for item in metadata]

if len(chunk_ids) == len(set(chunk_ids)):
    print("✅ PASS: No duplicate chunk IDs.")
else:
    print("❌ FAIL: Duplicate chunk IDs detected.")

# --------------------------------------------------
# 7. Check indexed issues
# --------------------------------------------------
doc_ids = {item["doc_id"] for item in metadata}

print("\nISSUES IN INDEX")
print("=" * 50)
print("Unique issues:", len(doc_ids))

for doc_id in sorted(doc_ids):
    count = sum(
        1 for item in metadata
        if item["doc_id"] == doc_id
    )
    print(f"{doc_id}: {count} chunks")
```

### The most important result

You want to see:

```text
FAISS INDEX
Vectors : 8692
Dimension: 384

METADATA
Chunks: 8692

CONSISTENCY CHECK
✅ PASS: Every FAISS vector has corresponding metadata.
✅ PASS: All metadata entries have required fields.
✅ PASS: No duplicate chunk IDs.
```

And **384** is correct if you're using `BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5`.

import numpy as np

# Get a few vectors directly from FAISS
test_vectors = index.reconstruct_n(0, min(5, index.ntotal))

print("Reconstructed vectors shape:", test_vectors.shape)
print("Contains NaN:", np.isnan(test_vectors).any())
print("Contains Inf:", np.isinf(test_vectors).any())
print("Vector norms:", np.linalg.norm(test_vectors, axis=1))

if (
    test_vectors.shape[1] == index.d
    and not np.isnan(test_vectors).any()
    and not np.isinf(test_vectors).any()
):
    print("✅ FAISS vectors look valid.")

    print("❌ FAISS vector validation failed.")

The FAISS `index.faiss` + `chunks_meta.json` pair is the important artifact for retrieval.

```text
Run 1

```

and then you later ran the validation cell without rebuilding from scratch.

Because your `store.py` is **incremental**, it sees the existing:

```text
data/index/index.faiss
data/index/chunks_meta.json
```

and adds new issues to them.

That's intentional behavior — but it means an old index can absolutely affect your test.

AI: Here's the updated version, keeping your original wording and just extending it with the GPU/timeline context:

---

**Our corpus (from A1)**

All BYTE issues on Internet Archive (archive.org/details/byte-magazine). A2 uses a 15-issue subset spanning 1975–1993 for development. Pages: 6603, Words: 4,282,808. What makes it hard to read: BYTE pages use a dense 2–3 column layout with sidebars, boxed code listings, and full-page ads that break normal reading order — naive OCR would scramble text across columns, which is why a layout/region-detection stage runs before OCR.

We processed 15 issues for A2 due to GPU limitations: we're running OCR on Kaggle's free GPU tier, and transcribing each layout-detected region per page with our VLM-based OCR model is computationally expensive. A single ~550-page issue takes roughly 11 hours to OCR end-to-end. Given Kaggle's free-tier session and quota limits, running the full corpus of all other BYTE issues at this rate was not feasible within the assignment timeline. We scoped to this 15-issue subset (already exceeding the ≥300-page/≥60,000-word minimum by a wide margin) as our working development and evaluation corpus, and will continue expanding coverage toward the full archive as our Kaggle GPU quota resets across future milestones.
---

**One accuracy check before you submit this:** your corpus README says the assignment's minimum threshold is ≥300 pages *and* ≥60,000 words — your subset is at 6,603 pages / ~4.28M words, so you're roughly **22x** the page floor and **71x** the word floor. Worth stating that ratio explicitly somewhere in this section (or in `design_choices.md`) since it's a strong, concrete way to show your subset is a legitimate, generously-sized development corpus rather than a bare-minimum shortcut — reinforces that the 15-issue scoping was a genuine compute constraint, not corner-cutting.

| **Index**                | **FAISS `IndexHNSWFlat`**                         | `M=32`, `ef_construction=200`, `ef_search=64`         | No learned weights. **Incrementally updated** as new issues are processed; existing vectors are retained             |
| **Duplicate protection** | `store.py` issue-level validation                 | Checks `doc_id` before adding vectors                 | Prevents accidentally indexing the same issue twice; refuses the update if an existing `doc_id` is detected          |

### Updated pipeline description

> **pages → preprocess → layout → OCR → chunk → embed → store**

More specifically:

> **pages → preprocess → DocLayout-YOLO layout detection → Qwen2-VL-2B OCR → rule-based chunking → BGE-small-en-v1.5 embeddings → incremental FAISS HNSW indexing.**

**Changes from the original/default design:** enhancement is currently disabled; layout was changed from the initial Detectron2 choice to **DocLayout-YOLO**; OCR uses **Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct**; embeddings were changed from MiniLM/Jina to **BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5** for a practical balance between retrieval quality and computational cost; chunking preserves the multi-column reading order produced by layout; and the FAISS HNSW index supports **incremental addition of new issues with `doc_id`-based duplicate protection**, rather than rebuilding the entire vector database each time.

One small correction: your table currently says **"HTR"**. Since BYTE is printed magazine text, I'd call that stage **OCR**, not OCR/HTR, unless you're actually processing handwriting.

### Updated table

| Stage                | Model / Implementation                             | Configuration / Parameters                                                | Training / Notes                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| -------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Ingest + enhance** | `loader.py` + preprocessing; enhancement disabled  | `enhance.enabled=false`                                                   | Enhancement was disabled after considering the computational cost and because it was not necessary for the current OCR pipeline. The original default enabled a diffusion-based `unet_small` enhancement stage.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| **Layout**           | **DocLayout-YOLO / DocStructBench**                | `score_thr=0.25`, `imgsz=1024`                                            | Pretrained, zero-shot. Changed from the default **Detectron2 layout detector**. The `score_thr` was selected experimentally by testing several threshold values and comparing the resulting region detections/reading order on representative BYTE pages. The final value of **0.25** provided a better balance between detecting smaller regions and avoiding excessive false detections.                                                                                                                                              |
| **OCR**              | **Qwen/Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct**                      | `max_new_tokens=512`, `finetune=false`                                    | Pretrained VLM, zero-shot OCR. Changed from the default **TrOCR-base-printed**. No fine-tuning was performed because, as stated by the instructor in the Q&A document, the large-corpus setting was exempted from the fine-tuning requirement.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| **Chunk**            | Rule-based splitter (`index/chunk.py`)             | `chunk_tokens=256`, `overlap=32`, grouped by `doc_id`                     | No learned model. Preserves the multi-column reading order produced by the layout stage and does not independently reorder regions.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| **Embed**            | **BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5**                         | `dim=384`                                                                 | Pretrained, zero-shot. Changed from the A1-selected **Jina Embeddings v5 Text Small** and the default MiniLM. Jina was initially selected for its stronger retrieval capabilities and multilingual support, but the current corpus is English BYTE magazine text, so multilingual capability was unnecessary. BGE-small was selected as a lighter English-focused retrieval model that provides a better practical runtime for the large corpus while retaining a 384-dimensional representation suitable for efficient FAISS indexing. |
| **Index**            | **FAISS `IndexHNSWFlat`**                          | `M=32`, `ef_construction=200`, `ef_search=64`                             | No learned weights. Uses HNSW for approximate nearest-neighbor retrieval and supports **incremental addition of newly processed issues**. `doc_id`-based duplicate detection prevents the same issue from accidentally being indexed twice.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| **Retrieve**         | Dense retrieval + optional cross-encoder reranking | `k=10`, `k_step=10`, `k_max=40`, `weak_threshold=0.35`, reranking enabled | Stage for the current A3 submission. Evidence-gated retrieval progressively widens the candidate set when retrieved evidence is weak.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |

AI: ### Changes from the default configuration

* **Enhancement:** Disabled the default diffusion-based enhancement because our BYTE corpus does not contain faded or severely degraded pages where enhancement would provide meaningful benefit. Disabling it also avoids unnecessary computational overhead.

* **Layout detection:** Replaced the default **Detectron2** layout detector with **DocLayout-YOLO/DocStructBench**. The `score_thr` was selected experimentally by testing several threshold values on representative BYTE pages; **0.25** provided a better balance between detecting smaller regions and avoiding excessive false detections.

* **OCR:** Replaced the default **TrOCR-base-printed** with **Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct**, as our experiments showed substantially better OCR accuracy with the VLM, particularly for the diverse layouts and regions present in BYTE magazine.

* **Fine-tuning:** Disabled OCR fine-tuning because, according to the instructor's Q&A document, projects using a large corpus were exempted from the fine-tuning requirement.

* **Embedding:** Replaced the earlier **Jina Embeddings v5 Text Small** choice from A1 with **BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5**. Since our corpus is English-only, Jina's multilingual capability was unnecessary, while BGE-small provides a more practical runtime for processing our large corpus.

## Section 6 — The Plan for A3

*What's left: turn the knowledge base into an answering agent, then evaluate it.*

|                            |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **What we'll build next**  | **Retrieval + reranking:** implement dense retrieval over the FAISS HNSW index using BGE-small embeddings, followed by the configured cross-encoder reranker. **Agent loop + tools:** connect retrieval to the agent so it can iteratively search for evidence and formulate answers grounded in the retrieved BYTE content. **Evidence-gated re-search:** if the best retrieved evidence is below the configured `weak_threshold`, widen `k` by `k_step` until `k_max`; if evidence remains insufficient, **abstain rather than hallucinate**. **Guardrails / HITL:** enforce fail-closed behaviour for unsupported answers and require approval where the configured autonomy policy calls for human-in-the-loop intervention. **Grounding:** ensure generated answers are supported by retrieved chunks and provide the corresponding document/page evidence. **Evaluation:** measure retrieval and answering quality, including groundedness, answer correctness, retrieval latency, and abstention behaviour. |
| **Our evaluation set**     | We will create a `tasks.jsonl` evaluation set based on realistic questions a user might ask about **BYTE magazine**, covering factual questions that can be directly answered from a page/article, questions requiring evidence from multiple retrieved chunks, and **unanswerable/abstention cases** where the required information is not present in the corpus. Each task will contain checkable reference information so that answer correctness and grounding can be evaluated. We will also include questions involving BYTE's multi-column/article structure to test whether the layout-aware OCR and chunking pipeline preserves sufficient context for retrieval.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| **Biggest risk / unknown** | **The biggest risk is retrieval/grounding failure caused by the complex BYTE magazine layout and OCR noise.** Even if the correct information exists in the corpus, incorrect reading order, OCR errors, or weak retrieval may cause the agent to receive incomplete or irrelevant evidence. Our first approach will be to evaluate retrieval independently before tuning the agent, inspect failed queries and retrieved chunks, tune `k`, `ef_search`, and reranking as needed, and rely on the **evidence-gated re-search + fail-closed abstention** mechanism rather than allowing the agent to answer when evidence is insufficient.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |

### One thing I'd emphasize

The **abstention cases** are especially important for your A3 because your agent isn't supposed to just be a "retrieve something → LLM answers" system. The mandatory behaviour is essentially:

```text
Query
  ↓
Retrieve k=10
  ↓
Evidence strong enough?
  ├── YES → answer with grounding
  │
  └── NO
       ↓
     k=20
       ↓
     strong enough?
       ├── YES → answer
       └── NO → k=30 → k=40
                         ↓
                    still weak?
                         ↓
                      ABSTAIN
```

That **fail-closed behaviour** should probably be one of the most visible parts of your A3 plan, because it's directly tied to what your instructor says will be graded.

Each task will contain checkable reference information so that answer correctness and grounding can be evaluated. We will also include questions involving BYTE's multi-column/article structure to test whether the layout-aware OCR and chunking pipeline preserves sufficient context for retrieval.

AI: We will create a `tasks.jsonl` evaluation set based on realistic questions a user might ask about **BYTE magazine**, including factual questions that can be answered directly from a page/article, questions requiring evidence from multiple retrieved chunks, and **unanswerable/abstention cases** where the required information is not present in the corpus. For example, a user may ask about **WordPerfect for Windows**, which is discussed in the March 1992 BYTE issue, while a question such as *“What were the major breakthroughs in AI discovered in the 2020s?”* has no supporting evidence in the BYTE corpus. In such cases, the agent should **abstain rather than generate an unsupported answer**. Each task will contain checkable reference information so that answer correctness, grounding, and abstention behaviour can be evaluated. We will also include questions involving BYTE's multi-column and complex article structure to test whether the layout-aware OCR and chunking pipeline preserves sufficient context for retrieval.

AI: Because there are **two separate problems** in those paths.

Your project structure is roughly:

```text
doc-agent-starter/
├── Makefile
├── scripts/
│   ├── run_ingest.py
│   └── run_index.py
└── src/
    └── doc_agent/
        ├── pipeline.py
        ├── config.py
        └── ...
```

So this:

```make
ingest:; python src/scripts/run_ingest.py
index: ; python src/scripts/run_index.py
```

is wrong because **`scripts/` is not inside `src/`**.

It should be:

```make
ingest: ; PYTHONPATH=src python scripts/run_ingest.py
index:  ; PYTHONPATH=src python scripts/run_index.py
```

### Why `PYTHONPATH=src`?

Because your actual package is:

```text
src/doc_agent/
```

and `run_ingest.py` contains:

```python
from doc_agent import config, pipeline
```

Python needs `src/` on its module search path to find `doc_agent`.

---

### So your Makefile should have

```make
.PHONY: setup seed ingest index eval serve test lint

setup: ; uv sync --frozen
seed:  ; PYTHONPATH=src python scripts/set_seed.py
ingest: ; PYTHONPATH=src python scripts/run_ingest.py
index:  ; PYTHONPATH=src python scripts/run_index.py
eval:   ; PYTHONPATH=src python scripts/run_eval.py
serve:  ; PYTHONPATH=src uvicorn doc_agent.serve.api:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8000
lint:   ; ruff check . && black --check . && mypy src
test:   ; PYTHONPATH=src pytest
```

The **critical fixes** are:

```diff
- ingest:; python src/scripts/run_ingest.py
+ ingest: ; PYTHONPATH=src python scripts/run_ingest.py

- index: ; python src/scripts/run_index.py
+ index:  ; PYTHONPATH=src python scripts/run_index.py
```

Then test just the import/wiring with:

## Pipeline Summary

The knowledge-base construction pipeline processes scanned BYTE magazine pages from ingestion through vector indexing:

**pages → ingest → preprocess → layout detection → OCR → chunk → embed → FAISS HNSW store**

### Stage 1 — Ingest and preprocessing

Scanned BYTE magazine pages are loaded and prepared for downstream processing. Image enhancement is currently **disabled**, since the corpus does not contain sufficiently faded pages to justify the additional computational cost.

### Stage 2 — Layout detection

**DocLayout-YOLO / DocStructBench** detects text and other regions on each page. The detection threshold was selected through experimentation on representative BYTE pages. The layout stage also establishes the reading order used by later stages, which is important for BYTE's multi-column articles.

### Stage 3 — OCR

**Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct** performs pretrained, zero-shot OCR on the detected regions. It was selected after experiments showed substantially better OCR behaviour than the alternative OCR models tested, including **Tesseract, TrOCR-base-printed, and another TrOCR configuration**.

Fine-tuning is disabled because the instructor's Q&A document explicitly exempts the project from fine-tuning requirements for the large corpus.

### Stage 4 — Chunking

The region-level OCR output is converted into retrieval-sized chunks:

* `chunk_tokens = 256`
* `overlap = 32`
* chunks are grouped by `doc_id`
* the existing layout-generated reading order is preserved
* chunks are stored as `Chunk` objects containing their text, document ID, and page IDs

The splitter is rule-based and does not require a learned model.

### Stage 5 — Embedding

Each final chunk is converted into a **384-dimensional embedding** using:

**`BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5`**

The earlier Jina embedding choice was replaced because the BYTE corpus is English-only and therefore does not require multilingual retrieval. BGE-small provides a more practical computational/runtime trade-off for the large corpus while remaining suitable for semantic retrieval.

### Stage 6 — Vector storage

The embeddings are stored in a **FAISS HNSW** index.

The current configuration uses:

* `M = 32`
* `efConstruction = 200`
* `efSearch = 64`

The index is designed for the project's scalability requirement and supports **incremental addition of new issues**. Before adding vectors, the implementation checks existing metadata using `doc_id` and rejects an issue if that `doc_id` is already present. This prevents accidentally indexing the same BYTE issue twice.

The vector index is stored separately from its metadata:

* `data/index/index.faiss` — FAISS HNSW vector index
* `data/index/chunks_meta.json` — metadata used to map FAISS vector IDs back to the original chunks

## Design Rationale

The pipeline is designed around the main characteristics of the BYTE corpus:

1. **Multi-column layouts** require layout-aware processing and preservation of reading order.
2. **Large corpus size** makes computationally expensive enhancement, OCR, and embedding choices important.
3. **English-only content** allows the use of a lightweight English retrieval model rather than requiring multilingual embeddings.
4. **Incremental corpus growth** requires the vector store to accept new issues without rebuilding the entire index.
5. **Duplicate protection** prevents the same issue from being accidentally added to the vector database multiple times.

```

## Cell 1 — Load the index and metadata

```python
# Cell — Load the FAISS index and chunk metadata

import json
import faiss
from pathlib import Path

INDEX_DIR = Path("../data/index")

INDEX_FILE = INDEX_DIR / "index.faiss"
META_FILE = INDEX_DIR / "chunks_meta.json"

# Load FAISS index
index = faiss.read_index(str(INDEX_FILE))

# Load metadata
with META_FILE.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    chunks_meta = json.load(f)

print("Index loaded successfully.")
print(f"Vectors in index : {index.ntotal}")
print(f"Embedding dim    : {index.d}")
print(f"Metadata entries : {len(chunks_meta)}")

assert index.ntotal == len(chunks_meta), \
    "❌ FAISS vector count and metadata count do not match."

print("✅ FAISS ↔ metadata consistency check passed.")
```

---

## Cell 2 — Load BGE-small

This has to use **the exact same embedding model that was used to create your index**.

```python
# Cell — Load the BGE-small embedding model

from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer

MODEL_NAME = "BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5"

model = SentenceTransformer(MODEL_NAME)

print(f"Loaded embedding model: {MODEL_NAME}")
print(f"Embedding dimension: {model.get_sentence_embedding_dimension()}")
```

## Cell 4 — Embed the query and search FAISS

```python
# Cell — Retrieve top-k chunks

import numpy as np

TOP_K = 5

# Embed query
query_vector = model.encode(
    [query],
    convert_to_numpy=True
).astype("float32")

print("Query embedding shape:", query_vector.shape)

# Search FAISS
scores, indices = index.search(query_vector, TOP_K)

print(f"\nTop {TOP_K} retrieved chunks:\n")

for rank, (score, idx) in enumerate(zip(scores[0], indices[0]), start=1):
    chunk = chunks_meta[idx]

    print("=" * 80)
    print(f"Rank       : {rank}")
    print(f"FAISS ID   : {idx}")
    print(f"Score      : {score:.4f}")
    print(f"Document   : {chunk['doc_id']}")
    print(f"Page       : {chunk['page_ids']}")
    print(f"Chunk ID   : {chunk['id']}")
    print(f"Text       : {chunk['text'][:500]}")
```

Now you'll get something like:

```text
================================================================================
Rank       : 1
FAISS ID   : 1832
Score      : 0.7821
Document   : 1985_06_BYTE_10-06_...
Page       : ['1985_06_BYTE_10-06_..._p0036']
Chunk ID   : ...
Text       : The processor used in this computer was...
```

assert index.ntotal == len(chunks_meta), (
    f"Mismatch: FAISS has {index.ntotal} vectors, "
    f"but metadata has {len(chunks_meta)} chunks."
)

print("✅ PASS: Every FAISS vector has corresponding metadata.")

# Check required metadata fields
required_fields = {
    "id",
    "doc_id",
    "text",
    "page_ids",
}

missing_fields = []

for i, chunk in enumerate(chunks_meta):
    missing = required_fields - set(chunk.keys())

    if missing:
        missing_fields.append((i, missing))

    print("❌ Missing metadata fields:", missing_fields[:5])

AI: Yep — based on this result, your understanding is correct. The important nuance is that **the relevant chunk was actually retrieved, but dense retrieval ranked an irrelevant chunk above it**. So this is primarily a **ranking failure**, not a complete retrieval failure.

For the A2 table, I'd write it like this:

### The worst failure we saw

For the query **“Which CompuPro disk controller supports up to four 5¼-inch hard-disk drives, and what is its price?”**, dense retrieval did not rank the correct chunk first. The top result was a CompuPro **Disk 1** description from the 1981-05 issue, with a similarity score of **0.8190**, even though it did not contain the requested information. The correct chunk, from the **1985-06 issue**, appeared at **rank 2** with a score of **0.7743** and explicitly stated that **Disk 3** supports up to four 5¼-inch hard-disk drives and costs **$595**.

The main cause is that the current A2 retrieval stage relies only on **dense embedding similarity (BGE-small + FAISS HNSW)**. Disk 1 and Disk 3 are both CompuPro disk controllers and therefore have highly similar terminology and context, so semantic similarity alone can rank the wrong product above the exact answer-containing chunk. The correct evidence was still retrieved within the top 5, but it was not ranked first.

This limitation motivates the A3 retrieval design: **reranking the dense-retrieval candidates with a cross-encoder** should improve fine-grained query-to-passage matching, particularly when several products or closely related topics occur on the same BYTE page. The evidence-gated re-search mechanism will also allow the agent to widen the retrieval set when the initial evidence is insufficient.

Me: **OCR quality on a sample**
*measure reading quality on a labelled sample (character/word error rate, or F1). State the number and the sample size.*

will this be done using the heldout pages? 

AI: Yes — **if you actually have those 15 held-out pages with ground-truth transcriptions (`labels.jsonl`)**, they are exactly what you should use for the OCR-quality evaluation.

The idea is:

* **Input:** the scanned page image from the held-out set.
* **Prediction:** your Qwen2-VL OCR output for that page.
* **Ground truth:** the manually transcribed text in `labels.jsonl`.
* **Metric:** preferably **CER (Character Error Rate)** or **WER (Word Error Rate)**.
* **Report:** e.g. `CER = 8.4% on 15 held-out pages`.

### Important distinction

Those pages should be **held out from tuning**. You shouldn't use them to decide things like the layout threshold, OCR parameters, etc., and then call the result an unbiased evaluation.

So your A2 table could eventually say:

> **OCR quality:** CER/WER measured against manually transcribed ground truth on **15 held-out pages** (`labels.jsonl`).

If you **haven't actually run the OCR evaluation yet**, don't put a made-up number there. You can leave the metric as pending until you calculate it.

Me: but for huge corpus the fine tuning was exempted 

AI: Exactly — **the fine-tuning exemption does not mean you skip evaluation**.

These are two separate things:

* **Fine-tuning:** You were exempted from fine-tuning because the corpus is huge, according to your instructor's Q&A guidance. So you use **Qwen2-VL-2B zero-shot**.
* **Evaluation:** You still need to demonstrate how well that pretrained OCR works. The **15 held-out manually transcribed pages** are useful for this.

So the setup is:

**BYTE corpus → Qwen2-VL-2B (zero-shot OCR)**
↳ **15 held-out pages → compare OCR output against manual transcription → CER/WER**

The held-out pages aren't being used to train the model. They're simply your **evaluation benchmark**.

One important caveat: if those 15 pages were also used while choosing/tuning things like the layout `score_thr` or OCR settings, then they're not truly held-out anymore. In that case, call them a **labelled evaluation sample** rather than a held-out test set.

And for your A2 table, you absolutely **shouldn't invent a CER/WER number** just because the evaluation set exists. Run the comparison first, then report the actual result.


Me: but how can i find CER or WER using these .-.

i do have the ocr results saved in the data/interim/ocr on my machine 

AI: Yep 😭 — you can calculate **CER/WER from the OCR outputs you already have**, but you need one extra thing: the **ground-truth transcription** for the same pages.

The basic comparison is:

```text
scanned page
     ↓
Qwen2-VL OCR
     ↓
OCR text ─────────────┐
                      ├── compare ──→ CER / WER
manual transcription ─┘
```

### What you need

For each of your 15 labelled pages, you need something like:

```text
labels.jsonl
```

with a structure along the lines of:

```json
{"page_id": "1985_06_BYTE_p0036", "text": "Our disk controller boards drive for efficiency..."}
```

And your existing OCR output might contain:

```json
{
    "id": "...",
    "doc_id": "...",
    "text": "Our disk controller boards drive for efficency...",
    "page_ids": ["1985_06_BYTE_p0036"]
}
```

Then we compare the **ground-truth text** against the concatenated OCR text for that page.

### CER

CER is basically:

> **How many character edits are needed to turn the OCR text into the correct text?**

For example:

```text
Ground truth:  "Disk controller"
OCR output:    "Disk controllor"
```

There is one character error (`e` → `o`).

So:

```text
CER = character errors / total ground-truth characters
```

### WER

Same idea, but at the **word level**.

```text
Ground truth: "Disk controller costs $595"
OCR output:   "Disk controllor costs $595"
```

One word is wrong:

```text
WER = word errors / total ground-truth words
```

### For your project

Since your OCR output is already in:

```text
data/interim/ocr/
```

you **don't need to rerun OCR**.

You just need:

1. Find the 15 labelled pages.
2. Load their manual transcriptions from `labels.jsonl`.
3. Find the corresponding OCR regions in `data/interim/ocr/`.
4. Concatenate the OCR regions belonging to each page **in their layout/read order**.
5. Calculate CER/WER.
6. Report something like:

> **OCR quality:** CER = 7.8%, WER = 14.2% on 15 manually transcribed pages.

The actual numbers, of course, need to come from your data.

If you show me the **actual structure/content of your `labels.jsonl`** (and ideally one corresponding OCR JSON), I can give you a small notebook cell that calculates **CER + WER for all 15 pages automatically**.

Me: import json
from pathlib import Path
from jiwer import cer, wer

# ============================================================
# OCR QUALITY — ONE LABELLED PAGE
# ============================================================

OCR_FILE = Path(
    "../data/interim/ocr/byte-magazine-1975-09.txt"
)

LABELS_FILE = Path(
    "../grading_kit/labels.jsonl"
)

TARGET_PAGE = (
    "1975_09_BYTE_00-01_The_Worlds_Greatest_Toy_p0020"
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 1. Load the ground-truth label
# ------------------------------------------------------------

reference_text = None

with LABELS_FILE.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:

    for line in f:

        line = line.strip()

        if not line:
            continue

        item = json.loads(line)

        if item.get("page_id") == TARGET_PAGE:
            reference_text = item["text"]
            break

if reference_text is None:
    raise ValueError(
        f"❌ Ground-truth label not found for {TARGET_PAGE}"
    )

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 2. Load OCR output
# ------------------------------------------------------------

with OCR_FILE.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    ocr_chunks = json.load(f)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 3. Extract OCR regions belonging to this page
# ------------------------------------------------------------

page_chunks = [
    chunk
    for chunk in ocr_chunks
    if TARGET_PAGE in chunk.get("page_ids", [])
]

if not page_chunks:
    raise ValueError(
        f"❌ No OCR regions found for {TARGET_PAGE}"
    )

# Preserve the order in the OCR file
ocr_text = "\n".join(
    chunk.get("text", "")
    for chunk in page_chunks
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 4. Calculate CER and WER
# ------------------------------------------------------------

page_cer = cer(reference_text, ocr_text)
page_wer = wer(reference_text, ocr_text)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 5. Display results
# ------------------------------------------------------------

print("=" * 70)
print("OCR QUALITY EVALUATION")
print("=" * 70)

print(f"Sample size     : 1 labelled page")
print(f"Page            : {TARGET_PAGE}")
print(f"OCR regions     : {len(page_chunks)}")
print(f"CER             : {page_cer:.2%}")
print(f"WER             : {page_wer:.2%}")

print("=" * 70)


ok in the held out set the texts are separated by "\n" but as the ocr was done region wise did merging these contained "\n"?

AI: Yes — **but there's an important distinction**.

Your current code does:

```python
ocr_text = "\n".join(
    chunk.get("text", "")
    for chunk in page_chunks
)
```

This means you're adding **one newline between every OCR region**.

If each OCR region's `text` already contains `\n`, those internal newlines are **preserved**. For example:

```text
Region 1 text:
"Hello world.\nThis is another line."

Region 2 text:
"Next column.\nMore text."
```

becomes:

```text
Hello world.
This is another line.
Next column.
More text.
```

So yes, the newlines **inside each region remain intact**.

### But there's a bigger CER/WER issue

Your held-out ground truth is apparently structured like:

```text
Line 1
Line 2
Line 3
Line 4
```

while your OCR is structured as **layout regions**:

```text
Region 1:
Line 1
Line 2

Region 2:
Line 3
Line 4
```

When calculating **CER/WER**, `\n` is effectively treated as whitespace by the word-level metric, but the exact formatting can still affect character-level comparison.

More importantly, **the order of the regions matters a lot**.

Your current:

```python
for chunk in ocr_chunks
```

assumes that the OCR JSON's chunk order is already the correct reading order. Given your project specifically uses DocLayout-YOLO to preserve multi-column reading order, that's actually what we *want* to evaluate.

### I would make one small change

Normalize whitespace on **both** reference and OCR before calculating CER/WER:

```python
import re

def normalize_text(text):
    return re.sub(r"\s+", " ", text).strip()

reference_text_normalized = normalize_text(reference_text)
ocr_text_normalized = normalize_text(ocr_text)

page_cer = cer(
    reference_text_normalized,
    ocr_text_normalized
)

page_wer = wer(
    reference_text_normalized,
    ocr_text_normalized
)
```

This makes the metric answer:

> **"How accurately did OCR recognize the actual text?"**

rather than partially measuring:

> **"Did the OCR region boundaries happen to put newlines in exactly the same places as the transcription?"**

That's much more defensible for your A2 report.

So your extraction stays:

```python
ocr_text = "\n".join(
    chunk.get("text", "")
    for chunk in page_chunks
)
```

and **then normalize both texts before CER/WER**.

One caveat: this measures **text recognition quality**, not layout/reading-order quality. That's actually useful because your A2 demo can separately show the DocLayout-YOLO boxes visually, while CER/WER measures whether the OCR text itself is accurate.

Me: can you pls compose text for a markdown cell explaining the reason the cer / wer is sp high is bec the regions are sometimes doubly detected for such 0.25 score threshold but if the threshold was bigger some regions would remain undetected, so ofcourse when i concatenate all these regions, the cer and wer quality will drop 

so the trade off of the cer/wer with info loss was done 

AI: 
### CER/WER Interpretation

The relatively high CER/WER observed on this sample should be interpreted in the context of our **layout-detection threshold selection**. We set the DocLayout-YOLO `score_thr` to **0.25** after testing different threshold values on representative BYTE pages. This threshold was selected to prioritize **text-region recall and information preservation**: increasing the threshold reduced some duplicate or overlapping detections, but also caused genuine text regions to be missed. For a retrieval system, losing an entire text region is more harmful because the missing information can no longer be retrieved or used as evidence.

At the lower threshold, however, some text regions can be detected more than once. Because our OCR output is generated region-by-region and the regions are subsequently concatenated, these duplicate detections introduce repeated text into the OCR transcript. When compared against the single ground-truth transcription, the repeated text is counted as recognition error and therefore increases both **CER and WER**.

Therefore, the measured CER/WER reflects not only OCR recognition errors but also the **precision–recall trade-off introduced by layout detection**. A higher `score_thr` could reduce duplicate detections and potentially lower CER/WER, but at the cost of losing valid regions and information. Conversely, the selected `0.25` threshold favors retaining potentially useful text at the cost of some duplicate detections and a higher measured CER/WER. For our BYTE retrieval task, we deliberately chose this **information-preserving operating point**, since retaining text that can later be retrieved is preferable to achieving a lower OCR error rate by discarding potentially relevant content.

AI: ## OCR Region Inspection

As part of the knowledge-base demo, we first inspect a representative BYTE magazine page to verify that the **layout-aware OCR pipeline is identifying distinct text regions** before evaluating the extracted text.

The sample page is taken from the **June 1985 BYTE issue** and contains multiple columns, headings, advertisements, and product descriptions. We load the page image together with the corresponding OCR output and filter the detected regions using the page ID. This lets us verify:

* how many regions DocLayout-YOLO detected on the page;
* whether the detected regions correspond to meaningful page content; and
* the text extracted from each detected region.

The region-level inspection provides a visual and textual sanity check before using the OCR output for the subsequent **retrieval demonstration**.

Me: !pip install -q jiwer
import json
import re
from pathlib import Path
from jiwer import cer, wer

# ============================================================
# OCR QUALITY — ONE LABELLED PAGE
# ============================================================

OCR_FILE = Path(
    "../data/interim/ocr/byte-magazine-1975-09.txt"
)

LABELS_FILE = Path(
    "../grading_kit/labels.jsonl"
)

TARGET_PAGE = (
    "1975_09_BYTE_00-01_The_Worlds_Greatest_Toy_p0020"
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 1. Load the ground-truth label
# ------------------------------------------------------------

reference_text = None

with LABELS_FILE.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:

    for line in f:

        line = line.strip()

        if not line:
            continue

        item = json.loads(line)

        if item.get("page_id") == TARGET_PAGE:
            reference_text = item["text"]
            break

if reference_text is None:
    raise ValueError(
        f"❌ Ground-truth label not found for {TARGET_PAGE}"
    )

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 2. Load OCR output
# ------------------------------------------------------------

with OCR_FILE.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    ocr_chunks = json.load(f)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 3. Extract OCR regions belonging to this page
# ------------------------------------------------------------

page_chunks = [
    chunk
    for chunk in ocr_chunks
    if TARGET_PAGE in chunk.get("page_ids", [])
]

if not page_chunks:
    raise ValueError(
        f"❌ No OCR regions found for {TARGET_PAGE}"
    )

# Preserve the order of OCR regions in the OCR output
ocr_text = "\n".join(
    chunk.get("text", "")
    for chunk in page_chunks
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 4. Normalize whitespace
# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Ground-truth labels use line breaks while OCR is region-based.
# We collapse consecutive whitespace so CER/WER measures text
# recognition rather than differences in line/region formatting.

def normalize_text(text):
    return re.sub(r"\s+", " ", text).strip()


reference_normalized = normalize_text(reference_text)
ocr_normalized = normalize_text(ocr_text)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 5. Calculate CER and WER
# ------------------------------------------------------------

page_cer = cer(
    reference_normalized,
    ocr_normalized
)

page_wer = wer(
    reference_normalized,
    ocr_normalized
)

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# 6. Display results
# ------------------------------------------------------------

print("=" * 70)
print("OCR QUALITY EVALUATION")
print("=" * 70)

print(f"Sample size     : 1 labelled page")
print(f"Page            : {TARGET_PAGE}")
print(f"OCR regions     : {len(page_chunks)}")
print(f"Reference chars : {len(reference_normalized)}")
print(f"OCR chars       : {len(ocr_normalized)}")
print(f"CER             : {page_cer:.2%}")
print(f"WER             : {page_wer:.2%}")

print("=" * 70)
